15.4 Partial kernels and quotient equilibrium

Definitions, results, and proofs on this subject.

27 Partial kernels and quotient equilibrium

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Let $X$ be a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$. Throughout this chapter, $L$ is a holomorphic line bundle on $X$ with a smooth Hermitian metric $h$. In a local holomorphic frame $e$ write $|e|_ h^2=\mathrm{e}^{-\chi }$; the curvature form $c_1(L,h)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi $ is a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form, independent of the frame, as in chapter 20.

A quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\varphi $ on $X$, the selecting potential, selects in each tensor power $L^ m$ the holomorphic sections $s$ for which $|s|_{h^ m}^2\mathrm{e}^{-m\varphi }$ is locally integrable. A partial Bergman kernel is the Bergman kernel of this space of sections for a Hilbert norm defined by a continuous weight: the singular function $\varphi $ decides which sections occur, but it does not enter their norms. On a compact Kähler manifold, if $\varphi $ is $c_1(L,h)$-plurisubharmonic with positive non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère mass, the normalized kernel measures converge to the Monge–Ampère measure of a partial envelope. The competitors defining this envelope have multiplier ideals contained in those of $\varphi $ at every positive real weight, and lie below the continuous weight on the set where the norm is computed. The envelope is the largest of these $c_1(L,h)$-plurisubharmonic functions. This chapter studies how much of this equilibrium theory survives when $X$ is not Kähler, and when the selecting potential is plurisubharmonic only with respect to a Hermitian form unrelated to the curvature of $L$.

One direction of the Kähler argument is local and works on every compact complex manifold. A Bernstein–Markov estimate, which follows from the submean inequality, keeps the partial Fubini–Study potentials below the weight, and Lelong-number estimates on local modifications keep their limits within the multiplier-ideal class of $\varphi $. Together these bound every limit of the potentials by the partial envelope. A matching lower bound requires global sections approximating the envelope, and this is where the Kähler hypothesis enters. If $X$ admits a modification from a compact Kähler manifold, the relative canonical bundle with a Jacobian metric makes the selected spaces upstairs and downstairs isometric, and the Kähler theorem gives convergence of the kernel measures on $X$ itself.

Fibrations show what can happen without such a modification. On a holomorphic submersion with compact connected fibers, every section of a pulled-back line bundle is constant along the fibers and descends to the base. Probability measures on the fibers make this descent an isometry, so the kernel measures on the total space are lifts of kernel measures on the base. Their limit, the quotient equilibrium measure, is the lift of the equilibrium measure of the base, and it appears at the growth rate $m^{\dim B}$ of the section spaces rather than at $m^{\dim X}$. On the Hopf manifold it is a Hermitian Monge–Ampère measure; in dimension three this gives a nonzero limit on a manifold without the bounded mass property.

A selecting potential that is plurisubharmonic only with respect to a Hermitian form need not descend, but the sections it selects still do, so its singularities act only through a vanishing condition on the base. For isolated logarithmic poles, for poles along a section over a base divisor, and for anisotropic singularities controlled by integrability along the fibers, this condition differs from a multiplier-ideal condition on the base by a bounded number of vanishing orders. Such shifts cost only $O(m^{\dim B-1})$ sections, and for point poles and for poles along a section over a divisor the kernel measures still converge to a quotient equilibrium measure. The price is a loss of information: moving a pole along its fiber changes the multiplier ideals on $X$ but leaves every kernel unchanged. Explicit point and divisor models on projective bases compute the limits through binomial tails, and a change of the integration volume separates the base equilibrium measure from the conditional fiber probabilities carried by its lift.

Two mechanisms control the explicit models that follow: the local metric weight determines the scale of evaluation, and the number of selected sections determines the mass of the kernel measure. When vanishing orders grow sublinearly in $m$, an entire-function model gives a Gaussian transition on a shrinking scale. Transferring it to a compact curve requires a uniform local comparison of norms and a global $L^2$ correction preserving the vanishing order; the remainder of the smooth weight moves the center of the transition. In two dimensions, competing monomials at a fixed curvature zero can already introduce a logarithmic factor into the scale of the full kernel.

The section count also distinguishes selections with similar potential limits. A Newton polytope gives a graded monomial family whose growth constraint determines an envelope and whose lattice points determine the limiting mass. For a lower-dimensional polytope, concentration of the monomial norms gives a positive limit carried by real tori, although the higher curvature powers of the envelope vanish. Multiplier-ideal spaces need not form a graded family: rounding at critical divisors can make the leading count oscillate between even and odd degrees, while at the base points of a pencil three neighboring monomial bands contribute to a single positive limit. Convergence of the potentials therefore needs to be separated from convergence of the normalized measures.

We use the following notation from earlier chapters. For quasi-plurisubharmonic functions $u,v$, we write $u\preceq v$ if $u\leq v+C$ for a constant $C$, and $u\sim v$ if $u\preceq v\preceq u$ (definition 1.12.1); we write $u\preceq _{\mathcal I}v$ if $\mathcal I(ku)\subseteq \mathcal I(kv)$ for every real $k>0$, and $u\sim _{\mathcal I}v$ if these ideals are equal for every $k>0$ (section 1.12). For a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta $, $P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ is the multiplier-ideal envelope of definition 16.2.1, and $V_\theta $ is the upper envelope of all nonpositive $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions, which has minimal singularities (section 1.19). The non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère measure of $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ is written $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)$, or $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)$ when its mass may be infinite (theorem 6.1.2).

27.1 Selected spaces and partial Bergman kernels

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Fix a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\varphi $ on $X$, the selecting potential, and a smooth positive probability measure $\nu $ on $X$. For each integer $m\geq 1$, the selected space of degree $m$ and its dimension are

\[ V_ m=H^0\bigl (X,L^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\varphi )\bigr ), \qquad N_ m=\dim V_ m. \]

Instead of $\varphi $, the Hilbert norm on $V_ m$ uses a real continuous weight $v\in C(X)$:

\[ \| s\| _{m,v,\nu }^2=\int _ X|s|_{h^ m}^2\mathrm{e}^{-mv}\, \mathrm{d}\nu \quad (s\in V_ m). \]

Since $v$ is bounded, this integral is finite for every global section. It vanishes only for $s=0$, because $\nu $ has a positive density and a holomorphic section vanishing almost everywhere vanishes identically. This separation of the selecting potential from the norm follows [Section 14.3, SGPT].

Fix also an integer $r\geq 1$, which determines the normalization below; it will be chosen as the growth degree of $N_ m$, which may be smaller than $n$. Let $s_1,\ldots ,s_{N_ m}$ be an orthonormal basis of $V_ m$ for the norm $\| \cdot \| _{m,v,\nu }$. The partial Bergman kernel and the partial kernel measure are

\[ B_{m,v,\varphi ,\nu }(x) =\mathrm{e}^{-mv(x)}\sum _ j|s_ j(x)|_{h^ m}^2 \quad (x\in X), \qquad \beta _ m^{(r)}=\frac{r!}{m^ r}B_{m,v,\varphi ,\nu }\, \nu . \]

By unitary invariance they do not depend on the orthonormal basis, and both are zero when $V_ m=\{ 0\} $. The evaluation argument of proposition 20.1.2 gives the extremal formula and the total mass:

\[ B_{m,v,\varphi ,\nu }(x) =\sup _{\substack {s\in V_ m\\ \| s\| _{m,v,\nu }\leq 1}} |s(x)|_{h^ m}^2\mathrm{e}^{-mv(x)}, \qquad \int _ X\beta _ m^{(r)}=\frac{r!N_ m}{m^ r}. \]

The selecting potential enters only through $V_ m$. In particular, for fixed $h$, $v$ and $\nu $, if the multiplier ideals $\mathcal I(m\varphi )$ are contained in those of another selecting potential, then so are the selected spaces, and the extremal formula shows that the kernels are ordered in the same way.

Replacing $h$ by $h\mathrm{e}^{-a}$ and $v$ by $v-a$, for a smooth real function $a$, leaves $h^ m\mathrm{e}^{-mv}$, and hence the norm and the kernel measure, unchanged.

27.2 A local Bernstein–Markov estimate

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A Bernstein–Markov estimate compares the supremum norm of sections on a weighted compact set with an integral norm, up to an exponential loss in the degree whose rate can be made arbitrarily small. It is what keeps partial Fubini–Study potentials below the weight in the limit. For a smooth positive volume and a continuous weight on the whole manifold, it follows from the submean inequality.

For a compact set $K\subseteq X$ and $w\in C(K)$, write

\[ \| s\| _{m,w,K}=\sup _ K |s|_{h^ m}\mathrm{e}^{-mw/2}. \]

A probability measure $\mu $ on $K$ is Bernstein–Markov for $(K,w)$ if, for every $\epsilon >0$, there is a constant $C_\epsilon $ such that

\[ \| s\| _{m,w,K}\leq C_\epsilon \mathrm{e}^{\epsilon m} \left(\int _ K|s|_{h^ m}^2\mathrm{e}^{-mw}\, \mathrm{d}\mu \right)^{1/2} \]

for all $m\geq 1$ and all $s\in H^0(X,L^ m)$. If $K$ is non-pluripolar, the supremum norm is a norm: the zero set of a nonzero section is an analytic hypersurface, hence pluripolar, and cannot contain $K$. The Bernstein–Markov inequality then shows that the integral

\[ \| s\| _{m,w,\mu }^2=\int _ K|s|_{h^ m}^2\mathrm{e}^{-mw}\, \mathrm{d}\mu \]

is also a norm on $H^0(X,L^ m)$, and in particular on every selected space $V_ m$. The definitions of section 27.1 therefore apply with $(X,v,\nu )$ replaced by $(K,w,\mu )$. We write $B_{m,w,\varphi ,\mu }$ for the resulting partial Bergman kernel on $K$ and $\beta _ m^{(r)}=(r!/m^ r)B_{m,w,\varphi ,\mu }\, \mu $ for the partial kernel measure; the extremal formula and the total mass $r!N_ m/m^ r$ hold as before.

Proposition 27.2.1. Let $v\in C(X)$. Every smooth positive probability measure on $X$ is Bernstein–Markov for $(X,v)$.

Proof. Let $\nu $ be a smooth positive probability measure, and fix $\epsilon >0$. Cover $X$ by finitely many coordinate charts on which $L$ has holomorphic frames, and choose relatively compact open subsets of these charts that still cover $X$. In a frame $e$ write $|e|_ h^2=\mathrm{e}^{-\chi }$ and $s=f e^{\otimes m}$, so that $|s|_{h^ m}^2\mathrm{e}^{-mv}=|f|^2\mathrm{e}^{-m(\chi +v)}$. By compactness and uniform continuity of the finitely many functions $\chi +v$, there is a radius $\delta >0$ with the following property: every point $x$ lies in one of the smaller sets, the coordinate ball $B(x,\delta )$ lies in the corresponding chart, and the oscillation of $\chi +v$ on $B(x,\delta )$ is at most $2\epsilon $. On these balls the density of $\nu $ with respect to coordinate Lebesgue measure is bounded below by a positive constant independent of $x$.

The submean inequality for the holomorphic function $f$ on $B(x,\delta )$ therefore gives

\[ \begin{split} |s(x)|_{h^ m}^2\mathrm{e}^{-mv(x)} & =|f(x)|^2\mathrm{e}^{-m(\chi +v)(x)}\\ & \leq C_\delta \mathrm{e}^{2\epsilon m} \int _{B(x,\delta )}|f|^2\mathrm{e}^{-m(\chi +v)}\, \mathrm{d}\nu \\ & \leq C_\delta \mathrm{e}^{2\epsilon m}\| s\| _{m,v,\nu }^2, \end{split} \]

with $C_\delta $ independent of $x$ and $m$. Taking the supremum over $x$ and then the square root gives the Bernstein–Markov inequality with $C_\epsilon =C_\delta ^{1/2}$. $\square$

For a general compact set $K$ and weight $w\in C(K)$, the Bernstein–Markov property is not automatic, and we assume it explicitly whenever it is needed.

27.3 Partial envelopes on a weighted compact set

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Two constraints act on limits of partial Fubini–Study potentials. The continuous weight is an obstacle on the set where sections are measured, and the selecting potential imposes a multiplier-ideal constraint on the whole manifold. The partial envelope is the largest potential satisfying both. Since an obstacle on a non-pluripolar compact set bounds every competitor globally, the partial envelope lies below a translate of the multiplier-ideal envelope of the selecting potential; conversely, a translate of that envelope lying below the obstacle is a competitor. The partial envelope therefore differs from the multiplier-ideal envelope by a bounded function.

Let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$, let $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, let $K\subseteq X$ be compact and non-pluripolar, and let $w\in C(K,\mathbb R)$. Recall that theorem 1.16.3 characterizes the order $\preceq _{\mathcal I}$ by Lelong numbers on local modifications. As in section 1.19, an inequality holds quasi-everywhere if it holds outside a pluripolar set. The partial envelope of $\varphi $ with obstacle $w$ on $K$ is

27.4
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} E_{\theta ,K}[\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}(w) & =\Bigl (\sup \bigl \{ a\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ): a\preceq _{\mathcal I}\varphi ,\\ & \hspace{52pt}a\leq w\text{ quasi-everywhere on }K\bigr \} \Bigr )^*. \end{aligned} \end{equation}

Theorem 27.3.1. The function $E=E_{\theta ,K}[\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}(w)$ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic, and it is the largest member of its defining family. Let $q=P_\theta [\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}$, and let $C$ be a constant as in lemma 1.19.1, so that $\sup _ Xa\leq C$ for every $a\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $a\leq w$ quasi-everywhere on $K$. Then

\[ q+\min _ Kw\leq E\leq q+C. \]

In particular, $E\sim q$ and

\[ E\sim _{\mathcal I}\varphi . \]

Proof. By theorem 16.2.2, $q\leq 0$ and $q\sim _{\mathcal I}\varphi $. Hence $q+\min _ Kw$ belongs to the defining family. Lemma 1.19.1 bounds the entire family above by $C$, so its regularized supremum $E$ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic, and $E\geq q+\min _ Kw$.

For the upper bound, let $a$ be a member of the family. Since $\sup _ Xa\leq C$, proposition 16.2.4, proposition 16.2.6 give

\[ a-C\leq a-\sup _ Xa\leq P_\theta [a]_{\mathcal I} \leq P_\theta [\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}=q. \]

Taking the regularized supremum over $a$ proves $E\leq q+C$.

The two bounds show that $E-q$ is bounded. Hence $E$ and $q$ have the same multiplier ideals, and these are the multiplier ideals of $\varphi $.

It remains to show that $E$ itself satisfies the obstacle condition, which is not automatic after regularization. By Choquet's lemma, $E$ is the regularization of the supremum of a countable subfamily. Replace its members by their successive finite maxima. Finite maxima are again members of the family: the multiplier-ideal order is preserved by theorem 1.16.3, and a finite union of pluripolar exceptional sets is pluripolar. We obtain an increasing sequence $a_ j$ in the family whose limit has regularization $E$. Outside a countable union of pluripolar subsets of $K$, every $a_ j$ is at most $w$, and hence so is $\lim _ ja_ j$. By the local upper-envelope theorem recalled in section 1.19, $E$ differs from $\lim _ ja_ j$ only on a pluripolar set. Thus $E\leq w$ quasi-everywhere on $K$.

We have shown that $E$ belongs to its defining family. It dominates every member by construction, so it is the largest member. In particular, the supremum in (27.4) equals $E$ at every point even before regularization. $\square$

Proposition 27.3.2. The partial envelope depends only on the multiplier-ideal class of $\varphi $, and it is increasing in the obstacle and in $\varphi $ with respect to $\preceq _{\mathcal I}$. For $w,w'\in C(K)$ and $c\in \mathbb R$,

\[ \begin{split} E_{\theta ,K}[\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}(w+c) & =E_{\theta ,K}[\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}(w)+c,\\ \bigl |E_{\theta ,K}[\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}(w) -E_{\theta ,K}[\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}(w')\bigr | & \leq \| w-w'\| _{C(K)}. \end{split} \]

The second line stands for the two corresponding pointwise inequalities, which make sense also at the poles. Let $\theta _0,\theta _1$ be smooth real $(1,1)$-forms on $X$, and let $\varphi _ i\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ i)$ and $w_ i\in C(K)$ for $i=0,1$. For $0<t<1$ put $\theta _ t=(1-t)\theta _0+t\theta _1$, and define $\varphi _ t$ and $w_ t$ in the same way. Then

\[ (1-t)E_{\theta _0,K}[\varphi _0]_{\mathcal I}(w_0) +tE_{\theta _1,K}[\varphi _1]_{\mathcal I}(w_1) \leq E_{\theta _ t,K}[\varphi _ t]_{\mathcal I}(w_ t). \]

Finally, for a smooth real function $f$, changing the background and the obstacle gives the identity

\[ E_{\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f,K}[\varphi -f]_{\mathcal I}(w-f|_ K) =E_{\theta ,K}[\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}(w)-f. \]

Proof. The first two assertions hold because the defining families are equal or nested. Adding a constant $c$ is a bijection between the families for $w$ and $w+c$, which proves the translation rule. Applying monotonicity and translation to $w'-\| w-w'\| _{C(K)}\leq w\leq w'+\| w-w'\| _{C(K)}$ gives the two inequalities in the second line.

For concavity, the left-hand side is $\theta _ t$-plurisubharmonic, and it lies below $w_ t$ outside the union of two pluripolar exceptional sets. By theorem 27.3.1, its two summands have the multiplier-ideal classes of $\varphi _0$ and $\varphi _1$, so the addition and scaling rules of lemma 1.16.4 show that the left-hand side has the multiplier-ideal class of $\varphi _ t$. It is therefore a member of the family defining the right-hand side. Finally, $a\mapsto a-f$ is a bijection between the two families in the last identity, and regularization commutes with subtraction of a continuous function. $\square$

In the next section the partial envelope bounds every limit of partial Fubini–Study potentials. The point is to show that both constraints, the multiplier-ideal order and the obstacle on $K$, survive $L^1$ limits.

27.4 An upper bound for partial Fubini–Study limits

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The Bernstein–Markov inequality keeps the partial Fubini–Study potentials below the weight on $K$, up to an error that becomes arbitrarily small as the degree grows. The multiplier-ideal constraint survives in the limit because, on a fixed local modification, the Lelong numbers of the potentials fall short of those of the selecting potential by at most $O(m^{-1})$. To pass the obstacle condition to $L^1$ limits when $K$ has zero smooth volume, we compare the potentials with the envelope of the obstacle alone, which is defined on all of $X$.

Now take $\theta =c_1(L,h)$, assume $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and let $\mu $ be a Bernstein–Markov probability measure for $(K,w)$, with $K$ and $w$ as in section 27.3. Use the selected spaces $V_ m$ and their dimensions $N_ m$ from section 27.1. Choose an orthonormal basis $s_1,\ldots ,s_{N_ m}$ of $V_ m$ for the norm $\| \cdot \| _{m,w,\mu }$ of section 27.2, and define the partial Fubini–Study potential

\[ U_ m=\frac1m\log \sum _{j=1}^{N_ m}|s_ j|_{h^ m}^2 \quad (N_ m>0). \]

Set $U_ m\equiv -\infty $ if $N_ m=0$. When $N_ m>0$, the local expression in a holomorphic frame, as in the proof of proposition 20.1.2, shows that $U_ m\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and that $U_ m$ has analytic singularities. On $K$ it satisfies $\mathrm{e}^{m(U_ m-w)}=B_{m,w,\varphi ,\mu }$.

Let $\pi :Y\to W$ be a local smooth modification of an open set $W\subseteq X$, let $y\in Y$, and write $J_\pi $ for the local holomorphic Jacobian determinant of $\pi $. Each $s_ j$ lies in $V_ m$, so lemma 1.17.9 applies to its local coefficient. Since the Lelong number of the logarithm of a sum of squared moduli of holomorphic functions is the minimum of their vanishing orders, and the smooth metric contributes nothing, we obtain

\[ \nu (U_ m\circ \pi ,y) \geq \nu (\varphi \circ \pi ,y) -\frac{n+\operatorname {ord}_ yJ_\pi }{m} \qquad (N_ m>0). \]

Theorem 27.4.1. The potentials $U_ m$ with $N_ m>0$ form a relatively compact subset of $L^1(X)$. Let $U$ be the $L^1$ limit of a sequence $U_{m_ j}$ with $N_{m_ j}>0$ and $m_ j\to \infty $. Then $U\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$,

\[ U\preceq _{\mathcal I}\varphi , \]

and $U\leq w$ quasi-everywhere on $K$. Consequently

\[ \left(\limsup _{m\to \infty }U_ m\right)^* \leq E_{\theta ,K}[\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}(w). \]

If only finitely many selected spaces are nonzero, the left-hand side is identically $-\infty $.

Proof. Step 1: compactness. The extremal formula for the kernel and the Bernstein–Markov inequality give, for every $\epsilon >0$ and with $C_\epsilon \geq 1$,

\[ U_ m\leq w+\delta _{m,\epsilon }\quad \hbox{on }K, \qquad \delta _{m,\epsilon }=2\epsilon +\frac{2\log C_\epsilon }{m}. \]

No factor depending on $N_ m$ occurs, because the extremal formula is a supremum over single sections of unit norm. Fix one $\epsilon >0$. Since $\delta _{m,\epsilon }\leq 2\epsilon +2\log C_\epsilon $, lemma 1.19.1 gives an upper bound for $\sup _ XU_ m$ that is uniform over all $m$ with $N_ m>0$. On the other hand, the total mass identity gives

\[ \int _ K \mathrm{e}^{m(U_ m-w)}\, \mathrm{d}\mu =N_ m\geq 1. \]

Since $\mu $ is a probability measure, $U_ m-w\geq 0$ somewhere on $K$, and therefore $\sup _ XU_ m\geq \min _ Kw$. The normalized compactness theorem (theorem 1.4.2) now gives relative compactness in $L^1(X)$, with limits in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$.

Step 2: the multiplier-ideal order. Let $U_{m_ j}\to U$ in $L^1(X)$, with $m_ j\to \infty $. Fix a local smooth modification $\pi :Y\to W$ of an open set $W\subseteq X$, and a point $y\in Y$. By lemma 1.22.13, $U_{m_ j}\circ \pi \to U\circ \pi $ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Upper semicontinuity of Lelong numbers (lemma 1.17.6) and the order bound before the theorem give

\[ \nu (U\circ \pi ,y) \geq \limsup _ j\nu (U_{m_ j}\circ \pi ,y) \geq \nu (\varphi \circ \pi ,y). \]

Since $\pi $ and $y$ are arbitrary, the local valuative characterization in theorem 1.16.3 gives $U\preceq _{\mathcal I}\varphi $. Note that the modification and the point are fixed before passing to the limit; the same convergent sequence serves for all of them.

Step 3: the obstacle. Since $K$ may have zero smooth volume, the inequality on $K$ in Step 1 does not pass directly to $L^1$ limits. We therefore use the envelope of the obstacle alone. Set

\[ G=E_{\theta ,K}[V_\theta ]_{\mathcal I}(w). \]

Every $a\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfies $a\leq V_\theta +\sup _ Xa$, and hence $a\preceq _{\mathcal I}V_\theta $. Thus the multiplier-ideal constraint in the definition of $G$ is no restriction, and $G$ is the largest $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function lying below $w$ quasi-everywhere on $K$. Since $U_ m-\delta _{m,\epsilon }$ is such a function, Step 1 gives, on all of $X$,

\[ U_ m\leq G+\delta _{m,\epsilon }. \]

Passing to the $L^1$ limit gives $U\leq G+2\epsilon $ almost everywhere, and hence everywhere by lemma 1.3.3. Letting $\epsilon \downarrow 0$ gives $U\leq G$. Since $G\leq w$ quasi-everywhere on $K$ by theorem 27.3.1, the same holds for $U$. Thus $U$ satisfies both defining conditions of the partial envelope, and $U\leq E_{\theta ,K}[\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}(w)$.

Step 4: the upper limit. By the local Hartogs lemma, if $U_{m_ j}\to U$ in $L^1$ and $x_ j\to x$, then $\limsup _ jU_{m_ j}(x_ j)\leq U(x)$. Given a point $x$, a sequence of indices $m_ j\to \infty $ with $N_{m_ j}>0$ and points $x_ j\to x$, pass to a subsequence along which $U_{m_ j}(x_ j)$ converges to $\limsup _ jU_{m_ j}(x_ j)$ and $U_{m_ j}$ converges in $L^1$. By Steps 2 and 3 the $L^1$ limit is at most the partial envelope, so $\limsup _ jU_{m_ j}(x_ j)\leq E_{\theta ,K}[\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}(w)(x)$. This proves the inequality for the regularized upper limit, also at the poles of the envelope. Indices with $N_ m=0$ contribute only $-\infty $. $\square$

A matching lower bound would require global sections approximating the envelope; for the projective models later in this chapter such sections are constructed explicitly. Before turning to limits of measures, we show that the partial kernel measures are the derivatives of a determinant functional.

27.5 The determinant functional

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

As the continuous weight varies, the partial kernel measures are the derivatives of a single functional, the logarithm of a Gram determinant. Orthogonal projection expresses its second derivative as minus a sum of squared norms, which gives concavity, and the total mass identity gives a Lipschitz bound in terms of the dimension of the selected space.

We return to the norm $\| \cdot \| _{m,v,\nu }$ on the whole manifold from section 27.1, with selecting potential $\varphi \in \mathrm{QPSH}(X)$. Fix $m$ with $N_ m>0$ and a basis of $V_ m$, and let $G_ m(v)$ be the Gram matrix of this basis for $\| \cdot \| _{m,v,\nu }$, a positive definite Hermitian matrix. The determinant functional is

\[ \mathcal F_ m^{(r)}(v) =-\frac{r!}{m^{r+1}} \log \frac{\det G_ m(v)}{\det G_ m(0)}. \]

A change of basis multiplies both determinants by the same positive factor, so $\mathcal F_ m^{(r)}$ does not depend on the basis. If $N_ m=0$, put $\mathcal F_ m^{(r)}=0$.

Proposition 27.5.1. For $v,f\in C(X,\mathbb R)$, the measure $\beta _ m^{(r)}$ at the weight $v$ satisfies

\[ \left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\mathcal F_ m^{(r)}(v+tf) =\int _ X f\, \mathrm{d}\beta _ m^{(r)}. \]

The functional $\mathcal F_ m^{(r)}$ is concave on $C(X,\mathbb R)$, and for $v,w\in C(X,\mathbb R)$ and $c\in \mathbb R$,

\[ \mathcal F_ m^{(r)}(v+c)=\mathcal F_ m^{(r)}(v) +\frac{r!N_ m}{m^ r}c, \qquad |\mathcal F_ m^{(r)}(v)-\mathcal F_ m^{(r)}(w)| \leq \frac{r!N_ m}{m^ r}\| v-w\| _\infty . \]

If $N_ m>0$, the second derivative of $t\mapsto \mathcal F_ m^{(r)}(v+tf)$ is strictly negative unless $f$ is constant.

Proof. Choose a basis $s_1,\ldots ,s_{N_ m}$ that is orthonormal for the weight $v$, so that $G_ m(v)=I$ is the identity matrix of size $N_ m$; the indices $i,j$ below range from $1$ to $N_ m$. Since the weights are bounded, we may differentiate under the integral sign. With pointwise inner products taken in $h^ m$, put

\[ A_{ij}=\int _ X f\langle s_ i,s_ j\rangle _{h^ m}\mathrm{e}^{-mv}\, \mathrm{d}\nu , \qquad C_{ij}=\int _ X f^2\langle s_ i,s_ j\rangle _{h^ m}\mathrm{e}^{-mv}\, \mathrm{d}\nu . \]

The first and second derivatives of $t\mapsto G_ m(v+tf)$ at $t=0$ are $-mA$ and $m^2C$. The identity $(\log \det G)'=\operatorname {tr}(G^{-1}G')$ gives the first derivative of $\mathcal F_ m^{(r)}$, and differentiating it once more yields

\[ \left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^2}{\mathrm{d}t^2}\right|_{t=0}\mathcal F_ m^{(r)}(v+tf) =-\frac{r!}{m^{r-1}} \left(\int _ X f^2B_{m,v,\varphi ,\nu }\, \mathrm{d}\nu -\sum _{i,j}|A_{ij}|^2\right). \]

Let $P_ v$ be the orthogonal projection of the weighted $L^2$ space of measurable sections of $L^ m$ onto $V_ m$. Since $\int _ Xf^2B_{m,v,\varphi ,\nu }\, \mathrm{d}\nu =\sum _ j\| fs_ j\| _{m,v,\nu }^2$ and $P_ v(fs_ j)=\sum _ iA_{ji}s_ i$, Pythagoras' theorem shows that the quantity in parentheses equals

\[ \sum _ j\| (I-P_ v)(fs_ j)\| _{m,v,\nu }^2\geq 0. \]

This proves concavity. Multiplying every Gram entry by $\mathrm{e}^{-mc}$ shows that $\det G_ m(v+c)=\mathrm{e}^{-mcN_ m}\det G_ m(v)$, which is the translation rule. By the first-variation formula and the total mass identity, the derivative of $\mathcal F_ m^{(r)}$ along the segment from $w$ to $v$ is bounded by $(r!N_ m/m^ r)\| v-w\| _\infty $, which gives the Lipschitz estimate.

Suppose finally that the second derivative vanishes. Then $fs_ j\in V_ m$ in $L^2$ for every $j$, so there are $0\ne s\in V_ m$ and $\widetilde s\in V_ m$ with $fs=\widetilde s$ almost everywhere. Both sides are continuous and $\nu $ has a positive density, so $fs=\widetilde s$ everywhere. On a ball where $s\ne 0$, the quotient $\widetilde s/s=f$ is a real-valued holomorphic function, hence a constant $c$. The identity theorem gives $\widetilde s=cs$ on the connected manifold $X$, so $f=c$ wherever $s\neq 0$, and by continuity everywhere. Conversely, for constant $f$ the translation rule shows that the second derivative vanishes. $\square$

Thus $\mathcal F_ m^{(r)}$ is a primitive of the partial kernel measures on every compact complex manifold. This does not affect the obstruction proved in chapter 3 to a primitive of the Hermitian Monge–Ampère operator, since the derivative here is a partial kernel measure and not a Monge–Ampère measure.

Corollary 27.5.2. If $N_ m=o(m^ n)$, then $\beta _ m^{(n)}\to 0$ in total variation, and $\mathcal F_ m^{(n)}(v)\to 0$ uniformly for $v$ in any bounded subset of $C(X)$.

Proof. The measures $\beta _ m^{(n)}$ are positive with total masses $n!N_ m/m^ n\to 0$. The second assertion follows from the Lipschitz estimate, since $\mathcal F_ m^{(n)}(0)=0$. $\square$

For example, suppose that $X$ is not Moishezon. By corollary 21.5.3, either every positive-degree section space of $L$ is zero, or $h^0(X,L^ m)=O(m^ d)$ for some $d<n$. Since $V_ m\subseteq H^0(X,L^ m)$, every sequence of selected spaces satisfies the hypothesis of corollary 27.5.2. By the last assertion of corollary 21.5.3, this applies in particular whenever $X$ fails the bounded mass property of definition 6.5.1.

27.5.1 Dependence on the continuous weight

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

When only the continuous weight changes, the selected space stays the same, and the two norms are related by a multiplication operator compressed to that space. Inverting this operator expresses the new reproducing vectors through the old ones. As a consequence the kernel itself, and not only the determinant functional, depends analytically on the weight.

Keep $m\geq 1$, the selecting potential $\varphi $, the metric $h$ and the probability measure $\nu $ fixed as in section 27.1, and write $B_ v=B_{m,v,\varphi ,\nu }$ and $\beta _ v=\beta _ m^{(r)}$ for the kernel and the kernel measure at the weight $v$. Let $P_ v$ be the orthogonal projection of the weighted $L^2$ space of measurable sections of $L^ m$, with the norm $\| \cdot \| _{m,v,\nu }$, onto $V_ m$, and let

\[ Z_ m=\{ x\in X:s(x)=0\text{ for every }s\in V_ m\} \]

be the common zero set of the selected sections. At each $x\in X$ choose a covector of unit length on the fiber $(L^ m)_ x$; it defines a complex-linear evaluation $\ell _ x:V_ m\to \mathbb C$. Let $k_{v,x}\in V_ m$ be its Riesz vector, so that $\ell _ x(s)=\langle s,k_{v,x}\rangle _{m,v,\nu }$ for all $s\in V_ m$. Then

27.9
\begin{equation} B_ v(x)=\mathrm{e}^{-mv(x)}\ell _ x(k_{v,x}) =\mathrm{e}^{-mv(x)}\| k_{v,x}\| _{m,v,\nu }^2. \end{equation}

These values do not depend on the phase of the chosen covector.

Proposition 27.5.3. The map $v\mapsto B_ v$ from $C(X,\mathbb R)$ to $C(X,\mathbb R)$ is real analytic. The map $v\mapsto \beta _ v$ is real analytic with values in the finite signed measures on $X$, equipped with the total variation norm $\| \cdot \| _{\mathrm{TV}}$. If $V_ m=\{ 0\} $, both maps vanish identically.

Proof. If $V_ m=\{ 0\} $ there is nothing to prove. Fix a real weight $v_0$. For $f\in C(X,\mathbb C)$ define an operator on $V_ m$ by

\[ T(f)s=P_{v_0}(\mathrm{e}^{-mf}s). \]

Since the exponential series converges in the uniform norm, $T$ is an entire function of $f$ with values in the endomorphisms of $V_ m$. Since $P_{v_0}$ is a contraction,

\[ \| T(f)-I\| \leq \mathrm{e}^{m\| f\| _\infty }-1. \]

Hence, for $m\| f\| _\infty <\log (3/2)$, we have $\| T(f)-I\| <1/2$, so the Neumann series for $T(f)^{-1}$ converges in operator norm and $\| T(f)^{-1}\| \leq 2$. For real $f$, the operator $T(f)$ is selfadjoint and represents the new inner product:

\[ \langle s,u\rangle _{m,v_0+f,\nu } =\langle T(f)s,u\rangle _{m,v_0,\nu }. \]

In particular it is positive definite, bounded below by $\mathrm{e}^{-m\| f\| _\infty }I$. The Riesz identity therefore gives

\[ k_{v_0+f,x}=T(f)^{-1}k_{v_0,x}. \]

For complex $f$ the right-hand side is still a holomorphic function of $f$. Consequently

\[ f\longmapsto \mathrm{e}^{-m(v_0(x)+f(x))} \ell _ x\bigl (T(f)^{-1}k_{v_0,x}\bigr ) \]

is a holomorphic extension of $f\mapsto B_{v_0+f}(x)$. Evaluations on the finite-dimensional space $V_ m$ are uniformly bounded over the compact manifold $X$, so the linear map $A\mapsto [x\mapsto \ell _ x(Ak_{v_0,x})]$ from the endomorphisms of $V_ m$ to $C(X)$ is bounded; it is well defined because the phases of $\ell _ x$ and $k_{v_0,x}$ cancel. Hence the extension is holomorphic as a map with values in $C(X)$. Finally, $B\mapsto (r!/m^ r)B\nu $ is a bounded linear map into finite measures with the total variation norm, which proves the assertion about $\beta _ v$. At points of $Z_ m$ the Riesz vector is zero, so the argument includes the common zero set. $\square$

Now let $I\subset \mathbb R$ be an open interval and let $t\mapsto v_ t$ be a smooth curve in $C(X,\mathbb R)$. The complete exponential Bell polynomials are

\[ \mathcal B_ j(x_1,\ldots ,x_ j) =\sum _{\sum _{\ell =1}^ j\ell n_\ell =j} j!\prod _{\ell =1}^ j \frac{(x_\ell /\ell !)^{n_\ell }}{n_\ell !}, \qquad j\geq 1, \]

where the sum runs over nonnegative integers $n_1,\ldots ,n_ j$. Write $\mathcal B_ j(-mv_ t)$ for $\mathcal B_ j$ evaluated at the first $j$ derivatives of $-mv_ t$ with respect to $t$, so that $\partial _ t^ j\mathrm{e}^{-mv_ t}=\mathrm{e}^{-mv_ t}\mathcal B_ j(-mv_ t)$ by Faà di Bruno's formula. Define operators on $V_ m$ by

\[ \mathcal T_{t,j}u=-P_{v_ t}\bigl (\mathcal B_ j(-mv_ t)u\bigr ), \qquad u\in V_ m. \]

For $k\geq 1$, let $\Lambda _ k$ be the set of compositions of $k$, that is, of ordered tuples $\alpha =(\alpha _1,\ldots ,\alpha _ p)$ of positive integers with sum $k$, and set $\alpha !=\prod _{j=1}^ p\alpha _ j!$.

Theorem 27.5.4. For every $k\geq 1$,

27.10
\begin{equation} \partial _ t^ k k_{v_ t,x} =\sum _{\alpha \in \Lambda _ k}\frac{k!}{\alpha !} \mathcal T_{t,\alpha _ p}\cdots \mathcal T_{t,\alpha _1}k_{v_ t,x}. \end{equation}

Suppose moreover that $s\geq 1$, that $J\Subset I$ is a compact interval, and that constants $A,R>0$ satisfy

\[ \sup _{t\in J}\| \partial _ t^ jv_ t\| _\infty \leq AR^ j(j!)^ s\qquad (j\geq 1). \]

Then there are constants $C,L>0$, depending only on the fixed data and on $m,r,J,A,R,s$, such that

27.11
\begin{equation} \sup _{t\in J} \left(\| \partial _ t^ kB_{v_ t}\| _\infty +\| \partial _ t^ k\beta _{v_ t}\| _{\mathrm{TV}}\right) \leq CL^ k(k!)^ s\qquad (k\geq 0). \end{equation}

Thus the kernel and the kernel measure are of Gevrey class $s$ in the parameter.

Proof. The derivative formula. By proposition 27.5.3, $k_{v_ t,x}$ depends smoothly on $t$, for any fixed norm on $V_ m$. Fix a real parameter $t$ and set

\[ H(a)=P_{v_ t}\bigl (\mathrm{e}^{-m(v_{t+a}-v_ t)}\, \cdot \, \bigr )|_{V_ m}. \]

Then $H(0)=I$, $H^{(j)}(0)=-\mathcal T_{t,j}$ by Faà di Bruno's formula, and $k_{v_{t+a},x}=H(a)^{-1}k_{v_ t,x}$ as in the proof of proposition 27.5.3. Since $H(a)-I=O(|a|)$, truncating the Neumann series of $H(a)^{-1}$ after $k$ powers leaves a remainder $O(|a|^{k+1})$. Expanding the truncated series to order $k$ in $a$ gives (27.10), with its ordered products and coefficients $k!/\alpha !$. Only a finite Taylor jet of the smooth curve is used.

Gevrey bounds. For nonnegative integers $n_\ell $ with $\sum \ell n_\ell =j$,

\[ \prod _{\ell =1}^ j(\ell !)^{(s-1)n_\ell } \leq (j!)^{s-1}. \]

The sum $\sum \prod _\ell (n_\ell !)^{-1}$ over these tuples is the coefficient of $z^ j$ in $\exp (z/(1-z))$, and evaluation at $z=1/2$ shows that it is at most $\mathrm{e}\, 2^ j$. Together with the assumed bounds this gives

\[ \sup _{t\in J}\| \mathcal B_ j(-mv_ t)\| _\infty \leq L_0^ j(j!)^ s \]

for some $L_0>0$. Since $P_{v_ t}$ is a contraction, each ordered product in (27.10) has operator norm at most $L_0^ k(\alpha !)^ s$. There are $2^{k-1}$ compositions of $k$, and $\alpha !\leq k!$. Since $v_ t$ is uniformly bounded for $t\in J$, the norms $\| \cdot \| _{m,v_ t,\nu }$ are uniformly equivalent, and the evaluations and Riesz vectors are bounded uniformly on $J\times X$. Hence the $t$-derivatives of the unweighted diagonal $x\mapsto \ell _ x(k_{v_ t,x})$ are bounded in $C(X)$ by $C(2L_0)^ k(k!)^ s$. The factor $\mathrm{e}^{-mv_ t}$ satisfies bounds of the same Gevrey order, by the estimate for $\mathcal B_ j(-mv_ t)$. Leibniz's formula and $j!(k-j)!\leq k!$ then give (27.11) for $B_{v_ t}$, and the bounded linear map $B\mapsto (r!/m^ r)B\nu $ gives the bound in total variation. $\square$

Corollary 27.5.5. Let $v_ t=v+tq$ with $v,q\in C(X,\mathbb R)$. For $x\notin Z_ m$, the probability measure

27.12
\begin{equation} \mathrm{d}\sigma _{t,x}(y) =\frac{|k_{v_ t,x}(y)|_{h^ m}^2\mathrm{e}^{-mv_ t(y)}}{\| k_{v_ t,x}\| _{m,v_ t,\nu }^2}\, \mathrm{d}\nu (y) \end{equation}

satisfies

27.13
\begin{equation} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\log B_{v_ t}(x) =m\left(\int _ Xq\, \mathrm{d}\sigma _{t,x}-q(x)\right). \end{equation}

On $Z_ m$ the kernel and all its $t$-derivatives vanish. Moreover, $\int _ X\partial _ tB_{v_ t}\, \mathrm{d}\nu =0$.

Proof. Since $\mathcal T_{t,1}u=mP_{v_ t}(qu)$, the case $k=1$ of (27.10) gives $\partial _ tk_{v_ t,x}=mP_{v_ t}(qk_{v_ t,x})$. Applying the fixed evaluation $\ell _ x$ and the Riesz identity gives

\[ \partial _ t\ell _ x(k_{v_ t,x}) =m\int _ Xq|k_{v_ t,x}|_{h^ m}^2 \mathrm{e}^{-mv_ t}\, \mathrm{d}\nu . \]

Dividing by the positive number $\ell _ x(k_{v_ t,x})=\| k_{v_ t,x}\| _{m,v_ t,\nu }^2$ and differentiating the factor $\mathrm{e}^{-mv_ t(x)}$ in (27.9) proves (27.13); the density in (27.12) has integral one by the definition of the norm. At points of $Z_ m$ the evaluations and Riesz vectors vanish for every weight. Finally, differentiating the identity $\int _ XB_{v_ t}\, \mathrm{d}\nu =N_ m$ in $C(X)$ proves the last assertion. For constant $q$ the right-hand side of (27.13) vanishes, in accordance with the invariance of the kernel under adding a constant to the weight. $\square$

The uniform norm on the weights is essential in these results, because it controls the weight on the whole region of integration. The next example shows that real-analytic dependence of the weight on a parameter at each interior point of a domain does not suffice.

Example 27.5.6. On the unit disk $D\subset \mathbb C$ with Euclidean area $\mathrm{d}A$, let $K_ t$ be the Bergman kernel of the Hilbert space of holomorphic functions $f$ on $D$ with the norm $(\int _ D|f|^2\rho _ t\, \mathrm{d}A)^{1/2}$, where

\[ \rho _ t(z)=1+\frac{t^2}{(1-|z|^2)^2+t^2},\qquad t\in \mathbb R. \]

The weights $-\log \rho _ t$ are bounded and depend real analytically on $(z,t)\in D\times \mathbb R$, but

27.14
\begin{equation} K_ t(0,0)= \begin{cases} [\pi (1+|t|\arctan (1/|t|))]^{-1},& t\ne 0,\\ \pi ^{-1},& t=0. \end{cases} \end{equation}

In particular, $K_ t(0,0)=\pi ^{-1}-|t|/2+O(t^2)$, so $K_ t(0,0)$ is not differentiable at $t=0$.

Proof. The density $\rho _ t$ is radial and lies between one and two. By radial symmetry, the constant functions are orthogonal to the holomorphic functions vanishing at the origin, so the reproducing value at the center is the reciprocal of $\int _ D\rho _ t\, \mathrm{d}A$. Polar coordinates and the substitution $a=1-r^2$ give, for $t\ne 0$,

\[ \int _ D\rho _ t\, \mathrm{d}A =\pi \int _0^1\left(1+\frac{t^2}{a^2+t^2}\right)\mathrm{d}a =\pi \bigl (1+|t|\arctan (1/|t|)\bigr ). \]

For $t=0$ the integral is $\pi $. This proves (27.14) and the expansion. For every $t\ne 0$ we have $\| \log \rho _ t\| _{L^\infty (D)}=\log 2$, whereas $\rho _0=1$. Thus the curve $t\mapsto \log \rho _ t$ is not continuous at $t=0$ for the uniform norm, and the example does not contradict proposition 27.5.3. $\square$

Throughout these estimates the degree $m$ and the selected space were fixed, and the constants depend on $m$. A limit envelope need not even be differentiable in the weight, as the corner in example 13.1.21 shows; differentiating limits would require estimates uniform in $m$.

27.5.2 Stability of normalized kernels

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The total mass of $B_ v\nu $ is the dimension of the selected space, so dividing by this dimension gives probability measures whose dependence on the weight can be controlled independently of the dimension. The squared reproducing kernel defines a symmetric coupling of such a probability measure with itself, and the positivity of the associated Markov operator sharpens the resulting variation bound to an optimal one.

Keep the fixed data of section 27.5.1, and assume $N_ m>0$. For each real $v\in C(X)$, the normalized kernel probability is

27.17
\begin{equation} \mu _ v=\frac{B_ v}{N_ m}\nu . \end{equation}

Choose an orthonormal basis $s_1,\ldots ,s_{N_ m}$ of $V_ m$ for the weight $v$, and use the unit evaluations $\ell _ x$ of section 27.5.1 to set

\[ F_ i(x)=\mathrm{e}^{-mv(x)/2}\ell _ x(s_ i),\qquad C_ v(x,y)=\sum _ i F_ i(x)\overline{F_ i(y)}. \]

The function $C_ v$ depends on the phases of the evaluations, but $|C_ v|$ does not. For $x\notin Z_ m$ let $\sigma _{v,x}$ be the probability measure (27.12) at the weight $v$, and define

\[ \mathcal Q_ v f(x)=\int _ X f(y)\, \mathrm{d}\sigma _{v,x}(y); \]

on $Z_ m$, which is a $\mu _ v$-null set, put $\mathcal Q_ vf=0$.

Lemma 27.5.7. The measure

27.18
\begin{equation} \mathrm{d}J_ v(x,y)=\frac{|C_ v(x,y)|^2}{N_ m} \, \mathrm{d}\nu (x)\, \mathrm{d}\nu (y) \end{equation}

on $X\times X$ is a symmetric probability measure whose two marginals are equal to $\mu _ v$. The operator $\mathcal Q_ v$ is a selfadjoint, positive semidefinite contraction of $L^2(\mu _ v)$; it fixes the constant functions $\mu _ v$-almost everywhere and preserves integrals against $\mu _ v$.

Proof. Orthonormality gives $\int _ X|C_ v(x,y)|^2\, \mathrm{d}\nu (y)=B_ v(x)$, so the first marginal of $J_ v$ is $\mu _ v$. By symmetry so is the second, and the total mass identity shows that $J_ v$ has mass one. Off $Z_ m$, dividing $|C_ v(x,\cdot )|^2\, \mathrm{d}\nu $ by $B_ v(x)$ gives the squared Riesz-vector density in (27.12). Thus the probabilities $\sigma _{v,x}$ are the conditional probabilities of $J_ v$ given the first coordinate, and $\mathcal Q_ v$ is the associated Markov operator. Since these conditional probabilities have mass one and the marginal of $J_ v$ is $\mu _ v$, the operator $\mathcal Q_ v$ fixes constants and preserves integrals; Jensen's inequality shows that it is a contraction of $L^2(\mu _ v)$, and the symmetry of $J_ v$ shows that it is selfadjoint. For $f\in L^2(\mu _ v)$, expanding the finite sum gives

\[ \langle \mathcal Q_ v f,f\rangle _{L^2(\mu _ v)} =\frac1{N_ m}\sum _{i,j} \left|\int _ X fF_ i\overline{F_ j}\, \mathrm{d}\nu \right|^2\geq 0. \]

Each of these integrals is independent of the phases, and it exists because $|F_ i\overline{F_ j}|\leq B_ v$ and $f\in L^1(\mu _ v)$. This proves positivity. $\square$

For $v,q\in C(X,\mathbb R)$ we write $D\mu _ v[q]$ for the derivative of $t\mapsto \mu _{v+tq}$ at $t=0$ in total variation, which exists by proposition 27.5.3.

Proposition 27.5.8. For $q,v,w\in C(X,\mathbb R)$, write $\operatorname {osc}_ Xq=\sup _ Xq-\inf _ Xq$. Then

27.19
\begin{equation} D\mu _ v[q]=m(\mathcal Q_ vq-q)\mu _ v, \qquad \| D\mu _ v[q]\| _{\mathrm{TV}} \leq \frac m2\operatorname {osc}_ Xq. \end{equation}

Consequently

27.20
\begin{equation} \| \mu _ w-\mu _ v\| _{\mathrm{TV}} \leq 2\tanh \left(\frac m4\operatorname {osc}_ X(w-v)\right). \end{equation}

These bounds do not depend on $N_ m$. In particular, let $v_ m,w_ m\in C(X,\mathbb R)$ along a sequence of degrees with nonzero selected spaces. If $m\operatorname {osc}_ X(w_ m-v_ m)\to 0$, then the two sequences of normalized kernel probabilities have the same weak, or total variation, limit whenever one of them converges in that topology.

Proof. The formula for $D\mu _ v[q]$ follows from corollary 27.5.5, since $N_ m$ does not depend on the weight. For the bound, put $g=q-\mathcal Q_ vq$, $A=\{ g>0\} $ and $p=\mu _ v(A)$. Since $\mathcal Q_ v$ preserves integrals, $g$ has mean zero, and selfadjointness gives

\[ \int _ Ag\, \mathrm{d}\mu _ v =\langle q,(I-\mathcal Q_ v)\mathbf1_ A\rangle _{L^2(\mu _ v)}. \]

The function $(I-\mathcal Q_ v)\mathbf1_ A$ is nonnegative on $A$ and nonpositive on its complement, almost everywhere, and its integral is zero. Subtracting $\inf _ Xq$ from $q$ does not change the pairing, which is therefore at most

\[ \operatorname {osc}_ Xq \bigl (p-\langle \mathbf1_ A,\mathcal Q_ v\mathbf1_ A\rangle \bigr ). \]

Positivity of $\mathcal Q_ v$, applied to $\mathbf1_ A-p$, gives $\langle \mathbf1_ A,\mathcal Q_ v\mathbf1_ A\rangle \geq p^2$: since $\mathcal Q_ v$ fixes constants and preserves integrals, $\langle \mathbf1_ A-p,\mathcal Q_ v(\mathbf1_ A-p)\rangle =\langle \mathbf1_ A,\mathcal Q_ v\mathbf1_ A\rangle -p^2$. As $g$ has mean zero, it follows that

\[ \| g\| _{L^1(\mu _ v)} =2\int _ Ag\, \mathrm{d}\mu _ v \leq 2\operatorname {osc}_ Xq\, p(1-p) \leq \tfrac 12\operatorname {osc}_ Xq. \]

The total variation norm of a signed measure is the integral of the absolute value of its density, so $\| D\mu _ v[q]\| _{\mathrm{TV}}=m\| g\| _{L^1(\mu _ v)}$, and (27.19) follows.

For the global estimate, take $q=w-v$, $v_ t=v+tq$, $\mu _ t=\mu _{v_ t}$ and $\mathcal Q_ t=\mathcal Q_{v_ t}$ for $0\leq t\leq 1$, and put $a=m\operatorname {osc}_ Xq$. For a Borel set $E$, the function $p_ t=\mu _ t(E)$ is differentiable, because integration against $\mathbf1_ E$ is a bounded linear functional for the total variation norm, and selfadjointness gives

\[ p'_ t=-m\langle q,(I-\mathcal Q_ t)\mathbf1_ E\rangle _{L^2(\mu _ t)}. \]

The function $(I-\mathcal Q_ t)\mathbf1_ E$ has positive and negative parts whose integrals both equal $p_ t-\langle \mathbf1_ E,\mathcal Q_ t\mathbf1_ E\rangle $. Bounding $q$ above and below on the supports of these parts, and applying positivity to $\mathbf1_ E-p_ t$ as before, we obtain

\[ |p'_ t|\leq a\bigl (p_ t-\langle \mathbf1_ E,\mathcal Q_ t\mathbf1_ E\rangle \bigr ) \leq ap_ t(1-p_ t). \]

All the kernels $B_{v_ t}$ are positive exactly off the common zero set $Z_ m$, so the probabilities $\mu _ t$ are mutually absolutely continuous. Hence, if $p_0$ is zero or one, then $p_ t=p_0$ for all $t$. Otherwise $0<p_ t<1$ for all $t$, and integration gives

\[ \left|\log \frac{p_1}{1-p_1}-\log \frac{p_0}{1-p_0}\right|\leq a. \]

Let $\sigma (z)=\mathrm{e}^ z/(1+\mathrm{e}^ z)$ be the logistic function. If $p_1\geq p_0$ and $z=\log (p_0/(1-p_0))$, then

\[ p_1-p_0\leq \sigma (z+a)-\sigma (z) =\frac{\sinh (a/2)}{\cosh (z+a/2)+\cosh (a/2)} \leq \tanh (a/4). \]

The case $p_1\leq p_0$ is symmetric. Since $\mu _ w-\mu _ v$ has total mass zero, its total variation norm is $2\sup _ E|\mu _ w(E)-\mu _ v(E)|$, which proves (27.20). Under the hypothesis of the last assertion the right-hand side tends to zero, which gives the final statement. $\square$

Example 27.5.9. For each $a>0$ and $m\geq 1$, the bound (27.20) is optimal as a supremum over smooth weights, already for a selected space of dimension one. Indeed, let $X=\mathbb P^1$ with the trivial line bundle and its trivial metric, take the selecting potential zero, and let $\nu $ be the Fubini–Study area normalized to mass one. Then there are smooth real weights $w_ j$ with $m\operatorname {osc}_ Xw_ j=a$ such that

\[ \| \mu _{w_ j}-\mu _0\| _{\mathrm{TV}}\longrightarrow 2\tanh (a/4). \]

Proof. The selected space consists of the constant functions, so the normalized kernel probability at a weight $u$ is $\mathrm{e}^{-mu}\nu /\int _ X\mathrm{e}^{-mu}\, \mathrm{d}\nu $. Choose a spherical cap $E$ with $\nu (E)=p=(1+\mathrm{e}^{a/2})^{-1}$; its boundary circle has zero area. Choose smooth functions $0\leq \chi _ j\leq 1$, equal to one on an inner cap and zero outside an outer cap, converging almost everywhere to $\mathbf1_ E$; each has oscillation one. Put $w_ j=-a\chi _ j/m$. The densities $\mathrm{e}^{a\chi _ j}$ converge boundedly almost everywhere, and their normalizing constants converge to a positive limit, so $\mu _{w_ j}$ converges in total variation to

\[ \frac{\mathrm{e}^{a\mathbf1_ E}}{Z}\nu , \qquad Z=1-p+\mathrm{e}^ ap=\mathrm{e}^{a/2}. \]

This limit gives $E$ the mass $1-p$, and its density with respect to $\nu $ is greater than one on $E$ and less than one outside $E$. Its total variation distance from $\nu =\mu _0$ is therefore $2(1-2p)=2\tanh (a/4)$. The triangle inequality transfers this limit to the measures $\mu _{w_ j}$. This shows that the bound is optimal as a supremum; we do not claim that it is attained by a smooth weight. $\square$

At the scale of perturbations of size $1/m$, the analytic dependence on the weight is also controlled independently of the dimension of the selected space.

Proposition 27.5.10. Put $\rho =\log (3/2)$. The map $f\mapsto \mu _{v+f/m}$ extends holomorphically to the ball $\{ f\in C(X,\mathbb C):\| f\| _\infty <\rho \} $, with values in complex measures of total variation at most three. For real $q\in C(X)$ and $k\geq 1$,

27.21
\begin{equation} \left\| \left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^ k}{\mathrm{d}t^ k}\right|_{t=0} \mu _{v+tq}\right\| _{\mathrm{TV}} \leq 3k!\left(\frac{m\operatorname {osc}_ Xq}{2\rho }\right)^ k. \end{equation}

The radius $\rho $ and the bound three do not depend on $m$, $N_ m$ or the real base weight $v$.

Proof. On $V_ m$ use $T(f)=P_ v(\mathrm{e}^{-f}\, \cdot \, )$, the operator in the proof of proposition 27.5.3 for the perturbation $f/m$ of the weight $v$. That proof gives $\| T(f)^{-1}\| \leq 2$ on the ball. The extended measure has the density

\[ \frac{\mathrm{e}^{-mv(x)-f(x)}}{N_ m} \ell _ x\bigl (T(f)^{-1}k_{v,x}\bigr ) \]

with respect to $\nu $. By the Riesz identity at the weight $v$, its absolute value is at most $2\mathrm{e}^{\| f\| _\infty }B_ v(x)/N_ m\leq 3B_ v(x)/N_ m$, so its total variation is at most three. Holomorphy follows from the same finite-dimensional construction and the bounded linear passage from densities to measures. For a complex constant $c$ we have $T(f+c)=\mathrm{e}^{-c}T(f)$, and the resulting factor $\mathrm{e}^{c}$ cancels the factor $\mathrm{e}^{-c}$ in the density; thus adding constants to $f$ does not change the extension. For nonconstant real $q$, replace $q$ by $q-c$ with $c=(\sup _ Xq+\inf _ Xq)/2$, so that $\| q-c\| _\infty =\operatorname {osc}_ Xq/2$. Then $t\mapsto \mu _{v+tq}$ extends holomorphically to the disk $|t|<\rho /(m\| q-c\| _\infty )$, with total variation at most three. The Cauchy estimates for Banach-valued holomorphic functions, applied on circles of radius less than $\rho /(m\| q-c\| _\infty )$, followed by letting the radius increase to that value, prove (27.21). If $q$ is constant, the measure does not depend on $t$, and all derivatives vanish. $\square$

The hypothesis $m\operatorname {osc}_ X(w_ m-v_ m)\to 0$ in proposition 27.5.8 cannot in general be replaced by uniform convergence $w_ m-v_ m\to 0$: a perturbation of order $1/m$ along the fibers of a product survives in a nonzero measure limit.

Example 27.5.11. Let $Y$ be a connected compact complex manifold of positive dimension with a smooth positive probability volume $\nu _ Y$. On $X=\mathbb P^1\times Y$, use the pullback of $\mathcal O(1)$ with its Fubini–Study metric, the selecting potential zero, and the volume $\nu =\nu _{\mathrm{FS}}\otimes \nu _ Y$, where $\nu _{\mathrm{FS}}$ is the Fubini–Study area of mass one. Choose a nonconstant $q\in C(Y,\mathbb R)$ and put

\[ v_ m(b,y)=q(y)/m,\qquad Z_ q=\int _ Y\mathrm{e}^{-q}\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ Y,\qquad \nu _ q=Z_ q^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-q}\nu _ Y. \]

Then $N_ m=m+1$ and, for every $m$, the kernel and the normalized kernel probability at the weight $v_ m$ are

27.22
\begin{equation} B_{m,v_ m}(b,y)=(m+1)\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-q(y)}}{Z_ q}, \qquad \mu _{m,v_ m}=\nu _{\mathrm{FS}}\otimes \nu _ q. \end{equation}

Thus $v_ m\to 0$ uniformly, but the kernel measures with $r=1$ converge in total variation to $\nu _{\mathrm{FS}}\otimes \nu _ q$, whereas at the weight zero the limit is $\nu _{\mathrm{FS}}\otimes \nu _ Y$. In both cases the potentials $m^{-1}\log B_{m,v}$ tend uniformly to zero. With the normalization by the dimension of $X$, both sequences of kernel measures tend to zero in total variation.

Proof. Every section descends to $\mathbb P^1$, because the fibers $\{ b\} \times Y$ are compact and connected, as in proposition 27.7.1. Hence the selected space is $H^0(\mathbb P^1,\mathcal O(m))$, of dimension $m+1$. Its unweighted Bergman kernel for $\nu _{\mathrm{FS}}$ is constant, since the unitary group acts transitively on $\mathbb P^1$ and preserves the metric and the volume; its integral $m+1$ determines the constant. For a section pulled back from $\mathbb P^1$, the norm on $X$ at the weight $v_ m$ is $Z_ q$ times the norm on $\mathbb P^1$, which proves (27.22). At the weight zero the normalized kernel probability is $\nu _{\mathrm{FS}}\otimes \nu _ Y$. These probabilities differ: equality would force $\mathrm{e}^{-q}/Z_ q=1$ almost everywhere, and then everywhere, because $q$ is continuous and every nonempty open set has positive volume; this contradicts the choice of $q$. The kernel measures with $r=1$ are $(m+1)/m$ times these probabilities, and the normalization in dimension $1+\dim Y\geq 2$ gives measures tending to zero in total variation. Finally, $m^{-1}\log B_{m,v_ m}=m^{-1}\bigl (\log (m+1)-q-\log Z_ q\bigr )$ and $m^{-1}\log B_{m,0}=m^{-1}\log (m+1)$ both tend uniformly to zero. $\square$

The same phenomenon, in which a fiber probability survives in the limit, reappears in section 27.11, where the limit measure on the total space carries the conditional fiber probabilities of the integration volume. So far the norm varied within a fixed selected space. Under a modification the integrability condition changes as well, and the next section compares both the selected spaces and their norms.

27.6 Partial kernels under a modification

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A modification identifies holomorphic sections, but it changes the integrability condition that defines multiplier ideals; the correction is a twist by the relative canonical bundle, through the Jacobian. If the relative canonical bundle carries the metric determined by two volume forms, the same Jacobian factor makes the identification of section spaces an isometry. The partial kernel measures downstairs are then exactly the pushforwards of those upstairs, and the convergence theorem on compact Kähler manifolds descends to every compact complex manifold that admits a Kähler modification.

Let $\pi :Y\to X$ be a modification of connected compact complex manifolds. We use the exceptional set $S\subset X$, its inverse image $D=\pi ^{-1}(S)\subset Y$, the relative canonical bundle $R_\pi $ and its Jacobian section $J_\pi $ from section 1.22.7, together with the transformation of multiplier ideals in lemma 1.22.15 and the invariance of the multiplier-ideal order in lemma 1.22.16.

Proposition 27.6.1. Let $\theta $ be a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form on $X$ and let $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. For $v\in C(X)$ let $E_ X(v)=E_{\theta ,X}[\varphi ]_{\mathcal I}(v)$ be the partial envelope with obstacle $v$ on the whole of $X$. Then

\[ E_{\pi ^*\theta ,Y}[\varphi \circ \pi ]_{\mathcal I}(v\circ \pi ) =E_ X(v)\circ \pi . \]

Moreover, for every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ the non-pluripolar measures satisfy

\[ \pi _*\mathrm{MA}_{\pi ^*\theta }(u\circ \pi ) =\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u) \]

as positive Borel measures, possibly of infinite total mass.

Proof. By proposition 1.22.12, $a\mapsto a\circ \pi $ is a bijection from $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ onto $\mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta )$. For a continuous obstacle on the whole manifold, the quasi-everywhere and the pointwise obstacle conditions are equivalent, as recalled in section 1.19, and since $\pi $ is surjective, $a\leq v$ on $X$ if and only if $a\circ \pi \leq v\circ \pi $ on $Y$. Together with lemma 1.22.16, this identifies the two families of competitors. By theorem 27.3.1, each partial envelope is the largest member of its family, which proves the envelope identity; no interchange of pullback and regularized supremum is needed.

The map $\pi $ is a biholomorphism from $Y\setminus D$ onto $X\setminus S$, so locality of the non-pluripolar product identifies the two measures there. Neither measure charges the analytic sets $D$ and $S$. Testing against nonnegative Borel functions, or exhausting the complements by increasing compact sets, proves the pushforward identity, including the case of infinite total mass. $\square$

Choose smooth positive probability volumes $\nu _ X$ on $X$ and $\nu _ Y$ on $Y$. They determine a smooth Hermitian metric $h_ R$ on $R_\pi $ such that

27.23
\begin{equation} |J_\pi |_{h_ R}^2\, \nu _ Y=\pi ^*\nu _ X. \end{equation}

Explicitly, let $f_ X$ and $f_ Y$ be the densities of $\nu _ X$ and $\nu _ Y$ in local coordinates. The local frame of $R_\pi $ given by the quotient of the coordinate canonical frames is given the squared norm $(f_ X\circ \pi )/f_ Y$. This function is smooth and strictly positive, also where $J_\pi $ vanishes, and the transformation rules of the coordinate densities show that it defines a metric. Equation (27.23) is an identity of smooth top-degree forms; it does not define the pullback of an arbitrary measure.

Proposition 27.6.2. Let the line bundle $L$ with metric $h$, the selecting potential $\varphi \in \mathrm{QPSH}(X)$ and the continuous weight $v\in C(X)$ be as in section 27.1, and use the volume $\nu _ X$ on $X$. On $Y$, use the line bundles $\pi ^*L^ m\otimes R_\pi $ with the metrics $\pi ^*h^ m\otimes h_ R$, the selecting potential $\varphi \circ \pi $, the continuous weight $v\circ \pi $ and the volume $\nu _ Y$, and define the kernels and kernel measures as in section 27.1, with the fixed factor $(R_\pi ,h_ R)$ added. Then the identification $s\mapsto J_\pi \pi ^*s$,

\[ H^0(X,L^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\varphi )) \simeq H^0(Y,\pi ^*L^ m\otimes R_\pi \otimes \mathcal I(m\varphi \circ \pi )), \]

is an isometry for the continuous weighted norms. The kernels and kernel measures on $Y$ and $X$ satisfy

\[ B_{Y,m}=|J_\pi |_{h_ R}^2(B_{X,m}\circ \pi ), \qquad \pi _*\beta _{Y,m}^{(r)}=\beta _{X,m}^{(r)} \]

for the same normalization integer $r$, also when the section spaces are zero.

Proof. The identification is lemma 1.22.15 with $M=L^ m$ and $a=m\varphi $. By (27.23) and the change of variables formula,

\[ \int _ Y|J_\pi \pi ^*s|_{\pi ^*h^ m\otimes h_ R}^2\mathrm{e}^{-mv\circ \pi }\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ Y =\int _ X|s|_{h^ m}^2\mathrm{e}^{-mv}\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ X. \]

The selecting potential has already determined the section spaces and does not enter the norms. Hence the identification maps an orthonormal basis to an orthonormal basis, and summing the squared pointwise norms of the basis vectors gives the kernel formula, also at the zeros of $J_\pi $. Multiplying by $r!/m^ r$ and integrating a continuous function pulled back from $X$ proves the measure formula. $\square$

27.6.1 Convergence with a Kähler modification

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Through the Jacobian isometry, the equilibrium theorem on a compact Kähler manifold can be pushed down along a modification. The limit does not depend on the modification, because both the partial envelope and its non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère measure descend.

Theorem 27.6.3. Suppose that $X$ admits a modification from a compact Kähler manifold. Let $L\to X$ be a holomorphic line bundle with a smooth Hermitian metric $h$, put $\theta =c_1(L,h)$, and let $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (\varphi )(X)>0. \]

Then, for every $v\in C(X)$ and every smooth positive probability volume $\nu _ X$, the partial kernel measures on $X$ satisfy

\[ \frac{n!}{m^ n}B_{m,v,\varphi ,\nu _ X}\, \nu _ X \rightharpoonup \mathrm{MA}_\theta (E_ X(v)). \]

The limit measure is finite and nonzero. Its total mass is

\[ M=\lim _{m\to \infty }\frac{n!}{m^ n} h^0(X,L^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\varphi )), \]

which does not depend on $v$ or $\nu _ X$. The limit does not depend on the choice of Kähler modification.

Proof. Choose a modification $\pi :Y\to X$ from a compact Kähler manifold $Y$, and a smooth positive probability volume $\nu _ Y$ on $Y$. The pullback $\varphi \circ \pi $ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta )$, and by proposition 27.6.1 its non-pluripolar mass equals the given positive mass of $\varphi $. This mass is finite, by the theory of closed classes on compact Kähler manifolds.

Apply [Theorem 14.3.1, SGPT] on $Y$, with the line bundle $\pi ^*L$, the auxiliary twisting line bundle $R_\pi $, the continuous obstacle $v\circ \pi $ on $Y$ and the probability volume $\nu _ Y$. Its Bernstein–Markov hypothesis follows from the proof of proposition 27.2.1, since a fixed smooth auxiliary bundle only changes the local constants. The multiplier ideals and the continuous weighted norms in that theorem agree exactly with those of proposition 27.6.2. The theorem gives

\[ \beta _{Y,m}^{(n)}\rightharpoonup \mathrm{MA}_{\pi ^*\theta } \bigl (E_{\pi ^*\theta ,Y}[\varphi \circ \pi ]_{\mathcal I}(v\circ \pi )\bigr ), \]

and the limit measure has finite positive mass.

Now push this convergence forward to $X$, testing against continuous functions on $X$. By the kernel identity of proposition 27.6.2 and the envelope and pushforward identities of proposition 27.6.1, this gives the asserted limit on $X$. Testing the constant function one shows that $n!N_ m/m^ n$ converges to the total mass of the limit, which proves the formula for $M$; the dimensions $N_ m$ involve neither $v$ nor the volumes. Finally, the limit is expressed entirely on $X$, so it does not depend on the modification. $\square$

Since $M>0$, the sections of $L$ grow at the maximal rate $m^ n$, so $X$ is Moishezon by corollary 21.5.3. A Kähler modification also forces the bounded mass property, by lemma 21.4.1; the Jacobian identities, by contrast, hold for every modification of compact complex manifolds. When the section spaces grow more slowly than $m^ n$, the total mass identity shows that the kernel measures must instead be normalized by the growth degree of $N_ m$, as in section 27.7.

Corollary 27.6.4. Under the hypotheses of theorem 27.6.3, let $M$ be the limit of the normalized dimensions in that theorem. The determinant functionals $\mathcal F_ m^{(n)}$ of section 27.5 converge pointwise on $C(X)$ to

\[ \mathcal F(v)=\int _0^1\! \left(\int _ X v\, \mathrm{d}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (E_ X(tv))\right)\mathrm{d}t. \]

This functional is concave and vanishes at zero, and for $v,w\in C(X,\mathbb R)$ and $c\in \mathbb R$,

\[ \mathcal F(v+c)=\mathcal F(v)+Mc,\qquad |\mathcal F(v)-\mathcal F(w)|\leq M\| v-w\| _\infty . \]

Proof. Here $N_ m=\dim H^0(X,L^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\varphi ))$. Integrate the first-variation formula of proposition 27.5.1 along the segment $t\mapsto tv$, $0\leq t\leq 1$. For each fixed $t$, the integrand converges by the theorem, and its absolute value is bounded by

\[ \| v\| _\infty \frac{n!N_ m}{m^ n}, \]

which is bounded in $m$ because it converges to $M\| v\| _\infty $. Dominated convergence on $[0,1]$ gives the formula; the limit integrand is measurable in $t$ as the pointwise limit of continuous functions. Concavity, the translation identity and the Lipschitz bound pass from $\mathcal F_ m^{(n)}$ to the pointwise limit, since $n!N_ m/m^ n\to M$. $\square$

This argument uses only weak convergence of measures along a segment of weights. It does not give a lower bound for the partial Fubini–Study potentials complementing theorem 27.4.1; such a bound would require the construction of sections approximating the envelope.

27.7 Kernels on a fibration

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On a fibration with compact connected fibers, every section of a pulled-back line bundle is constant along the fibers and descends to the base. If probability measures are chosen on the fibers, this descent becomes an isometry, and the kernel measures on the total space are obtained from those on the base by integration along the fibers.

Let $\pi :X\to B$ be a surjective holomorphic submersion of connected compact complex manifolds, with connected fibers. Write $n=\dim X$ and $q=\dim B\geq 1$. Let $A$ be a holomorphic line bundle on $B$ with a smooth Hermitian metric $h_ A$, choose a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\psi $ on $B$, and set

\[ (L,h)=(\pi ^*A,\pi ^*h_ A),\qquad \varphi =\pi ^*\psi . \]

For $y\in B$ write $X_ y=\pi ^{-1}(y)$. Choose a family of smooth positive probability measures $\nu _ y$ on the fibers $X_ y$ that varies smoothly with $y$; for instance, restrict a smooth positive vertical volume density to the fibers and divide by its fiber integrals. For $F\in C(X)$ set

\[ \mathcal A F(y)=\int _{X_ y}F\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ y. \]

The function $\mathcal A F$ is continuous: a proper submersion is locally a smooth fiber bundle, the densities vary smoothly in a local trivialization, and $F$ is uniformly continuous on compact sets. For a finite positive Borel measure $\mu $ on $B$, define its lift $\mathcal L\mu $ by

\[ \int _ X F\, \mathrm{d}\mathcal L\mu =\int _ B\mathcal A F\, \mathrm{d}\mu \qquad (F\in C(X)). \]

Since $\mathcal A$ is positive and $\mathcal A1=1$, the Riesz representation theorem makes $\mathcal L\mu $ a finite positive measure with the same total mass as $\mu $. Since $\mathcal AF$ is continuous, weak convergence of finite measures on $B$ implies weak convergence of their lifts.

Proposition 27.7.1. Pullback of sections induces an isomorphism

\[ H^0\bigl (B,A^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\psi )\bigr ) \simeq H^0\bigl (X,L^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\varphi )\bigr ). \]

Let $K\subseteq B$ be closed and non-pluripolar, let $w\in C(K)$, and let $\mu $ be a Bernstein–Markov probability measure for $(K,w)$ on $B$. Set $K_ X=\pi ^{-1}(K)$, $w_ X=w\circ \pi $ and $\mu _ X=\mathcal L\mu $. Then pullback is an isometry from the norm $\| \cdot \| _{m,w,\mu }$ on $B$ to the norm $\| \cdot \| _{m,w_ X,\mu _ X}$ on $X$, the measure $\mu _ X$ is Bernstein–Markov for $(K_ X,w_ X)$, and the partial kernel measures on $B$ and $X$, normalized with the same integer $r$, satisfy

\[ \beta _{X,m}^{(r)}=\mathcal L\bigl (\beta _{B,m}^{(r)}\bigr ). \]

Proof. Let $s$ be a holomorphic section of $\pi ^*A^ m$. Over an open set of $B$ on which $A^ m$ is trivial, the coefficient of $s$ is a holomorphic function on the inverse image, and it is constant on each fiber, since the fibers are compact and connected. It is therefore the pullback of a function on the base, which is holomorphic because it is the composition of the coefficient with a local holomorphic section of the submersion. These local functions transform by the transition functions of $A^ m$, so they define a section of $A^ m$ whose pullback is $s$. This proves the identification without multiplier ideals.

By lemma 1.16.6, the multiplier ideal $\mathcal I(m\varphi )$ on $X$ is generated by the pullbacks of the germs in $\mathcal I(m\psi )$. A pulled-back germ belongs to it exactly when the base germ belongs to $\mathcal I(m\psi )$: pullback gives one implication, and restriction to a local holomorphic section of $\pi $ gives the other. This identifies the selected spaces.

Pointwise norms of corresponding sections agree and are constant along the fibers. Since each $\nu _ y$ has mass one,

\[ \int _{K_ X}|\pi ^*s|_{h^ m}^2\mathrm{e}^{-mw_ X}\, \mathrm{d}\mu _ X =\int _ K|s|_{h_ A^ m}^2\mathrm{e}^{-mw}\, \mathrm{d}\mu . \]

The supremum norms on $K_ X$ and $K$ also agree, because $\pi $ is surjective. Thus the Bernstein–Markov inequality transfers from $(K,w,\mu )$ to $(K_ X,w_ X,\mu _ X)$, and the integral norms on $X$ are nondegenerate. Pulling back an orthonormal basis gives $B_{X,m}=B_{B,m}\circ \pi $ on $K_ X$, where each kernel is set to zero outside its weighted set. Integrating a continuous test function first along the fibers proves the identity of measures. $\square$

27.7.1 The quotient equilibrium measure

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On a Kähler base, the partial kernel measures converge to the Monge–Ampère measure of the partial envelope. Since lifting is continuous, the kernel measures on the total space converge to the lift of this measure, with the normalization given by the dimension of the base.

Keep the submersion and the weighted compact set $(K,w,\mu )$ above. Assume that $B$ is Kähler, put $\theta =c_1(A,h_ A)$, and take $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(B,\theta )$ with positive non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère mass. Write $B_{B,m}$ and $B_{X,m}$ for the partial Bergman kernels on $K$ and $K_ X$, put $\mu _ X=\mathcal L\mu $, and let

\[ E=E_{\theta ,K}[\psi ]_{\mathcal I}(w) \]

be the partial envelope of section 27.3. We call $\mathcal L(\mathrm{MA}_\theta (E))$ the quotient equilibrium measure of these data on $X$.

Theorem 27.7.2. Under these hypotheses,

\[ \frac{q!}{m^ q}B_{X,m}\, \mu _ X \rightharpoonup \mathcal L\bigl (\mathrm{MA}_\theta (E)\bigr ). \]

If $n>q$, then $(n!/m^ n)B_{X,m}\, \mu _ X\to 0$ in total variation.

Proof. Apply [Theorem 14.3.1, SGPT] on the compact Kähler manifold $B$, with trivial auxiliary twisting line bundle. Its hypotheses are exactly our assumptions: $\psi $ has positive mass, $K$ is closed and non-pluripolar, $w$ is continuous, and $\mu $ is a Bernstein–Markov probability measure. It gives

\[ \frac{q!}{m^ q}B_{B,m}\, \mu \rightharpoonup \mathrm{MA}_\theta (E). \]

By proposition 27.7.1, the corresponding measures on $X$ are the lifts of these measures, and lifting preserves weak convergence; this proves the first assertion. In particular the $q$-normalized total masses are bounded, and multiplying them by $(n!/q!)m^{q-n}\to 0$ proves the last assertion when $n>q$. $\square$

27.7.2 The Hopf equilibrium measure

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On the Hopf manifold, the quotient equilibrium measure is a Hermitian Monge–Ampère measure: the vertical part of the Hopf metric supplies the missing fiber direction. The constant relating the two measures combines the fiber probability with a coefficient of the top power.

Use the Hopf manifold $X_ n$, $n\geq 2$, its projection $\pi :X_ n\to \mathbb P^{n-1}$, the Fubini–Study form $\sigma $ on $\mathbb P^{n-1}$ with $\int \sigma ^{n-1}=1$, and the forms $\alpha =\pi ^*\sigma $, $\zeta $ and $\omega =\alpha +\zeta $ of chapter 22; recall that $\zeta ^2=0$ and that $\zeta $ has integral two on each fiber. Take $A=\mathcal O(1)$ with its Fubini–Study metric, so that $\theta =\sigma $, and keep the selecting potential $\psi $ and the weighted data $(K,w,\mu )$ above. On each elliptic fiber put $\nu _ y=\zeta |_{X_ y}/2$. The lift of a base measure $\tau $ is then

\[ \mathcal L\tau =\tfrac 12\pi ^*\tau \wedge \zeta , \]

in the fiber-integration notation of chapter 22. Since $E\in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^{n-1},\sigma )$, theorem 22.2.1 gives $\mathcal L(\mathrm{MA}_\sigma (E))=(2n)^{-1}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (\pi ^*E)$, and theorem 27.7.2 becomes

27.24
\begin{equation} \frac{(n-1)!}{m^{n-1}}B_{X_ n,m}\, \mu _ X \rightharpoonup \frac{1}{2n}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (\pi ^*E). \end{equation}

The factor $1/(2n)$ combines the fiber probability $1/2$ with the coefficient $n$ of $\alpha ^{n-1}\wedge \zeta $ in $\omega ^ n=(\alpha +\zeta )^ n$. The closed curvature form $\alpha $ alone gives nothing: by theorem 22.2.1, descended potentials have zero Monge–Ampère measure with respect to $\alpha $, and the nonzero limit comes from the vertical form $\zeta $. For $n=3$ this gives a nonzero limit, with the normalization $2/m^2$, on a manifold without the bounded mass property (theorem 8.0.1).

For a positive integer $k$, every $k\alpha $-plurisubharmonic function on $X_ n$ is pulled back from $\mathbb P^{n-1}$, by theorem 22.1.1. An $\omega $-plurisubharmonic function, by contrast, can have an isolated pole, and then it does not descend. Its selected sections still descend, so the pole is recorded by a vanishing condition on the base. The next two sections determine this condition and its effect on the kernels.

27.8 Isolated logarithmic poles on a fibration

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Return to a fibration with compact connected fibers, and let the selecting potential have isolated logarithmic poles on the total space. Every selected section descends to the base, and in large tensor powers it must vanish to a certain order at the images of the poles. This order is computed from integrability in all variables of the total space, so it differs from the order imposed by a pole of the same coefficient on the base by at most the fiber dimension. The jets in between span $O(m^{q-1})$ dimensions on a $q$-dimensional base. This is negligible after normalization by $m^ q$, so the kernel measures converge to the same quotient equilibrium measure as for the corresponding poles on the base.

The comparison rests on the following elementary fact: for nested section spaces, the total variation of the difference of their kernel measures is the normalized codimension.

Lemma 27.8.1. Let $B$ be a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $q\geq 1$, let $A\to B$ be a holomorphic line bundle with a smooth Hermitian metric, let $v\in C(B)$, and let $\nu $ be a smooth positive probability volume on $B$. For every integer $m\geq 1$ let $W_ m\subseteq V_ m\subseteq H^0(B,A^ m)$ be subspaces, both with the norm $\| \cdot \| _{m,v,\nu }$, and put $d_ m=\dim V_ m-\dim W_ m$. Write $B_{V_ m,v}$, $B_{W_ m,v}$ for their kernels and $\beta _{V_ m,v}^{(q)}$, $\beta _{W_ m,v}^{(q)}$ for their kernel measures, defined as in section 27.1 with the space indicated by the subscript. Then

\[ \begin{gathered} B_{V_ m,v}-B_{W_ m,v}\geq 0,\\ \| \beta _{V_ m,v}^{(q)}-\beta _{W_ m,v}^{(q)}\| _{\mathrm{TV}} =\frac{q!d_ m}{m^ q}. \end{gathered} \]

The determinant functionals of the two spaces, each normalized to vanish at the zero weight, satisfy

\[ |\mathcal F_{V_ m}^{(q)}(v)-\mathcal F_{W_ m}^{(q)}(v)| \leq \frac{q!d_ m}{m^ q}\| v\| _\infty . \]

These statements include the case of zero-dimensional spaces.

Proof. Extend an orthonormal basis of $W_ m$ to an orthonormal basis of $V_ m$ for the norm at the weight $v$. The difference of the kernels is the sum of the squared weighted evaluations of the $d_ m$ added basis vectors. It is nonnegative, and its integral against $\nu $ is $d_ m$, which gives the total variation formula. For the determinant functionals, apply the first-variation formula of proposition 27.5.1 to each of the two fixed spaces, and integrate the difference along $t\mapsto tv$, $0\leq t\leq 1$. By what we have just proved, at each $t$ the difference of the kernel measures is positive with total mass $q!d_ m/m^ q$, so its integral against $v$ is at most $q!d_ m\| v\| _\infty /m^ q$ in absolute value. Since both functionals vanish at zero, this proves the last assertion. $\square$

Return to a submersion $\pi :X\to B$ and a pulled-back line bundle $L=\pi ^*A$ as in section 27.7, and write $f=n-q$ for the fiber dimension. Fix distinct points $p_1,\ldots ,p_ N$ of $X$ and positive rational numbers $a_1,\ldots ,a_ N$. Suppose that $\varphi \in \mathrm{QPSH}(X)$ is locally bounded away from these points and that, in local coordinates $x$ centered at $p_ j$,

27.25
\begin{equation} \varphi (x)=a_ j\log |x|^2+O(1). \end{equation}

Write

\[ Z=\{ \pi (p_1),\ldots ,\pi (p_ N)\} ,\qquad a_ b=\max _{\pi (p_ j)=b}a_ j\quad (b\in Z), \]

and, for an integer $m\geq 1$,

\[ r_{m,b}=\max \{ \lfloor ma_ b\rfloor -n+1,0\} ,\qquad s_{m,b}=\max \{ \lfloor ma_ b\rfloor -q+1,0\} . \]

Let $\mathfrak m_ b$ denote the ideal sheaf of a point $b\in B$; its zeroth power is $\mathcal O_ B$.

Proposition 27.8.2. Pullback identifies the selected space $H^0(X,L^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\varphi ))$ with

\[ V_ m=H^0\left(B,A^ m\otimes \bigcap _{b\in Z}\mathfrak m_ b^{r_{m,b}}\right). \]

The space

\[ W_ m=H^0\left(B,A^ m\otimes \bigcap _{b\in Z}\mathfrak m_ b^{s_{m,b}}\right) \]

satisfies $W_ m\subseteq V_ m$ and

\[ 0\leq \dim V_ m-\dim W_ m \leq \sum _{b\in Z}\sum _{j=r_{m,b}}^{s_{m,b}-1} \binom {j+q-1}{q-1} =O(m^{q-1}), \]

where an empty inner sum is zero. The implied constant depends only on the points and their coefficients, not on $m$.

Proof. Lemma 1.16.1, applied with a point as center, gives

\[ \mathcal I(m\varphi )_{p_ j}=\mathfrak m_{p_ j}^{\max \{ \lfloor ma_ j\rfloor -n+1,0\} }; \]

away from the points $p_ j$ the ideal is the full structure sheaf.

Every section of $L^ m$ descends to $B$, by the argument in proposition 27.7.1. Near $p_ j$ choose submersion coordinates $(z,\tau )$ with $\pi (z,\tau )=z$. A pulled-back section has a local coefficient $g(z)$ that does not depend on $\tau $, and its vanishing order at $(0,0)$ equals the vanishing order of $g$ at $z=0$. The conditions imposed by the points of one fiber are therefore vanishing conditions at the same base point, and the strongest of them is vanishing to order $r_{m,b}$. This proves the identification.

For the dimension bound, map each section in $V_ m$ to its jets modulo $\mathfrak m_ b^{s_{m,b}}$ at the finitely many points $b\in Z$. The kernel of this map is $W_ m$, so local trivializations of $A^ m$ give an injection

\[ V_ m/W_ m\hookrightarrow \bigoplus _{b\in Z}\mathfrak m_ b^{r_{m,b}}/ \mathfrak m_ b^{s_{m,b}}. \]

Surjectivity of the jet map is not needed. In $q$ variables there are $\binom {j+q-1}{q-1}$ monomials of degree $j$, which gives the dimensions of these quotients. Finally $0\leq s_{m,b}-r_{m,b}\leq f$ and $s_{m,b}=O(m)$, which proves the bound, also when $q=1$ or $f=0$. $\square$

Equip $A$ with a smooth metric $h_ A$ and put $\theta =c_1(A,h_ A)$. Choose a smooth positive probability volume $\nu _ B$ on $B$, smooth fiber probabilities as in section 27.7, and the smooth positive volume $\nu _ X=\mathcal L\nu _ B$ on $X$. Use the pullback metric on $L$, and for $v\in C(B)$ use the weight $v\circ \pi $ in the norm on $X$. We write $\beta _{X,m,v\circ \pi ,\varphi }^{(q)}$ and $\mathcal F_{X,m}^{(q)}$ for the kernel measure and the determinant functional on $X$ defined by these data and the selecting potential $\varphi $.

Theorem 27.8.3. Assume that $B$ is Kähler and that there is $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(B,\theta )$ with positive non-pluripolar mass, locally bounded off $Z$, such that

\[ \psi (z)=a_ b\log |z-b|^2+O(1)\quad \hbox{near each }b\in Z \]

in local coordinates $z$ on $B$. Let $E(v)=E_{\theta ,B}[\psi ]_{\mathcal I}(v)$. Then, for the selecting potential $\varphi $ of (27.25),

\[ \beta _{X,m,v\circ \pi ,\varphi }^{(q)} \rightharpoonup \mathcal L\bigl (\mathrm{MA}_\theta (E(v))\bigr ). \]

More precisely, the total variation distance between $\beta _{X,m,v\circ \pi ,\varphi }^{(q)}$ and the lift of the kernel measure on $B$ selected by $\psi $ is $O(m^{-1})$. The determinant functionals satisfy

\[ \mathcal F_{X,m}^{(q)}(v\circ \pi )\longrightarrow \int _0^1\! \left(\int _ B v\, \mathrm{d}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (E(tv))\right)\mathrm{d}t. \]

The normalized dimensions $q!N_ m/m^ q$ converge to the total mass of the limit measure, which is finite and positive. If $n>q$, the kernel measures with the normalization $n!/m^ n$ tend to zero in total variation.

Proof. By lemma 1.16.1 in dimension $q$, the space of sections of $A^ m$ selected by $\psi $ on $B$ is the space $W_ m$ of proposition 27.8.2.
