Corresponding sections in $V_ m$ and on $X$ have equal norms, since their pointwise norms and the continuous weight are pulled back from $B$ and every fiber probability has mass one. As in proposition 27.7.1, pulling back an orthonormal basis shows that the kernel measure on $X$ is the lift of the kernel measure of $V_ m$ on $B$.

By lemma 27.8.1, proposition 27.8.2, the $q$-normalized kernel measures of $V_ m$ and $W_ m$ on $B$ differ by a positive measure of total mass $O(m^{-1})$, and so do their lifts. Apply theorem 27.6.3 on $B$, with the identity as Kähler modification; its hypotheses are among ours. It gives the convergence of the kernel measures of $W_ m$, and since the difference tends to zero, the asserted limit on $X$ follows.

By the same lemma, the determinant functionals of $V_ m$ and $W_ m$ differ by at most $O(m^{-1})\| v\| _\infty $. The isometric identification of the norms and corollary 27.6.4 on $B$ give the limit of the functionals. Testing the constant function one proves the assertion about the dimensions. Since the $q$-normalized masses are bounded, multiplying them by $(n!/q!)m^{q-n}$ proves the last assertion. $\square$

If $\theta >0$, lemma 1.16.2, applied on $B$ with the Hermitian form $\theta $, constructs the required $\psi $ whenever the coefficients $a_ b$ are sufficiently small. Off the poles, $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi $ then dominates a positive multiple of $\theta $, so the non-pluripolar mass of $\psi $ is positive; it is finite because $\theta $ is closed and $B$ is Kähler. The same lemma, applied on $X$ with a Hermitian form, constructs $\varphi $. If the fibers have positive dimension, such a function $\varphi $ does not descend to $B$, since it is finite at the other points of a fiber containing a pole.

The section formula of proposition 27.8.2 also shows that the kernels cannot recover the singularities of the selecting potential. Moving an isolated pole within its fiber does not change any selected space, and neither does adding further poles in that fiber whose coefficients do not exceed the largest one. For the fixed pullback metric $h=\pi ^*h_ A$, continuous weight $v\circ \pi $ and lifted volume $\nu _ X$, all kernels and determinant functionals are therefore the same in every degree. The multiplier ideals on $X$ are nevertheless different: for large weights, the ideal of one potential is a proper ideal at a point where the ideal of the other is the full local ring. Under the hypotheses of theorem 27.8.3, this loss of information occurs together with a positive limit at the growth rate $m^ q$. In the closed theory, by contrast, the limit envelope has the multiplier-ideal type of the selecting potential (theorem 27.3.1); this property is lost when the selecting potential is plurisubharmonic only with respect to a Hermitian form.

27.9 A point pole on the Hopf manifold

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On the Hopf manifold the comparison of section 27.8 can be made completely explicit. A single point pole selects the homogeneous polynomials whose degree in affine coordinates centered at the image point is bounded below, and summing the squared norms of the normalized monomials expresses the kernel as a binomial tail. The exponential rate of this tail determines the limit of the Fubini–Study potentials, and its concentration determines the limit of the measures. The limit potential has a pole along the entire fiber through the original pole, which makes the loss of the point singularity explicit.

We keep the notation of section 27.7.2, with $q=n-1$, so that $\pi :X_ n\to \mathbb P^ q$. Use $L=L_1=\pi ^*\mathcal O(1)$ with its pulled-back Fubini–Study metric and the probability volume $\nu _ X=\omega ^ n/(2n)$, which is the lift of $\sigma ^ q$ for the fiber probabilities $\zeta |_{X_ y}/2$. Let $p\in X_ n$ lie over $b=[1:0:\cdots :0]$. Suppose that $\varphi $ has a single logarithmic pole at $p$, with rational coefficient $0<a<1$, as in (27.25), and is locally bounded elsewhere. The continuous weight is zero. In homogeneous coordinates $[Z_0:\cdots :Z_ q]$ on $\mathbb P^ q$, set

\[ \begin{gathered} t([Z_0:\cdots :Z_ q])= \frac{|Z_1|^2+\cdots +|Z_ q|^2}{|Z_0|^2+\cdots +|Z_ q|^2},\\ r_ m=\max \{ \lfloor ma\rfloor -q,0\} , \qquad D_ m=\binom {m+q}{q}. \end{gathered} \]

Thus $t=0$ exactly at $b$. Define the function $e_ a$ on $[0,1]$ by

27.26
\begin{equation} e_ a(t)= \begin{cases} a\log (t/a)+(1-a)\log ((1-t)/(1-a)),& 0<t<a,\\ 0,& a\leq t\leq 1,\\ -\infty ,& t=0, \end{cases} \end{equation}

and put $E_ a=e_ a\circ t$ on $\mathbb P^ q$. For $q=1$ this is the function $\Gamma _ a$ of (18.2); here it arises from a point pole on the total space of a fibration. We write $B_{X_ n,m}=B_{m,0,\varphi ,\nu _ X}$ for the partial Bergman kernel.

Proposition 27.9.1. The kernel and the dimension of the selected spaces are

\[ B_{X_ n,m}=D_ m\left( \sum _{j=r_ m}^{m}\binom mj t^ j(1-t)^{m-j}\right)\circ \pi , \qquad N_ m=D_ m-\binom {r_ m+q-1}{q}, \]

where the last binomial coefficient is zero when $r_ m=0$. In particular, all selected spaces are nonzero, and

\[ \lim _{m\to \infty }\frac{q!N_ m}{m^ q}=1-a^ q>0. \]

Proof. Since $n=q+1$, proposition 27.8.2 identifies the selected space with the space of homogeneous polynomials of degree $m$ on $\mathbb C^{q+1}$ that vanish to order at least $r_ m$ at $b$. For $\ell =(\ell _0,\ldots ,\ell _ q)\in \mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{q+1}$ write $|\ell |=\sum _ i\ell _ i$ and $Z^\ell =\prod _ iZ_ i^{\ell _ i}$. In the affine chart $Z_0=1$, the vanishing order of $Z^\ell $ at $b$ is $\ell _1+\cdots +\ell _ q$, so the selected space is spanned by the monomials $Z^\ell $ with $|\ell |=m$ and $\ell _1+\cdots +\ell _ q\geq r_ m$.

Distinct monomials are orthogonal, by invariance under the coordinate circle actions. Their squared norms with respect to $\sigma ^ q$ are

27.30
\begin{equation} \int _{\mathbb P^ q}\frac{|Z^\ell |^2}{|Z|^{2m}}\, \sigma ^ q =\frac{q!\, \ell _0!\cdots \ell _ q!}{(m+q)!}, \qquad |\ell |=m. \end{equation}

To verify this, integrate $|z^\ell |^2\mathrm{e}^{-|z|^2}$ over $\mathbb C^{q+1}$ in two ways. As a product of one-variable integrals it equals $\pi ^{q+1}\prod _ i\ell _ i!$, while in polar coordinates it is the average of $|z^\ell |^2$ over the unit sphere times a radial integral that contributes the factor $(m+q)!$. Dividing by the same computation for $\ell =0$ gives the right-hand side of (27.30) as the normalized sphere average, and the pushforward of the normalized sphere measure to $\mathbb P^ q$ is $\sigma ^ q$, since both are the probability measures invariant under the unitary group. This proves (27.30).

The reciprocal of the squared norm is $D_ mm!/(\ell _0!\cdots \ell _ q!)$. Summing first over the monomials with $\ell _1+\cdots +\ell _ q=j$, the multinomial formula gives the contribution

\[ D_ m\binom mj t^ j(1-t)^{m-j} \]

to the kernel on $\mathbb P^ q$. Since $\nu _ X$ is the lift of $\sigma ^ q$, the norms and kernels lift exactly to $X_ n$, which proves the kernel formula. There are $\binom {j+q-1}{q-1}$ monomials of affine degree $j$, and summing over the excluded degrees $0\leq j<r_ m$ gives $\binom {r_ m+q-1}{q}$. Since $0\leq r_ m\leq m$ and $r_ m/m\to a$, this proves the dimension formula, the nonvanishing and the limit. $\square$

Theorem 27.9.2. Let $U_ m=m^{-1}\log B_{X_ n,m}$ be the partial Fubini–Study potential for the zero weight. Then

\[ U_ m\longrightarrow E_ a\circ \pi \]

in $L^1(X_ n)$, pointwise as extended-real functions, and locally uniformly off the fiber $\pi ^{-1}(b)$. The normalized kernel measures converge in total variation:

\[ \frac{q!}{m^ q}B_{X_ n,m}\, \nu _ X \longrightarrow \mathbf1_{\{ t\circ \pi >a\} }\nu _ X. \]

Proof. Write $P_ m(t)$ for the binomial sum in proposition 27.9.1. For fixed $0<t<1$, $P_ m(t)$ is the probability that a binomial random variable with parameters $(m,t)$ is at least $r_ m$. Its variance is $mt(1-t)$, so Chebyshev's inequality and $r_ m/m\to a$ give

\[ P_ m(t)\longrightarrow \begin{cases} 0,& t<a,\\ 1,& t>a.\end{cases} \]

The pushforward of $\sigma ^ q$ under $t$ is $q t^{q-1}\, \mathrm{d}t$. To see this, note that in the affine chart the Fubini–Study measure has radial density $2q r^{2q-1}(1+r^2)^{-q-1}\, \mathrm{d}r$, and $t=r^2/(1+r^2)$. In particular, the level set $\{ t=a\} $ has measure zero. Since $0\leq P_ m\leq 1$ and $q!D_ m/m^ q\to 1$, dominated convergence gives convergence of the densities in $L^1(\sigma ^ q)$, hence in $L^1(\nu _ X)$ after lifting; this is the asserted convergence in total variation. The limit has mass $\int _ a^1 qt^{q-1}\, \mathrm{d}t=1-a^ q$.

To identify the limit of the potentials, put

\[ H(s)=-s\log s-(1-s)\log (1-s),\qquad 0\leq s\leq 1, \]

with $0\log 0=0$. Then

\[ \frac{\mathrm{e}^{mH(j/m)}}{m+1}\leq \binom mj \leq \mathrm{e}^{mH(j/m)}\quad (0\leq j\leq m). \]

Indeed, $\binom mj\mathrm{e}^{-mH(j/m)}$ is the $j$th term of the binomial distribution with parameters $(m,j/m)$. It is at most one, and it is at least $1/(m+1)$ because, as the ratio of neighboring terms shows, it is a largest of the $m+1$ terms, which sum to one. For $j=0$ and $j=m$ it equals one.

The logarithm of a sum of at most $m+1$ nonnegative terms differs from the largest of their logarithms by at most $\log (m+1)$. Hence $m^{-1}\log P_ m(t)$ differs by at most $\log (m+1)/m$ from

\[ \max _{r_ m\leq j\leq m} \left\{ H(j/m)+(j/m)\log t+(1-j/m)\log (1-t)\right\} . \]

On compact subintervals of $(0,1)$, uniform continuity and $r_ m/m\to a$ show that these maxima converge uniformly to the maximum of the same expression over $a\leq s\leq 1$. Maximizing in $s$, we find that this maximum is zero for $t\geq a$, attained at $s=t$, and equals the first expression in (27.26) for $t<a$, attained at $s=a$. Thus the limit is $e_ a(t)$, also at $t=a$.

The convergence is also uniform near $t=1$. Choose $c$ with $a<c<1$. The same variance estimate gives $P_ m(t)\geq 1-C/m$ uniformly for $c\leq t\leq 1$ and large $m$, so $m^{-1}\log P_ m\to 0$ uniformly there. Together with the argument on compact subintervals and $m^{-1}\log D_ m\to 0$, this proves locally uniform convergence off $\{ t=0\} $. At $t=0$ we have $r_ m>0$ for large $m$, so $P_ m(0)=0$ and the pointwise limit is $-\infty $.

Finally, $t^ m\leq P_ m(t)\leq 1$, because the term $j=m$ occurs in every binomial sum. Since $m^{-1}\log D_ m$ is nonnegative and bounded, it follows that $\log (t\circ \pi )\leq U_ m\leq C$ off the fiber over $b$. In local submersion coordinates near this fiber, $\log (t\circ \pi )=\log |z|^2+O(1)$, which is integrable against a smooth volume. The pointwise convergence and these bounds give convergence in $L^1$, by dominated convergence. $\square$

The function $E_ a$ is the partial envelope of a point pole on the base. Put $Z'=(Z_1,\ldots ,Z_ q)$ and $\psi _ a=a\log t$ on $\mathbb P^ q$. For $0<a<1$ this function is $\sigma $-plurisubharmonic. Indeed, away from $b$ we have $\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _ a=(1-a)\sigma +a\sigma '$, where $\sigma '$ is the semipositive pullback of the Fubini–Study form under the projection $[Z_0:Z']\mapsto [Z']$ (and $\sigma '=0$ if $q=1$); near $b$, the function $\psi _ a$ is $a\log |z|^2$ plus a smooth function, so the positivity extends across $b$.

Proposition 27.9.3. The function $E_ a$ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^ q,\sigma )$, and

\[ E_ a=E_{\sigma ,\mathbb P^ q}[\psi _ a]_{\mathcal I}(0), \qquad \mathrm{MA}_\sigma (E_ a)=\mathbf1_{\{ t>a\} }\sigma ^ q. \]

Moreover, $E_ a\circ \pi $ is the largest $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X_ n,\alpha )$ with $u\leq 0$ and $u\preceq _{\mathcal I}\varphi $. Consequently

\[ \mathbf1_{\{ t\circ \pi >a\} }\nu _ X =\frac1{2n}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (E_ a\circ \pi ). \]

Proof. In the chart $Z_0=1$ put $z=Z'/Z_0$ and $R^2=a/(1-a)$. For $|z|<R$, the local potential of $\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}E_ a$ is

\[ \log (1+|z|^2)+E_ a =a\log |z|^2-a\log a-(1-a)\log (1-a). \]

Its complex Hessian is semipositive and, away from $z=0$, of rank $q-1$. For $|z|>R$ we have $E_ a=0$, so the form is $\sigma $. The two expressions for $E_ a$ have equal values and equal first derivatives on the sphere $|z|=R$, so they glue to a $C^{1,1}$ function near this sphere, whose distributional Hessian has no part carried by the sphere. Near $b$, $E_ a$ has a logarithmic pole of coefficient $a$. This proves that $E_ a$ is $\sigma $-plurisubharmonic and that $E_ a\sim \psi _ a$.

For the envelope identity, let $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^ q,\sigma )$ satisfy $w\leq 0$ and $w\preceq _{\mathcal I}\psi _ a$. Since $\psi _ a$ has analytic singularities, lemma 1.17.10 gives $w\leq \psi _ a+C$. Fix a complex line through $0$ in the affine chart. If the restriction of $w+\log (1+|z|^2)$ to this line is identically $-\infty $, there is nothing to prove on the line; otherwise the restriction is subharmonic. On the punctured disk of radius $R$ in the line, subtract $a\log |z|^2$. The difference is subharmonic and, since $w\leq \psi _ a+C$, bounded above near $0$, so it extends subharmonically across $0$. On the boundary circle it is at most $\log (1+R^2)-a\log R^2=-a\log a-(1-a)\log (1-a)$, because $w\leq 0$. By the maximum principle and the formula above, $w\leq E_ a$ on the disk. Every point with $|z|<R$ lies on such a line, and for $|z|\geq R$ we have $w\leq 0=E_ a$. Since $E_ a$ is itself a competitor, it is the envelope. For the continuous obstacle zero, the quasi-everywhere and the pointwise obstacle conditions agree, as recalled in section 1.19.

Off $b$ the local potentials of $\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}E_ a$ are $C^{1,1}$. By proposition 1.7.4, their Bedford–Taylor top products are given by the determinants of their complex Hessians almost everywhere. By the rank computation, this determinant vanishes where $t<a$, while for $t>a$ the measure is $\sigma ^ q$. The sphere $\{ t=a\} $ has volume zero, and a $C^{1,1}$ local potential puts no mass on it. Finally, the non-pluripolar measure does not charge the point $b$. This proves the measure formula.

For the assertion on $X_ n$, every $\alpha $-plurisubharmonic function has the form $u=w\circ \pi $ with $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^ q,\sigma )$, by proposition 1.22.3. Since $\varphi $ has analytic singularities, $u\preceq _{\mathcal I}\varphi $ implies $u\preceq \varphi $ by lemma 1.17.10. Restricting this inequality to $\tau =0$ in product coordinates $(z,\tau )$ at $p$ gives $w(z)\leq a\log |z|^2+C$, and hence $w\preceq \psi _ a$ on $\mathbb P^ q$. Conversely, if $w\preceq \psi _ a$, then

\[ w(z)\leq a\log |z|^2+C \leq a\log (|z|^2+|\tau |^2)+C \]

near $p$. Away from $p$, the function $\varphi $ is locally bounded and $w$ is bounded above, so by compactness the inequality $w\circ \pi \leq \varphi +C$ holds globally. Thus $w\circ \pi \preceq \varphi $, which implies $w\circ \pi \preceq _{\mathcal I}\varphi $. Since lemma 1.17.10 also applies on the base, the map $u=w\circ \pi \mapsto w$ identifies the competitors on $X_ n$ with the competitors for $E_ a$ considered above. Their largest members therefore correspond under pullback. Finally, theorem 22.2.1 and the measure formula on the base give the last identity. $\square$

For sufficiently small rational $a$, lemma 1.16.2 gives a function $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X_ n,\omega )$ whose only pole is at $p$. In particular, theorem 27.9.2 applies on the Hopf threefold $X_3$, which does not have the bounded mass property, and gives a nonzero limit measure with the normalization $2/m^2$. The limit potential $E_ a\circ \pi $ has a pole along the whole elliptic fiber over $b$. At the other points of this fiber $\varphi $ is locally bounded, whereas $E_ a\circ \pi $ has nontrivial multiplier ideals for large weights. Thus, although the sections grow at a positive rate, the limit does not recover the multiplier-ideal type of a selecting potential that is plurisubharmonic only with respect to a Hermitian form.

The limit measure also differs from the Hermitian Monge–Ampère measure of $\varphi $ itself. In the construction of lemma 1.16.2, choose $\varphi $ to vanish outside a small neighborhood of $p$. A neighborhood of another point of the fiber over $b$ is then an open set outside this neighborhood on which $t\circ \pi <a$. There $\varphi =0$, so $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (\varphi )=\omega ^ n$ by locality, whereas the limit of the kernel measures vanishes. The identity in proposition 27.9.3 concerns the envelope $E_ a\circ \pi $, not the selecting potential $\varphi $.

27.10 Singularities along a section over a divisor

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now replace the isolated poles by a pole along the image of a holomorphic section of the fibration over a divisor of the base. The selected sections must then vanish to a certain order along that divisor. As for point poles, the normal variables in the fiber shift the integrability threshold only by a bounded amount, so this order differs from the order imposed by a divisorial pole on the base by a bounded number. Restriction sequences along the divisor show that each unit of vanishing order costs $O(m^{q-1})$ sections, and kernel convergence again reduces to the equilibrium theorem on the base.

For the restriction sequences we need a bound for the dimensions of section spaces that is uniform over a range of twists growing linearly with $m$.

Lemma 27.10.1. Let $M$ be a compact complex manifold of dimension $d\geq 0$, and let $E,F$ be holomorphic line bundles on $M$. For each $C_0>0$ there is a constant $C_1$ such that

\[ h^0(M,E^ m\otimes F^ j)\leq C_1m^ d \qquad (m\geq 1,\ j\in \mathbb Z,\ |j|\leq C_0m). \]

Proof. For $d=0$, the manifold $M$ is a finite set and the dimension is at most its number of points. Let $d\geq 1$. Fix smooth Hermitian metrics on $E$ and $F$, a smooth positive volume form $\mathrm{d}V$, and finitely many coordinate charts with holomorphic frames, together with smaller subcharts that still cover $M$. For $|j|\leq C_0m$, the local weight of the metric on $E^ m\otimes F^ j$ has second derivatives bounded by $Cm$. At a point $p$ of a smaller subchart, multiply the frame by the exponential of a holomorphic affine function to remove the constant and real-linear terms of this weight at $p$. The new weight vanishes at $p$ and is bounded by $Cm|z-p|^2$ on a fixed neighborhood. A ball of radius $\varepsilon m^{-1/2}$ about $p$ lies in the chart, with $\varepsilon >0$ independent of $p$, $m$ and $j$, and on this ball the weight is bounded. The submean inequality for the holomorphic coefficient of a section $s$ on this ball gives

\[ |s(p)|^2\leq Cm^ d\int _ M|s|^2\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

where all constants depend only on the fixed charts, metrics, volume and $C_0$. Evaluation at $p$ has a one-dimensional target, so for an $L^2$-orthonormal basis $s_1,\ldots ,s_ N$ of $H^0(M,E^ m\otimes F^ j)$ its squared norm is $\sum _{\ell =1}^ N|s_\ell (p)|^2\leq Cm^ d$. Integrating over $M$ gives $N\leq C\operatorname {Vol}(M)m^ d$. $\square$

Let $\pi :X\to B$ be a surjective holomorphic submersion of connected compact complex manifolds, with connected fibers of dimension $r\geq 1$, and suppose that $B$ is Kähler of dimension $q\geq 1$. Fix a holomorphic line bundle $A$ on $B$ with a smooth Hermitian metric $h_ A$ whose curvature $\theta =c_1(A,h_ A)$ is positive. Let $Z\subset B$ be a nonempty smooth divisor, and suppose that $\pi $ has a holomorphic section $\sigma :Z\to X$ over $Z$; put $Y=\sigma (Z)$. Fix $c>0$ and a smooth metric $h_ Z$ on $\mathcal O_ B(Z)$, with curvature $\Theta _ Z$, such that $\alpha :=\theta -c\Theta _ Z>0$. Let $s_ Z$ be the canonical section of $\mathcal O_ B(Z)$, and set $\psi =c\log |s_ Z|_{h_ Z}^2$.

Fix a Hermitian form $\omega $ on $X$, chosen independently of $h_ A$. Suppose that $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ is locally bounded on $X\setminus Y$ and that, in submersion coordinates $(x,z',w_1,\ldots ,w_ r)$ near each point of $Y$,

27.33
\begin{equation} \begin{gathered} \varphi =c\log \left(|x|^2+\sum _{j=1}^ r|w_ j|^2\right)+O(1),\\ Z=\{ x=0\} ,\qquad Y=\{ x=w_1=\cdots =w_ r=0\} , \end{gathered} \end{equation}

with a locally bounded remainder. Here $x$ and $z'$ are coordinates on the base, and the vector $z'$ of the $q-1$ tangential coordinates is absent when $q=1$.

Theorem 27.10.2. The function $\psi $ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic, with positive non-pluripolar mass

\[ \int _ B\mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi )=\int _ B\alpha ^ q>0. \]

For every integer $m\geq 1$ put $\ell _ m=\lfloor mc\rfloor $ and $k_ m=\max \{ \ell _ m-r,0\} $. Under holomorphic descent, the spaces selected by $\varphi $ on $X$ and by $\psi $ on $B$ are

\[ \begin{split} V_ m& :=H^0\bigl (X,\pi ^*A^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\varphi )\bigr ) =H^0\bigl (B,A^ m(-k_ m Z)\bigr ),\\ W_ m& :=H^0\bigl (B,A^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\psi )\bigr ) =H^0\bigl (B,A^ m(-\ell _ m Z)\bigr )\subseteq V_ m. \end{split} \]

There is a constant $C$, depending only on the fixed data, such that

\[ 0\leq \dim V_ m-\dim W_ m\leq Cm^{q-1}. \]

For $q=1$ the difference is at most $r\, \# Z$ for every $m$.

Proof. By the Poincaré–Lelong formula, $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =\alpha +c[Z]\geq 0$. The divisorial measure identity (15.31), applied with the residual form $\alpha >0$ and the bounded part zero, gives $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi )=\alpha ^ q$, which proves the mass formula.

Every section of $\pi ^*A^ m$ descends by proposition 27.7.1. In the $r+1$ normal variables $(x,w)$, lemma 1.16.1 gives

\[ \mathcal I(m\varphi )=(x,w_1,\ldots ,w_ r)^{k_ m}. \]

The local coefficient $g(x,z')$ of a pulled-back section does not depend on $w$. Expanding $g$ in powers of $x$, we see that $g$ lies in this ideal exactly when the coefficients of $x^ j$ vanish for $j<k_ m$, that is, when $x^{k_ m}$ divides $g$. This is an identity of germs in all tangential variables, so it holds at every point of $Z$. Outside $Y$ the function $\varphi $ is locally bounded. On the base, the same lemma in the single normal variable $x$ gives $\mathcal I(m\psi )=\mathcal O_ B(-\ell _ mZ)$. These facts prove the two identities and the inclusion.

For $k_ m\leq j<\ell _ m$, the restriction sequence along $Z$,

\[ \begin{split} 0& \longrightarrow A^ m(-(j+1)Z)\longrightarrow A^ m(-jZ)\\ & \longrightarrow \bigl (A^ m(-jZ)\bigr )|_ Z\longrightarrow 0, \end{split} \]

shows that passing from vanishing order $j$ to order $j+1$ along $Z$ loses at most $h^0(Z,(A^ m(-jZ))|_ Z)$ dimensions. Apply lemma 27.10.1 on $Z$ with $E=A|_ Z$ and $F=\mathcal O_ B(-Z)|_ Z$; since $0\leq j\leq cm$, each loss is at most $Cm^{q-1}$, uniformly in $j$. There are at most $r$ steps, which proves the estimate. When $q=1$, the divisor $Z$ is a finite set and each restriction space has dimension $\# Z$, which gives the last assertion directly. $\square$

We call $\psi $ the reference potential, $W_ m$ the reference spaces, and $\dim V_ m-\dim W_ m$ the defect; the same terms are used for the analogous comparisons below. The following example shows that the order $m^{q-1}$ in theorem 27.10.2 is sharp.

Example 27.10.3. For all integers $q,r,k\geq 1$ and every real $0<c<k$, the hypotheses above are satisfied on $X=\mathbb P^ q\times \mathbb P^ r$ with $B=\mathbb P^ q$ and $A=\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^ q}(k)$, for suitable choices made in the proof. For $q\geq 2$ the defect is

\[ \dim V_ m-\dim W_ m =\frac{r(k-c)^{q-1}}{(q-1)!}m^{q-1}+O(m^{q-2}), \]

and for $q=1$ it equals $\min \{ r,\lfloor mc\rfloor \} $ for every $m$.

Proof. Use homogeneous coordinates $[Z_0:\cdots :Z_ q]$ on the base and $[W_0:\cdots :W_ r]$ on the fiber. Let $Z=\{ Z_0=0\} $ and $Y=Z\times \{ [1:0:\cdots :0]\} $, and let $\omega _ B,\omega _ F$ be the Fubini–Study forms normalized to have top integrals one. Define

\[ R_ B=\frac{|Z_0|^2}{\sum _{i=0}^ q|Z_ i|^2},\qquad R_ F=\frac{\sum _{j=1}^ r|W_ j|^2}{\sum _{j=0}^ r|W_ j|^2},\qquad \varphi =c\log (R_ B+R_ F),\quad \psi =c\log R_ B. \]

The function $\varphi $ is smooth off $Y$ and satisfies (27.33). Over a common denominator, the numerator of $R_ B+R_ F$ is

\[ \sum _{j=0}^ r|Z_0W_ j|^2+\sum _{i=0}^ q\sum _{j=1}^ r|Z_ iW_ j|^2, \]

whose logarithm is plurisubharmonic in each affine chart. Hence $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq -c(\omega _ B+\omega _ F)$. Choose an integer $b\geq c$ and set $\omega =k\omega _ B+b\omega _ F$. This is a Hermitian form, and $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq (k-c)\omega _ B+(b-c)\omega _ F\geq 0$. On the base, $\theta =k\omega _ B$, $\Theta _ Z=\omega _ B$ and $\alpha =(k-c)\omega _ B>0$.

Put $\ell _ m=\lfloor mc\rfloor $. Divisibility of homogeneous polynomials by powers of $Z_0$ gives the exact dimensions

\[ \dim W_ m=\binom {km-\ell _ m+q}{q},\qquad \dim V_ m=\binom {km-\max \{ \ell _ m-r,0\} +q}{q}. \]

For large $m$, the two residual degrees differ by $r$ and equal $(k-c)m+O(1)$. Subtracting the two binomial coefficients, which are polynomials of degree $q$ in the residual degrees, gives the asserted leading coefficient and remainder. For $q=1$ the difference is $\ell _ m-\max \{ \ell _ m-r,0\} =\min \{ r,\ell _ m\} $ for every $m$. $\square$

The bounded shift of divisorial order thus has negligible normalized codimension. To compare the kernels under the hypotheses of theorem 27.10.2, fix a smooth positive probability volume $\nu _ B$ on $B$, smooth fiber probabilities, and the lifting map $\mathcal L$ of section 27.7, and put $\nu _ X=\mathcal L\nu _ B$. Use the pullback metric on $\pi ^*A$ and, for a real function $v\in C(B)$, the weight $v\circ \pi $ in the norm on $X$. Write $\beta _{X,m,v\circ \pi ,\varphi }^{(q)}$ and $\mathcal F_{X,m}^{(q)}$ for the kernel measure and the determinant functional on $X$, as in section 27.8, and

\[ E(v)=E_{\theta ,B}[\psi ]_{\mathcal I}(v). \]

Corollary 27.10.4. The total variation distance between the kernel measure $\beta _{X,m,v\circ \pi ,\varphi }^{(q)}$ selected by $\varphi $ on $X$ and the lift of the kernel measure selected by $\psi $ on $B$ is $O(m^{-1})$. In particular,

\[ \beta _{X,m,v\circ \pi ,\varphi }^{(q)} \rightharpoonup \mathcal L\bigl (\mathrm{MA}_\theta (E(v))\bigr ). \]

The determinant functionals, normalized to vanish at the zero weight, satisfy

\[ \mathcal F_{X,m}^{(q)}(v\circ \pi )\longrightarrow \int _0^1\left(\int _ Bv\, \mathrm{d}\mathrm{MA}_\theta (E(tv))\right)\mathrm{d}t. \]

With the normalization $(q+r)!/m^{q+r}$ given by the dimension of $X$, the kernel measures tend to zero in total variation.

Proof. As in proposition 27.7.1, fiber integration identifies the norm on $X$ with the norm on $V_ m\subseteq H^0(B,A^ m)$, so the kernel measure on $X$ is the lift of the kernel measure of $V_ m$ on $B$. By lemma 27.8.1, theorem 27.10.2, the latter differs from the kernel measure of $W_ m$ by a positive measure of total mass $q!(\dim V_ m-\dim W_ m)/m^ q=O(m^{-1})$, and so do the lifts. Since $B$ is Kähler and $\psi $ has positive non-pluripolar mass, the equilibrium theorem used in the proof of theorem 27.7.2 applies to $W_ m$ and gives the weak limit. By lemma 27.8.1, the determinant functionals of $V_ m$ and $W_ m$ differ by at most $O(m^{-1})\| v\| _\infty $, and corollary 27.6.4, applied on $B$ with the identity modification, gives the limit of the functionals; its dominated convergence bound is $\| v\| _\infty $ times the bounded sequence of $q$-normalized dimensions. Finally, the $q$-normalized masses are bounded, so multiplying them by $(q+r)!m^{-r}/q!$ proves the last assertion. $\square$

Thus the selecting potentials of (27.33) admit the approximation asked for in 33.9.2, whenever the curvature margin $\theta -c\Theta _ Z$ is positive.

27.10.1 A bounded divisorial band from fiber integrability

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The comparison of theorem 27.10.2 does not need the exact local form (27.33). It suffices to bound the selecting potential from below by a divisorial pole on all of $X$, and from above by an anisotropic model in a single chart over the divisor. The lower bound gives the inclusion of the reference spaces into the selected spaces. The upper bound forces vanishing along the divisor to an order that falls short of the reference order by a bounded amount: separate powers of the normal variables replace the shift by the fiber dimension with a weighted shift. The selecting potential may then be singular along a positive-dimensional subset of a fiber, and it need not descend to the base.

Let $\pi :X\to B$ be a surjective holomorphic submersion of connected compact complex manifolds with connected fibers of dimension $f\geq 1$, and let $B$ be Kähler of dimension $q\geq 1$. Fix a Hermitian form $\omega $ on $X$, a holomorphic line bundle $A$ on $B$ with a smooth Hermitian metric $h$, and $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$. Write $\theta =c_1(A,h)$, and use holomorphic descent to regard the selected spaces as subspaces of $H^0(B,A^ m)$:

\[ V_ m=H^0\bigl (X,\pi ^*A^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\varphi )\bigr ) \subseteq H^0(B,A^ m),\qquad m\geq 1. \]

Let $D\subset B$ be a nonempty smooth connected reduced divisor. Fix a smooth metric $H_ D$ on $\mathcal O_ B(D)$, and write $s_ D$ for its canonical section and $\eta =c_1(\mathcal O_ B(D),H_ D)$ for its curvature.

Lemma 27.10.5. Let $r\geq 1$ and $\ell \geq 0$ be integers, let $p,b_1,\ldots ,b_ r$ be positive integers, and let $P>0$. The function

\[ |z|^{2\ell }\left(|z|^{2p}+\sum _{j=1}^ r|w_ j|^{2b_ j}\right)^{-P} \]

of $(z,w)\in \mathbb C\times \mathbb C^ r$ is locally integrable near the origin if and only if

\[ \frac{\ell +1}{p}+\sum _{j=1}^ r\frac1{b_ j}>P. \]

In the case of equality, the integral diverges logarithmically.

Proof. After integrating over the angles, substitute $x_0=|z|^{2p}$ and $x_ j=|w_ j|^{2b_ j}$. Up to a positive constant, the integral becomes

\[ \int (x_0+\cdots +x_ r)^{-P}x_0^{(\ell +1)/p-1} \prod _{j=1}^ r x_ j^{1/b_ j-1}\, \mathrm dx_0\cdots \mathrm dx_ r \]

over a box near the origin. This box contains a small simplex $\{ \sum _ jx_ j<\delta \} $ and is contained in a larger one. On either simplex use the coordinates $\rho =\sum _ jx_ j$ and $x_ j=\rho y_ j$. The integral over $y$ is finite and positive, because all its exponents exceed $-1$, and the integral over $\rho $ is

\[ \int _0^\delta \rho ^{(\ell +1)/p+\sum b_ j^{-1}-P-1}\, \mathrm d\rho . \]

This gives the threshold and the logarithmic divergence at equality. Bounded factors in the weight, holomorphic units and additional tangential variables do not affect this local test. $\square$

Theorem 27.10.6. Suppose that $c>0$ and $u\in C^\infty (B,\mathbb R)$ satisfy

\[ \beta =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u-c\eta >0, \qquad \varphi \geq c\log \| \pi ^*s_ D\| _{H_ D}^2-C \quad \hbox{on }X \]

for a constant $C$. Suppose further that there is one submersion polydisk over an open subset of $D$, with coordinates

\[ \pi (z,t,w,v)=(z,t),\quad D=\{ z=0\} ,\quad t\in \mathbb C^{q-1},\quad w\in \mathbb C^ r,\quad v\in \mathbb C^{f-r}, \]

where $1\leq r\leq f$, on which

\[ \varphi \leq \frac cp\log \left(|z|^{2p}+\sum _{j=1}^ r|w_ j|^{2b_ j}\right)+C' \]

for positive integers $p,b_1,\ldots ,b_ r$ and a constant $C'$. Put

\[ a=p\sum _{j=1}^ r b_ j^{-1},\quad L_ m=\lfloor mc\rfloor ,\quad K_ m=\max \{ \lfloor mc-a\rfloor ,0\} ,\quad \psi =u+c\log \| s_ D\| _{H_ D}^2. \]

Then $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(B,\theta )$, its non-pluripolar mass is $\int _ B\beta ^ q\in (0,\infty )$, and for every $m\geq 1$,

\[ W_ m:=H^0\bigl (B,A^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\psi )\bigr ) =H^0(B,A^ m(-L_ mD))\subseteq V_ m \subseteq H^0(B,A^ m(-K_ mD)). \]

Moreover $\dim V_ m-\dim W_ m=O(m^{q-1})=o(m^ q)$. For $q=1$ this difference is at most $\lceil a\rceil $ for every $m$.

Proof. By the Poincaré–Lelong formula, $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =\beta +c[D]$. Off $D$, locality identifies the non-pluripolar top power with $\beta ^ q$; the non-pluripolar product puts no mass on $D$, and neither does the smooth measure $\beta ^ q$. Hence $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi )=\beta ^ q$, whose mass is finite and positive because $\beta >0$ and $B$ is compact. The one-variable integrability condition $\ell +1>mc$ gives $\mathcal I(m\psi )=\mathcal O_ B(-L_ mD)$, also when $mc$ is an integer. Since $u$ is bounded, the global lower bound implies $\varphi \geq \psi \circ \pi -C''$. Hence integrability on the base gives integrability of the pullback in every bounded submersion chart, which proves $W_ m\subseteq V_ m$.

For the upper inclusion, take a nonzero section in $V_ m$ and let $\ell $ be its vanishing order along $D$; note that $D$ is irreducible, being smooth and connected. In the polydisk of the upper bound, the coefficient of the section is $z^\ell g(z,t)$, where $g(0,t)$ does not vanish identically on the open subset of $D$, since otherwise the identity theorem would increase the order along $D$. Shrink the polydisk about a point with $w=0$ at which $g(0,t)\ne 0$, so that $g$ becomes a holomorphic unit. The upper bound on $\varphi $ and lemma 27.10.5 then force $\ell +1>mc-a$. For an integer $\ell \geq 0$ this is equivalent to $\ell \geq K_ m$, which proves the upper inclusion.

Since $\lfloor mc\rfloor -\lfloor mc-a\rfloor \leq \lceil a\rceil $, we have $0\leq L_ m-K_ m\leq \lceil a\rceil $. Use the restriction sequences along $D$ from the proof of theorem 27.10.2 for $K_ m\leq j<L_ m$. Each step loses at most $h^0(D,(A^ m(-jD))|_ D)$ dimensions, and by lemma 27.10.1 this is at most $Cm^{q-1}$, uniformly for $0\leq j\leq cm$. Since the number of steps is bounded, the estimate follows. For $q=1$ the connected divisor $D$ is a single point, and each restriction space has dimension one. $\square$

Corollary 27.10.7. Keep the positivity condition $\beta >0$ of theorem 27.10.6. Suppose that a nonempty compact smooth submanifold $Z\subset \pi ^{-1}(D)$ projects submersively onto $D$, and that near every point of $Z$ there are coordinates as in that theorem in which

\[ Z=\{ z=w_1=\cdots =w_ r=0\} ,\qquad \varphi =\frac cp\log \left(|z|^{2p}+\sum _{j=1}^ r|w_ j|^{2b_ j}\right)+O(1), \]

with fixed $c,p,b_ j$. Suppose also that $\varphi $ is locally bounded outside $Z$. Then $\varphi $ satisfies the hypotheses of theorem 27.10.6, so its conclusions hold, and moreover

\[ V_ m=H^0(B,A^ m(-K_ mD))\qquad (m\geq 1). \]

Proof. The local model is bounded below by $c\log |z|^2$ up to a constant. Near points of $\pi ^{-1}(D)$ outside $Z$, the function $\varphi $ is locally bounded, so the same lower bound holds there; away from $\pi ^{-1}(D)$ both functions are locally bounded. By compactness, finitely many charts give the global lower bound. The upper bound holds in any chart of the model. Conversely, if a section is divisible by $z^{K_ m}$, its residual coefficient is bounded and holomorphic in each chart of the model, and the strict inequality $K_ m+1>mc-a$ together with lemma 27.10.5 gives integrability there. Outside $Z$ the function $\varphi $ is locally bounded, which proves the reverse inclusion. $\square$

Such exact models exist with pole sets of positive dimension inside the fibers. Let $B$ be projective, let $A$ be ample, and let $X=B\times (\mathbb P^1)^ f$ with $\pi $ the first projection. Mark a point $o_ j$ in each of the first $r$ factors $\mathbb P^1$ of the fiber, and let $E_ j\subset X$ be the inverse image of $o_ j$. With smooth metrics on the corresponding line bundles, put

\[ Q=\| \pi ^*s_ D\| ^{2p}+\sum _{j=1}^ r\| s_{E_ j}\| ^{2b_ j}, \qquad \varphi =\lambda \log Q,\qquad c=p\lambda . \]

The common zero set of the terms of $Q$ is $D\times \{ o_1\} \times \cdots \times \{ o_ r\} \times (\mathbb P^1)^{f-r}$. Near it, the positive factors of the metrics give exactly the local model of corollary 27.10.7, and $\varphi $ is smooth elsewhere. The inequality $Q\geq \| \pi ^*s_ D\| ^{2p}$ gives the global lower bound. Locally, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log Q$ is bounded below by a convex combination of the negatives of the smooth curvature forms of the terms; hence $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log Q\geq -C_0\omega $ for a constant $C_0$, first off the common zero set and then across it, after a quadratic correction, by the extension theorem for plurisubharmonic functions. Since $A$ is ample, there is $u$ with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0$. For sufficiently small $\lambda >0$ we then have $\lambda C_0\leq 1$, so that $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u-p\lambda \eta >0$. Thus the exact series of the corollary also occurs when $r<f$.

Example 27.10.8. Let $B=\mathbb P^ q$ with a hyperplane $D$, let $A=\mathcal O_ B(1)$, and let $X=B\times \mathbb P^1$. Let $\omega _ B,\omega _ F$ be the Fubini–Study forms normalized to have total mass one, and use the corresponding metrics on the divisor line bundles. For a point $o\in \mathbb P^1$ set

\[ \varphi =\tfrac 14\log \bigl (\| s_ D\| ^4+\| s_ o\| ^6\bigr ),\qquad \omega =\omega _ B+\omega _ F. \]

Then $p=2$, $b_1=3$, $c=1/2$, $a=2/3$, and the reference potential $\psi =\tfrac 12\log \| s_ D\| ^2$ has mass $2^{-q}$. The spaces $V_1$ and $W_1$ agree, and for every $m\geq 2$ the defect is

\[ \dim V_ m-\dim W_ m =\binom {\lceil m/2\rceil +q}{q-1} \sim \frac{2^{1-q}}{(q-1)!}m^{q-1}. \]

For $q=1$ the defect is exactly one for every $m\geq 2$.

Proof. Locally, the complex Hessian of the logarithm of a sum of two squared norms of holomorphic sections is bounded below by a convex combination of the negatives of their curvature forms. Hence $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq -\tfrac 12\omega _ B-\tfrac 34\omega _ F$ off the pole set, and across it by the extension theorem after a quadratic correction. Thus $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$. The pole set is $D\times \{ o\} $, along which $\varphi $ has the exact local model of corollary 27.10.7. Here $u=0$, $\beta =\tfrac 12\omega _ B$, and $K_ m=L_ m-1$ for $m\geq 2$, while $K_1=L_1=0$. The spaces $V_ m$ and $W_ m$ therefore correspond to homogeneous polynomials of degrees $m-L_ m+1$ and $m-L_ m$, and since $m-L_ m=\lceil m/2\rceil $, the difference of their dimensions is the displayed binomial coefficient. $\square$

In this example $\varphi $ is finite on most of each fiber over $D$ and equals $-\infty $ on a proper subset of it, so $\varphi $ differs from every pullback of a function on $B$ by an unbounded amount. For $p=b_ j=1$ and $r=f$, the shift $a=f$ is the shift by the fiber dimension found in theorem 27.10.2. Theorem 27.10.6 thus gives sufficient conditions for the approximation asked for in 33.9.2; we do not know which other fiber singularities admit such an approximation.

The assumption $f\geq 1$ excludes only a trivial case. If the fibers have dimension zero, then $\pi $ is a covering, since $X$ is compact and $\pi $ is a submersion, and hence a biholomorphism, since the fibers are connected. If, in addition, the descended selecting potential is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic with finite positive mass, taking it as $\psi $ gives $V_ m=W_ m$.

27.10.2 Several crossing base divisors

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Keep the submersion $\pi :X\to B$, the line bundle $(A,h)$, the selecting potential $\varphi $ and the descended spaces $V_ m$ of section 27.10.1. Several divisorial singularities can be handled at once if the divisors cross normally. At their intersections the integrability test on the base separates in normal coordinates, and a bounded change of each divisorial order again costs only $O(m^{q-1})$ sections. For this we need the restriction estimate with all the divisorial twists at once.

Lemma 27.10.9. Let $M$ be a compact complex manifold of dimension $d\geq 0$, and let $E,F_1,\ldots ,F_ N$ be holomorphic line bundles on $M$. For each $C_0>0$ there is a constant $C_1$ such that

\[ h^0\left(M,E^ m\otimes \bigotimes _{i=1}^ NF_ i^{j_ i}\right)\leq C_1m^ d \qquad (m\geq 1,\ j_ i\in \mathbb Z,\ |j_ i|\leq C_0m). \]

Proof. For $d=0$ the dimension is at most the number of points of $M$. For $d\geq 1$, use fixed smooth metrics, a smooth positive volume form and finitely many framed charts as in the proof of lemma 27.10.1. The local weight of the metric is $m\phi _ E+\sum _ i j_ i\phi _{F_ i}$, whose second derivatives are bounded by $Cm$ uniformly over the stated range. After its constant and real-linear terms at a point have been removed by a holomorphic affine change of frame, it is bounded by a fixed constant on a ball of radius $\varepsilon m^{-1/2}$ about that point. The submean inequality then gives $|s(x)|^2\leq Cm^ d\| s\| _{L^2}^2$, uniformly in $x$, $m$ and the integers $j_ i$. Since evaluation has a one-dimensional target, an orthonormal basis satisfies $\sum _\ell |s_\ell (x)|^2\leq Cm^ d$, and integration gives the estimate. The constants depend only on the fixed bundles, metrics, charts, volume and $C_0$. $\square$

Let $D_1,\ldots ,D_ N$ be distinct nonempty smooth connected reduced divisors on $B$ whose union has simple normal crossings. For each $i$, choose a smooth metric $H_ i$ on $\mathcal O_ B(D_ i)$, and write $s_ i$ for the canonical section and $\eta _ i$ for the curvature.

Theorem 27.10.10. Suppose that $c_1,\ldots ,c_ N>0$ and $u\in C^\infty (B,\mathbb R)$ satisfy

\[ \beta =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u-\sum _{i=1}^ Nc_ i\eta _ i>0, \qquad \varphi \geq \sum _{i=1}^ Nc_ i\log \| \pi ^*s_ i\| _{H_ i}^2-C \quad \hbox{on }X. \]

Suppose that for each $i$ there is a submersion polydisk over an open subset of $D_ i\setminus \bigcup _{j\ne i}D_ j$, with coordinates $(z_ i,t_ i,w_ i,v_ i)$ as in theorem 27.10.6, on which

\[ \varphi \leq \frac{c_ i}{p_ i}\log \left(|z_ i|^{2p_ i} +\sum _{j=1}^{r_ i}|w_{ij}|^{2b_{ij}}\right)+C_ i, \qquad 1\leq r_ i\leq f, \]

where $p_ i$ and $b_{ij}$ are positive integers and $C_ i$ is a constant. Put

\[ \begin{gathered} a_ i=p_ i\sum _{j=1}^{r_ i}b_{ij}^{-1},\quad L_{m,i}=\lfloor mc_ i\rfloor ,\quad K_{m,i}=\max \{ \lfloor mc_ i-a_ i\rfloor ,0\} ,\\ \psi =u+\sum _{i=1}^ Nc_ i\log \| s_ i\| _{H_ i}^2. \end{gathered} \]

Then $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(B,\theta )$ has non-pluripolar mass $\int _ B\beta ^ q\in (0,\infty )$, and for every $m\geq 1$,

\[ \begin{split} W_ m& :=H^0\bigl (B,A^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\psi )\bigr ) =H^0\left(B,A^ m\left(-\sum _ iL_{m,i}D_ i\right)\right)\subseteq V_ m,\\ V_ m& \subseteq H^0\left(B,A^ m\left(-\sum _ iK_{m,i}D_ i\right)\right). \end{split} \]

Moreover $\dim V_ m-\dim W_ m=O(m^{q-1})$. For $q=1$ this difference is at most $\sum _ i\lceil a_ i\rceil $ for every $m$.

Proof. By the Poincaré–Lelong formula, $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =\beta +\sum _ i c_ i[D_ i]$. Off the divisors the non-pluripolar measure equals $\beta ^ q$, and neither measure charges their union. Thus $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi )=\beta ^ q$, with finite positive total mass.

Near a point of an intersection, the divisors through that point are distinct coordinate hyperplanes $\{ z_ i=0\} $. If a holomorphic germ is divisible by each corresponding $z_ i^{L_{m,i}}$, then it is divisible by their product; its residual coefficient is bounded, and since $L_{m,i}+1>mc_ i$, Fubini's theorem gives integrability. Conversely, integrability at generic nearby points of each coordinate hyperplane forces divisibility by $z_ i^{L_{m,i}}$, by the one-variable threshold, and the divisor of the germ then gives divisibility throughout the chart. This proves

\[ \mathcal I(m\psi )=\mathcal O_ B\left(-\sum _ iL_{m,i}D_ i\right). \]

Since $u$ is bounded, the global lower bound implies $\varphi \geq \psi \circ \pi -C'$, and integration in submersion coordinates gives $W_ m\subseteq V_ m$. For a nonzero section in $V_ m$, the polydisk over $D_ i$ on which the upper bound holds detects the generic vanishing order of the section along the irreducible divisor $D_ i$. The argument with a unit coefficient in the proof of theorem 27.10.6, together with the strict integrability threshold of lemma 27.10.5, shows that this order is at least $K_{m,i}$. Imposing all these orders proves the upper inclusion.

To compare dimensions, raise the orders from $(K_{m,i})_ i$ to $(L_{m,i})_ i$, one divisor and one unit at a time. At each step, the restriction sequence loses at most

\[ h^0\left(D_ i,\left.A^ m\left(-\sum _ j n_ jD_ j\right)\right|_{D_ i}\right), \qquad 0\leq n_ j\leq mc_ j, \]

dimensions. By lemma 27.10.9 with $E=A|_{D_ i}$ and $F_ j=\mathcal O_ B(-D_ j)|_{D_ i}$, this is $O(m^{q-1})$ uniformly over all steps, and there are at most $\sum _ i\lceil a_ i\rceil $ steps. When $q=1$, the distinct connected divisors are points, and each restriction space has dimension one. $\square$

The upper bounds are required only away from the crossings; integrability at the crossings is controlled by the global lower bound. Exact models can nevertheless have fiber poles over the crossings, as in the following example, where different divisors use separate fiber coordinates.

Example 27.10.11. Let $B=(\mathbb P^1)^ q$, $X=B\times (\mathbb P^1)^ q$ and $A=\mathcal O_ B(1,\ldots ,1)$. For each $i$, let $D_ i\subset B$ be the inverse image of a marked point in the $i$th factor of $B$, and let $E_ i\subset X$ be the inverse image of a marked point in the $i$th factor of the fiber. Use normalized Fubini–Study metrics on the corresponding line bundles, and write $\omega _{B,i},\omega _{F,i}$ for the pullbacks to $X$ of the normalized Fubini–Study forms on the $i$th factors of $B$ and of the fiber. Put

\[ \begin{gathered} R_ i=\| \pi ^*s_ i\| ^2,\quad T_ i=\| s_{E_ i}\| ^2,\quad \varphi =\frac14\sum _{i=1}^ q\log (R_ i^2+T_ i^3),\\ \omega =\sum _ i(\omega _{B,i}+\omega _{F,i}),\qquad \psi =\frac12\sum _ i\log \| s_ i\| ^2. \end{gathered} \]

Then $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, and theorem 27.10.10 applies with $u=0$, $c_ i=1/2$, $p_ i=2$, $b_{i1}=3$ and $a_ i=2/3$. The reference potential $\psi $ has mass $q!/2^ q$. With $L_ m=\lfloor m/2\rfloor $ and $K_ m=\max \{ \lfloor m/2-2/3\rfloor ,0\} $, the selected and reference spaces are exactly

\[ \begin{split} V_ m& =H^0\bigl (B,\mathcal O_ B(m-K_ m,\ldots ,m-K_ m)\bigr ),\\ W_ m& =H^0\bigl (B,\mathcal O_ B(m-L_ m,\ldots ,m-L_ m)\bigr ). \end{split} \]

They agree for $m=1$, and for $m\geq 2$ the defect is

\[ \dim V_ m-\dim W_ m =(\lceil m/2\rceil +2)^ q-(\lceil m/2\rceil +1)^ q. \]

For $q\geq 2$ this is $q2^{1-q}m^{q-1}+O(m^{q-2})$, and for $q=1$ it is exactly one.

Proof. By the calculation in example 27.10.8, the complex Hessian of each summand of $\varphi $ is bounded below by $-\tfrac 12\omega _{B,i}-\tfrac 34\omega _{F,i}$, also across its pole set by the extension theorem after a quadratic correction. Summing proves $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$. The global lower bound follows from $R_ i^2+T_ i^3\geq R_ i^2$. For the upper bound over $D_ i$, away from the other divisors, place the $i$th fiber coordinate at its marked point; all the other summands are smooth there. The residual is $\beta =\tfrac 12\sum _ i\omega _{B,i}>0$, whose top integral is $q!/2^ q$.

The theorem gives the required generic orders along every $D_ i$. Conversely, near a point where several of the pairs $(R_ i,T_ i)$ vanish, the density $\mathrm{e}^{-m\varphi }$ is comparable to the product of the factors $(|z_ i|^4+|w_ i|^6)^{-m/4}$ in disjoint pairs of variables. A descended coefficient divisible by every $z_ i^{K_ m}$ has a bounded holomorphic residual. Each factor is integrable because $K_ m+1>m/2-2/3$, and Fubini's theorem gives integrability at the intersections; elsewhere the same argument uses fewer factors. This proves the identity for $V_ m$, and the theorem gives $W_ m$. A line bundle $\mathcal O_ B(d,\ldots ,d)$ with $d\geq 0$ has $(d+1)^ q$ independent sections, the products of monomials in the separate factors. Since $K_ m=L_ m-1$ for $m\geq 2$ and $m-L_ m=\lceil m/2\rceil $, subtraction gives the displayed defect and its leading term. $\square$

As before, the selecting potential takes finite values and the value $-\infty $ in the fibers over the divisors, so it does not descend. Theorem 27.10.10 extends the sufficient conditions for 33.9.2 to normal crossing divisors; it does not cover base ideals of other forms, and it gives no necessary conditions.

27.10.3 Fiber integrability on a modified base

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Keep $\pi :X\to B$, $(A,h)$, $\theta $, $\varphi $ and $V_ m$ as in section 27.10.1. A singularity of higher codimension on the base can become divisorial after a modification of the base. By lemma 1.22.15, the integrability test upstairs then applies to the pulled-back section multiplied by the Jacobian. This fixed factor shifts the divisorial orders, while the restriction estimate still bounds the number of lost sections.

Let $\rho :Y\to B$ be a modification of connected compact complex manifolds, and form the fiber product and its projections

\[ X'=X\times _ B Y,\qquad \pi ':X'\to Y,\qquad \mu :X'\to X. \]

In local submersion coordinates one sees that $X'$ is smooth, that $\pi '$ is a submersion with the same compact connected fibers as $\pi $, and that $\mu $ is a modification. Let $R_\rho $ and $J_\rho $ be the relative canonical bundle and the Jacobian section of $\rho $, as in section 1.22.7. The coordinate Jacobian matrix of $\mu $ is that of $\rho $, extended by the identity in the fiber variables, so

\[ R_\mu =\pi '^*R_\rho ,\qquad J_\mu =\pi '^*J_\rho . \]

Put $\varphi '=\varphi \circ \mu $ and $\theta '=\rho ^*\theta $. Choose distinct nonempty smooth connected reduced divisors $D_1,\ldots ,D_ N$ on $Y$ whose union has simple normal crossings, together with smooth metrics $H_ i$ on $\mathcal O_ Y(D_ i)$, canonical sections $s_ i$ and curvatures $\eta _ i$.

Theorem 27.10.12. Suppose that $c_ i>0$ and $u\in C^\infty (Y,\mathbb R)$ satisfy

\[ \beta =\theta '+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u-\sum _ i c_ i\eta _ i>0, \qquad \varphi '\geq \sum _ i c_ i\log \| \pi '^*s_ i\| _{H_ i}^2-C. \]

Suppose that for each $i$ the function $\varphi '$ satisfies an anisotropic upper bound, with respect to $\pi '$, on a submersion polydisk over an open subset of $D_ i$ away from the other divisors, as in theorem 27.10.10, with fixed $p_ i,b_{ij}$. Define $a_ i$, $L_{m,i}$ and $K_{m,i}$ by the formulas of that theorem, and put

\[ \psi '=u+\sum _ i c_ i\log \| s_ i\| _{H_ i}^2, \qquad M_ m=\rho ^*A^ m\otimes R_\rho . \]

Then there is a unique $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(B,\theta )$ with $\psi \circ \rho =\psi '$, and

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi )=\rho _*(\beta ^ q),\qquad \int _ B\mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi )=\int _ Y\beta ^ q\in (0,\infty ). \]

The map $T_ m(s)=J_\rho \rho ^*s$ identifies $H^0(B,A^ m)$ with $H^0(Y,M_ m)$, and for $W_ m=H^0(B,A^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\psi ))$,

\[ \begin{split} T_ m(W_ m)& =H^0\left(Y,M_ m\left(-\sum _ iL_{m,i}D_ i\right)\right),\\ T_ m(W_ m)& \subseteq T_ m(V_ m) \subseteq H^0\left(Y,M_ m\left(-\sum _ iK_{m,i}D_ i\right)\right). \end{split} \]

Consequently $W_ m\subseteq V_ m$ and $\dim V_ m-\dim W_ m=O(m^{q-1})$. If $q=1$, the difference is at most $\sum _ i\lceil a_ i\rceil $ for every $m$.

Proof. By the Poincaré–Lelong formula, $\theta '+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi '=\beta +\sum _ i c_ i[D_ i]$. Thus $\psi '\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\rho ^*\theta )$, and proposition 1.22.12 gives the unique potential $\psi $ on $B$ with $\psi \circ \rho =\psi '$. On $Y$, locality off the union of the divisors and the fact that the non-pluripolar measure does not charge this union give $\mathrm{MA}_{\theta '}(\psi ')=\beta ^ q$. The measure identity in proposition 27.6.1 then gives the pushforward formula and the mass.

Lemma 1.22.15, applied first with weight zero and then with weight $m\psi $, gives the identification of the full section spaces and the formula for $T_ m(W_ m)$; here $\mathcal I(m\psi ')=\mathcal O_ Y(-\sum _ iL_{m,i}D_ i)$ by the integrability computation for simple normal crossings in the proof of theorem 27.10.10. Since $u$ is bounded, the global lower bound gives $\varphi '\geq \psi '\circ \pi '-C'$. As $\rho \circ \pi '=\pi \circ \mu $ and $\mu $ is surjective, this descends to $\varphi \geq \psi \circ \pi -C'$, and integrability in bounded submersion charts proves $W_ m\subseteq V_ m$.

For $s\in V_ m$, the transformation of multiplier ideals under $\mu $ gives

\[ J_\mu \mu ^*s=\pi '^*T_ m(s) \in H^0\bigl (X',\pi '^*M_ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\varphi ')\bigr ). \]

Hence, in each polydisk where the upper bound holds, the descended coefficient of $T_ m(s)$ vanishes to order at least $K_{m,i}$ along $D_ i$, by lemma 27.10.5 and the argument with a unit coefficient in the proof of theorem 27.10.6. This is the vanishing order of $T_ m(s)$, including the Jacobian factor, and it proves the upper inclusion.

The chain of restrictions along the divisors on $Y$ has at most $\sum _ i\lceil a_ i\rceil $ steps. On $D_ i$, each target is $(\rho ^*A|_{D_ i})^ m$ twisted by powers of the bundles $\mathcal O_ Y(-D_ j)|_{D_ i}$ in a linear range and by the fixed bundle $R_\rho |_{D_ i}$. Including the latter in lemma 27.10.9 with exponent one, we see that every target has dimension $O(m^{q-1})$, uniformly. Summing gives the estimate. For $q=1$ each divisor is a point and every target has dimension one. $\square$

The theorem requires the modification and the divisorial data to be given, with a positive residual; it does not say which selecting potentials admit such data. In the following model with a point pole on the base, both the residual and the Jacobian correction are explicit.

Example 27.10.13. Let $q\geq 2$, $B=\mathbb P^ q$, $A=\mathcal O_ B(1)$, $p=[1:0:\cdots :0]$ and $X=B\times \mathbb P^1$. Use the normalized Fubini–Study forms $\omega _ B,\omega _ F$ and the corresponding metrics, and for a point $o\in \mathbb P^1$ put

\[ R=\frac{\sum _{j=1}^ q|Z_ j|^2}{\sum _{j=0}^ q|Z_ j|^2},\quad T=\| s_ o\| ^2,\quad \varphi =\tfrac 14\log (R^2+T^3),\quad \psi =\tfrac 12\log R. \]

Then $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega _ B+\omega _ F)$, and $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(B,\omega _ B)$ has mass $1-2^{-q}$. Let $\mathfrak m_ p$ be the ideal sheaf of $p$. The selected and reference spaces are exactly

\[ \begin{gathered} V_ m=H^0\bigl (B,A^ m\otimes \mathfrak m_ p^{k_ m}\bigr ),\qquad W_ m=H^0\bigl (B,A^ m\otimes \mathfrak m_ p^{\ell _ m}\bigr ),\\ k_ m=\max \{ \lfloor m/2-2/3\rfloor -q+1,0\} ,\qquad \ell _ m=\max \{ \lfloor m/2\rfloor -q+1,0\} . \end{gathered} \]

Their defect vanishes for $m<2q$, and for $m\geq 2q$ it is

\[ \dim V_ m-\dim W_ m=\binom {\lfloor m/2\rfloor -1}{q-1} \sim \frac{2^{1-q}}{(q-1)!}m^{q-1}. \]

Proof. Over a common denominator, $R^2+T^3$ is a sum of squared moduli of holomorphic coefficients of sections of $\mathcal O_ X(2,3)$, divided by the corresponding metric factor. Hence $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq -\tfrac 12\omega _ B-\tfrac 34\omega _ F$, also across the common zero set by the extension theorem.

Let $\rho :Y\to B$ be the blowup of $p$, with exceptional divisor $E$, and let $\tau :Y\to \mathbb P^{q-1}$ be the extension to $Y$ of the projection $[Z_0:\cdots :Z_ q]\mapsto [Z_1:\cdots :Z_ q]$. Since $(\rho ,\tau )$ is an embedding, the forms

\[ \alpha =\rho ^*\omega _ B,\quad \gamma =\tau ^*\omega _{\mathbb P^{q-1}},\qquad \beta =\tfrac 12(\alpha +\gamma ) \]

satisfy $\beta >0$. The divisor bundle is $\mathcal O_ Y(E)=\rho ^*\mathcal O_ B(1)\otimes \tau ^*\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^{q-1}}(-1)$; its quotient metric has curvature $\alpha -\gamma $ and satisfies $R\circ \rho =\| s_ E\| ^2$. Thus the theorem applies with the single divisor $E$, $u=0$, $c=1/2$, $a=2/3$ and residual $\beta $. Along $E\times \{ o\} $ the pulled-back selecting potential has the exact local model $\tfrac 14\log (|z|^4+|w|^6)+O(1)$, and it is locally bounded elsewhere. If $T_ m(s)$ is divisible by $z^{K_ m}$, where $K_ m=\max \{ \lfloor m/2-2/3\rfloor ,0\} $, then lemma 27.10.5 gives integrability in every chart of this model; this gives the inclusion opposite to the upper inclusion of the theorem.

In the blowup coordinates $Z_1/Z_0=z$ and $Z_ j/Z_0=zt_ j$ for $j\geq 2$, the Jacobian of $\rho $ is $z^{q-1}$. Hence, if a section $s$ of $A^ m$ vanishes to order $d$ at $p$, then $T_ m(s)$ vanishes to order $d+q-1$ generically along $E$. The two divisorial thresholds $\lfloor m/2\rfloor $ and $K_ m$ therefore give exactly the orders $\ell _ m$ and $k_ m$ above. Homogeneous polynomials of degree $m$ realize every jet of total degree at most $m$ at $p$. For $m\geq 2q$ we have $k_ m=\ell _ m-1$, and the additional jets of degree $\ell _ m-1$ are spanned by $\binom {\ell _ m+q-2}{q-1}$ monomials, which gives the defect. For $m<2q$ both orders vanish.

Finally, in cohomology $[\beta ]=\rho ^*c_1(A)-\tfrac 12[E]$. The intersection numbers containing both $\rho ^*c_1(A)$ and $[E]$ vanish, because $\rho ^*c_1(A)$ restricts to zero on $E$. The normal bundle of $E\simeq \mathbb P^{q-1}$ is $\mathcal O_ E(-1)$, so $\int _ Y[E]^ q=(-1)^{q-1}$, while $\int _ Y(\rho ^*c_1(A))^ q=1$. Expanding gives $\int _ Y\beta ^ q=1-2^{-q}$, which is the mass of $\psi $ by the theorem. $\square$

The selecting potential is finite at most points of the fiber over $p$ and equals $-\infty $ at the point $(p,o)$, so again it does not descend, even up to a bounded function. The reference potential $\psi $ has a pole of codimension $q$ at $p$, and the Jacobian factor is what converts the divisorial orders along $E$ into vanishing orders at $p$.

27.10.4 Semipositive residuals with positive volume

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Strict positivity of the residual $\beta $ was used only to make the reference potential $\psi $ plurisubharmonic with positive non-pluripolar mass; the estimates for the divisorial orders do not use it. The section comparison therefore holds for semipositive residuals with positive top integral. In particular, it does not require the residual class to contain a positive form.

Corollary 27.10.14. In each of theorem 27.10.6, theorem 27.10.10, theorem 27.10.12, replace the condition $\beta >0$ by

\[ \beta \geq 0,\qquad \int _ M\beta ^ q>0, \]

where $M=B$ in the first two theorems and $M=Y$ in the third, and keep all the other hypotheses. Then all the conclusions remain valid. The same replacement is valid in corollary 27.10.7.

Proof. In each case the reference current is $\beta +\sum _ i c_ i[D_ i]$, which is positive. Locality off the union of the divisors, together with the fact that the non-pluripolar measure does not charge this union, gives the non-pluripolar measure $\beta ^ q$ on $M$. Its mass is finite because $\beta $ is smooth and $M$ is compact, and positive by assumption. In the case of a modified base, the potential descends and its measure is $\rho _*(\beta ^ q)$, by the same descent and measure identities as before.

The multiplier ideal on the base depends only on the logarithmic divisorial orders and the bounded smooth term. The lower inclusion uses the global lower bound for the selecting potential, and the upper inclusion uses the anisotropic threshold in a polydisk where the upper bound holds; neither uses strict positivity of $\beta $. The restriction estimates concern fixed line bundles, whatever their curvature, and the Jacobian and the fixed canonical twist in the case of a modified base are unchanged. Hence the inclusions and dimension bounds in all three proofs remain valid. The reverse inclusion for the exact anisotropic model uses only its local integrability test and the local boundedness off the pole set, so it also remains valid. $\square$

The condition on the top integral concerns the chosen reference: a semipositive residual may have zero top integral. Conversely, a semipositive residual with positive top integral need not become strictly positive after any smooth change of gauge. The following example shows this, with an exact computation of the selected spaces.

Example 27.10.15. Let $q\geq 2$, let $\rho :B\to \mathbb P^ q$ be the blowup of $p=[1:0:\cdots :0]$, and let $E$ be its exceptional divisor. Let $\tau :B\to \mathbb P^{q-1}$ be the extension to $B$ of the projection from $p$, and put

\[ \alpha =\rho ^*\omega _{\mathbb P^ q},\qquad \gamma =\tau ^*\omega _{\mathbb P^{q-1}},\qquad \kappa =\alpha +\gamma >0, \]

where $\omega _{\mathbb P^ q}$ and $\omega _{\mathbb P^{q-1}}$ are the normalized Fubini–Study forms. Equip $\mathcal O_ B(E)=\rho ^*\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^ q}(1)\otimes \tau ^*\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^{q-1}}(-1)$ with the quotient metric, whose curvature is $\eta =\alpha -\gamma $, and let $s_ E$ be its canonical section. For an integer $k\geq 1$, equip

\[ A=\rho ^*\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^ q}(k)\otimes \mathcal O_ B(E) \quad \hbox{with curvature}\quad \theta =k\alpha +\eta . \]

Let $X=B\times \mathbb P^1$, with projection $\pi $ to $B$. For a point $o\in \mathbb P^1$, use the normalized Fubini–Study metric on $\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(o)$, and set

\[ \begin{gathered} R=\| \pi ^*s_ E\| ^2,\quad T=\| s_ o\| ^2,\quad \varphi =\tfrac 12\log (R^2+T^3),\quad \omega =\pi ^*\kappa +2\omega _{\mathbb P^1},\\ \psi =\log \| s_ E\| ^2. \end{gathered} \]

Then $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(B,\theta )$ and

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi )=(k\alpha )^ q,\qquad \int _ B\mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi )=k^ q. \]

The residual $k\alpha $ cannot be made strictly positive by a smooth change of gauge. Nevertheless, for every $m\geq 1$ the selected and reference spaces coincide as subspaces of $H^0(B,A^ m)$:

\[ V_ m=W_ m=H^0(B,A^ m) =s_ E^ m\rho ^*H^0(\mathbb P^ q,\mathcal O(km)), \qquad \dim V_ m=\binom {km+q}{q}. \]

Proof. Since $(\rho ,\tau )$ is an embedding, $\kappa >0$. The quotient metric satisfies

\[ \| s_ E\| ^2=\rho ^*\left(\frac{\sum _{j=1}^ q|Z_ j|^2}{\sum _{j=0}^ q|Z_ j|^2}\right), \]

as in example 27.10.13. The same calculation over a common denominator, now with coefficient $1/2$, gives

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq -\pi ^*\alpha -\tfrac 32\omega _{\mathbb P^1} \]

off the pole set $E\times \{ o\} $, and across it by the extension theorem. Hence $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq \pi ^*\gamma +\tfrac 12\omega _{\mathbb P^1}\geq 0$. Along the smooth pole set $E\times \{ o\} $, the function $\varphi $ has the exact local model $\tfrac 12\log (|z|^4+|w|^6)+O(1)$, and it is locally bounded elsewhere. It also satisfies the global lower bound $\varphi \geq \log R$.

We may therefore take $c=1$, $u=0$, $p=2$, $b_1=3$ and $a=2/3$, with residual $\beta =\theta -\eta =k\alpha \geq 0$. Its top integral is $k^ q$, since the normalized Fubini–Study form has top integral one and $\rho $ is a modification. Corollary 27.10.14, Corollary 27.10.7 therefore give

\[ W_ m=H^0(B,A^ m(-mE)),\qquad V_ m=H^0(B,A^ m(-(m-1)E)). \]

Let $t\geq 0$ be an integer. A section of $\rho ^*\mathcal O(km)\otimes \mathcal O_ B(tE)$, divided by $s_ E^ t$, gives a holomorphic section of $\mathcal O(km)$ on $\mathbb P^ q\setminus \{ p\} $, which extends across $p$ by Hartogs' theorem since $q\geq 2$. Pulling the extension back and multiplying by $s_ E^ t$ recovers the original section off $E$, hence everywhere by the identity theorem. Thus every section of $A^ m$ has the form $s_ E^ m\rho ^*s$, and the natural embeddings of $W_ m$ and $V_ m$ into $H^0(B,A^ m)$ both have this image. Its dimension is the number $\binom {km+q}{q}$ of monomials of degree $km$.

Finally, let $C\subset E\simeq \mathbb P^{q-1}$ be a projective line; it is contracted by $\rho $. For every smooth real function $u$, Stokes' theorem gives $\int _ C(k\alpha +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)=0$, whereas a strictly positive form has positive integral over $C$. Changing the smooth metric on the divisor bundle changes the residual only by $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ of a smooth function, so this proves the obstruction. Moreover, $\varphi $ is finite on most of each fiber over $E$ and equals $-\infty $ at the point $o$ of that fiber, so it does not descend, even up to a bounded function. $\square$

Thus the selected and reference spaces agree, although the selecting potential does not descend and the reference has positive mass. The reason is that $E$ is a fixed component of every section of $A^ m$: all global sections vanish along $E$ to order $m$, more than the local integrability threshold requires. As before, the criterion presupposes the global bounds for the selecting potential and a residual with positive top integral; it does not produce them for arbitrary fiber singularities.

27.10.5 Fixed exceptional orders and coefficient thresholds

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

In example 27.10.15 the fixed divisor $E$ absorbs the local integrability conditions. If the coefficient of the selecting potential increases, the selected sections must eventually also vanish at the point that was blown up. The following family shows when this happens, and when the mass of the reference potential vanishes.

Keep the blowup $\rho :B\to \mathbb P^ q$ with $q\geq 2$, its exceptional divisor $E$, the extended projection $\tau $, the forms $\alpha ,\gamma ,\kappa $, and the quotient metric on $\mathcal O_ B(E)$ with curvature $\eta $ from that example, and write $x=\rho (E)$ for the point blown up. Let $k,e\geq 1$ be integers and equip

\[ A_ e=\rho ^*\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^ q}(k)\otimes \mathcal O_ B(eE), \qquad \theta =k\alpha +e\eta \]

with the product metric. Let $X=B\times \mathbb P^1$ with its projection $\pi $, let $o\in \mathbb P^1$, and let $R=\| \pi ^*s_ E\| ^2$ and $T=\| s_ o\| ^2$ as before. For positive integers $p,b$ and a real number $c>0$, put

\[ \varphi _ c=\frac cp\log (R^ p+T^ b),\quad \omega _ c=c\pi ^*\kappa +(cb/p+1)\omega _{\mathbb P^1},\quad a=\frac pb, \]

and let $V_ m(c)\subseteq H^0(B,A_ e^ m)$ be the space selected by $\mathcal I(m\varphi _ c)$, regarded on $B$ by descent.

Proposition 27.10.16. For every $c>0$ we have $\varphi _ c\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega _ c)$, and the selected space is

\[ \begin{gathered} V_ m(c)=s_ E^{em}\rho ^*H^0\bigl (\mathbb P^ q,\mathcal O(km)\otimes \mathfrak m_ x^{v_ m}\bigr ),\\ v_ m=\max \{ \lfloor m(c-e)-a\rfloor ,0\} ,\qquad m\geq 1, \end{gathered} \]

where $\mathfrak m_ x$ is the ideal sheaf of $x$. For $0<c\leq e+k$ put

\[ d=\max \{ c,e\} ,\quad \delta =d-e,\quad \psi _ d=d\log \| s_ E\| ^2,\quad \ell _ m=\lfloor m\delta \rfloor . \]

Then $\psi _ d\in \mathrm{PSH}(B,\theta )$ and

\[ \begin{gathered} \mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi _ d)=\bigl ((k-\delta )\alpha +\delta \gamma \bigr )^ q, \qquad \int _ B\mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi _ d)=k^ q-\delta ^ q. \end{gathered} \]

The reference spaces satisfy

\[ \begin{split} W_ m(c)& :=H^0\bigl (B,A_ e^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\psi _ d)\bigr )\\ & =s_ E^{em}\rho ^*H^0\bigl (\mathbb P^ q,\mathcal O(km)\otimes \mathfrak m_ x^{\ell _ m}\bigr ) \subseteq V_ m(c). \end{split} \]

If $0<c\leq e$, both spaces equal $H^0(B,A_ e^ m)$ for every $m$. If $e<c<e+k$, the defect and the leading term of the dimension are

\[ \begin{gathered} \dim V_ m(c)-\dim W_ m(c) =\binom {\ell _ m+q-1}{q}-\binom {v_ m+q-1}{q}=O(m^{q-1}),\\ \dim V_ m(c)=\frac{k^ q-(c-e)^ q}{q!}m^ q+O(m^{q-1}), \end{gathered} \]

where $\binom {q-1}{q}=0$. At $c=e+k$ the reference $\psi _ d$ has zero mass and

\[ \dim V_ m(e+k) =\frac{(\lceil a\rceil +1)k^{q-1}}{(q-1)!}m^{q-1}+O(m^{q-2}). \]

If $c>e+k$, the selected space is zero exactly when

\[ m(c-e-k)\geq a+1; \]

in particular, it is zero for every $m\geq \lceil (a+1)/(c-e-k)\rceil $.

Proof. As in example 27.10.15, the norm identity writes $R^ p+T^ b$ over the common denominator for $\mathcal O(p,b)$ on $\mathbb P^ q\times \mathbb P^1$, pulled back to $X$, and its numerator is a sum of squared moduli of holomorphic coefficients. The lower bound for the complex Hessian of the logarithm of such a sum, together with the extension theorem across the pole set, gives

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ c\geq -c\pi ^*\alpha -(cb/p)\omega _{\mathbb P^1}. \]

Thus $\omega _ c+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ c\geq c\pi ^*\gamma +\omega _{\mathbb P^1}\geq 0$.

Hartogs' theorem, as in that example, shows that every global section of $A_ e^ m$ can be written uniquely as $s_ E^{em}\rho ^*s$ with $s\in H^0(\mathbb P^ q,\mathcal O(km))$. If $s$ vanishes to order $j$ at $x$, this section vanishes to order $em+j$ generically along $E$. In a chart of the pole set, $\varphi _ c=(c/p)\log (|z|^{2p}+|w|^{2b})+O(1)$. The argument with a unit coefficient in the proof of theorem 27.10.6 and lemma 27.10.5 require

\[ em+j+1>mc-a, \quad \hbox{equivalently}\quad j\geq \max \{ \lfloor m(c-e)-a\rfloor ,0\} . \]

Conversely, this divisorial order gives integrability in every chart of the pole set, and $\varphi _ c$ is locally bounded elsewhere. The vanishing order of $s$ at $x$ equals the generic vanishing order of $\rho ^*s$ along $E$, as the blowup coordinates of example 27.10.13 show. This proves the formula for $V_ m(c)$.

For $0\leq \delta \leq k$, the Poincaré–Lelong formula gives

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _ d=(k-\delta )\alpha +\delta \gamma +d[E]\geq 0. \]

The non-pluripolar measure does not charge $E$, and it equals the top power of the smooth residual $(k-\delta )\alpha +\delta \gamma $. The class of this residual is $k\rho ^*c_1(\mathcal O(1))-\delta [E]$. The mixed intersection numbers vanish and $\int _ B[E]^ q=(-1)^{q-1}$, as computed in example 27.10.13, so its mass is $k^ q-\delta ^ q$. The divisorial ideal is $\mathcal I(m\psi _ d)=\mathcal O_ B(-\lfloor md\rfloor E)$. Since $em$ is an integer, $\lfloor md\rfloor =em+\ell _ m$, and the condition on $s$ is exactly $j\geq \ell _ m$. This gives the formula for $W_ m(c)$, and the inclusion follows from $\ell _ m\geq v_ m$. If $c\leq e$, both point orders are zero.

For an integer $0\leq j\leq km+1$, homogeneous polynomials of degree $km$ realize every jet of total degree less than $j$ at $x$, and there are $\binom {j+q-1}{q}$ such jets. Hence

\[ h^0\bigl (\mathbb P^ q,\mathcal O(km)\otimes \mathfrak m_ x^ j\bigr ) =\binom {km+q}{q}-\binom {j+q-1}{q}. \]

For $e<c<e+k$ we have $0\leq v_ m\leq \ell _ m\leq km$ and $\ell _ m-v_ m\leq \lceil a\rceil $, and subtraction gives the exact defect. Each additional jet degree contributes $O(m^{q-1})$ monomials, which gives the bound, and $v_ m=m(c-e)+O(1)$ gives the leading term of $\dim V_ m(c)$.

At $c=e+k$ we have $v_ m=\max \{ km-\lceil a\rceil ,0\} $. For large $m$, the allowed monomials have vanishing orders $km-\lceil a\rceil ,\ldots ,km$ at $x$, and each of these $\lceil a\rceil +1$ degrees has $k^{q-1}m^{q-1}/(q-1)!+O(m^{q-2})$ monomials, which proves the asymptotic formula. The residual is then $k\gamma $, whose top power vanishes. For $c>e+k$, a nonzero polynomial of degree $km$ vanishes at $x$ to order at most $km$. Hence $V_ m(c)=0$ exactly when $v_ m>km$, which is equivalent to $m(c-e-k)-a\geq 1$. $\square$

For $0<c<e+k$ the reference $\psi _ d$ has positive mass and the defect is $O(m^{q-1})$, so this family admits the approximation asked for in 33.9.2. At $c=e+k$ the reference mass vanishes and the growth degree of the selected spaces drops to $q-1$; beyond this threshold the selected spaces eventually vanish.

27.10.6 A semipositive residual with nonzero defect

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Blowing up two points instead of one, we obtain a residual that is semipositive, cannot be made strictly positive, and still has positive top integral. Unlike the example with one blown-up point, the gap between the two divisorial thresholds now produces a nonzero defect of the largest possible order $m^{q-1}$. Counting the spaces requires the following elementary interpolation of jets at several points.

Lemma 27.10.17. Let $x_1,\ldots ,x_ N$ be distinct points of $\mathbb P^ q$, $q\geq 1$, and let $d,j_1,\ldots ,j_ N$ be nonnegative integers with $\sum _ i j_ i\leq d+1$. Then the map from $H^0(\mathbb P^ q,\mathcal O(d))$ to the jets of order less than $j_ i$ at the points $x_ i$ is surjective. In particular,

\[ h^0\left(\mathbb P^ q,\mathcal O(d)\otimes \bigcap _ i\mathfrak m_{x_ i}^{j_ i}\right) =\binom {d+q}{q}-\sum _ i\binom {j_ i+q-1}{q}, \]

where $\binom {q-1}{q}=0$.

Proof. We may discard the points with $j_ i=0$; if no point remains, the target is zero. Choose an affine chart containing all the remaining points, and use the corresponding trivialization of $\mathcal O(d)$. For each pair $i\ne k$ choose an affine linear function $L_{ik}$ vanishing at $x_ k$ and nonzero at $x_ i$, and set

\[ Q_ i=\prod _{k\ne i}L_{ik}^{j_ k}. \]

Given a jet $b_ i$ at $x_ i$, divide it by the invertible jet of $Q_ i$ at $x_ i$, and let $T_ i$ be the Taylor polynomial of degree at most $j_ i-1$ of the quotient. Then $Q_ iT_ i$ has the jet $b_ i$ at $x_ i$ and vanishes to order at least $j_ k$ at every other point $x_ k$, and its degree is at most $\sum _ kj_ k-1\leq d$. Hence $\sum _ iQ_ iT_ i$, homogenized to degree $d$, realizes all the prescribed jets. In $q$ variables there are $\binom {j_ i+q-1}{q}$ monomials of total degree less than $j_ i$, which gives the dimension formula. $\square$

Example 27.10.18. Let $q\geq 2$ and let $\rho :B\to \mathbb P^ q$ be the blowup of the two points $x_1=[1:0:\cdots :0]$ and $x_2=[0:1:0:\cdots :0]$, with exceptional divisors $E_1,E_2$. Let $\tau _ i:B\to \mathbb P^{q-1}$ be the extension to $B$ of the projection from $x_ i$. With the normalized Fubini–Study forms, put

\[ \alpha =\rho ^*\omega _{\mathbb P^ q},\qquad \gamma _ i=\tau _ i^*\omega _{\mathbb P^{q-1}},\qquad \kappa =\alpha +\gamma _1+\gamma _2>0. \]

Equip $\mathcal O_ B(E_ i)=\rho ^*\mathcal O(1)\otimes \tau _ i^*\mathcal O(-1)$ with the quotient metric, whose curvature is $\eta _ i=\alpha -\gamma _ i$, and let $s_ i$ be its canonical section. Equip

\[ A=\rho ^*\mathcal O(2)\otimes \mathcal O_ B(E_1+E_2), \qquad \theta =2\alpha +\eta _1+\eta _2 \]

with the product metric. On $X=B\times \mathbb P^1$, with projection $\pi $ and a point $o\in \mathbb P^1$, set

\[ \begin{gathered} R_ i=\| \pi ^*s_ i\| ^2,\qquad T=\| s_ o\| ^2,\\ \varphi =\log (R_1^2+T^3)+\log (R_2^2+T^3),\\ \omega =4\pi ^*\kappa +7\omega _{\mathbb P^1},\\ \psi =2\log \| s_1\| ^2+2\log \| s_2\| ^2, \qquad \beta =\gamma _1+\gamma _2. \end{gathered} \]

Then $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(B,\theta )$, and

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\theta (\psi )=\beta ^ q,\qquad \int _ B\beta ^ q=2^ q-2>0. \]

No smooth change of gauge makes $\beta $ strictly positive. Nevertheless, writing $V_ m$ and $W_ m$ for the selected and reference spaces as before, their images in $H^0(B,A^ m)$ are, for every $m\geq 1$,

\[ \begin{aligned} V_ m& =s_1^ ms_2^ m\rho ^*H^0\left(\mathbb P^ q,\mathcal O(2m) \otimes \mathfrak m_{x_1}^{m-1}\mathfrak m_{x_2}^{m-1}\right),\\ W_ m& =s_1^ ms_2^ m\rho ^*H^0\left(\mathbb P^ q,\mathcal O(2m) \otimes \mathfrak m_{x_1}^{m}\mathfrak m_{x_2}^{m}\right). \end{aligned} \]

The defect and the common leading term of the dimensions are

\[ \begin{gathered} \dim V_ m-\dim W_ m=2\binom {m+q-2}{q-1} \sim \frac{2}{(q-1)!}m^{q-1},\\ \dim V_ m=\frac{2^ q-2}{q!}m^ q+O(m^{q-1}),\qquad \dim W_ m=\frac{2^ q-2}{q!}m^ q+O(m^{q-1}). \end{gathered} \]

Proof. The map $(\rho ,\tau _1,\tau _2)$ is an embedding: away from the exceptional divisors $\rho $ is already an embedding, and along $E_ i$ the map $\tau _ i$ separates the tangent directions of $E_ i$ while $\rho $ detects the normal direction. Hence $\kappa >0$. The identity for the quotient metric is the one-point identity of example 27.10.15, applied at each center. For each $i$, the calculation over a common denominator writes $R_ i^2+T^3$ as a sum of squared moduli of holomorphic coefficients over the metric denominator for $\mathcal O(2,3)$. Together with the extension theorem across the pole set, this gives

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq -4\pi ^*\alpha -6\omega _{\mathbb P^1}. \]

It follows that $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq 4\pi ^*\beta +\omega _{\mathbb P^1}\geq 0$.

By the Poincaré–Lelong formula,

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =\beta +2[E_1]+2[E_2]\geq 0. \]

The non-pluripolar product does not charge the divisors and equals $\beta ^ q$. The class of $\beta $ is $2H-[E_1]-[E_2]$, where $H=\rho ^*c_1(\mathcal O(1))$. As in example 27.10.13, $H^ q=1$, the mixed intersection numbers involving $H$ and some $[E_ i]$ vanish, and $[E_ i]^ q=(-1)^{q-1}$; moreover, the two exceptional divisors are disjoint. Expanding gives $\int _ B\beta ^ q=2^ q-2$.

Let $C$ be the strict transform of the line through $x_1$ and $x_2$. Both maps $\tau _ i$ are constant on $C$, so $\int _ C\beta =0$. By Stokes' theorem, $\int _ C(\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)=0$ for every smooth real function $u$, whereas a strictly positive form has positive integral over $C$. This proves the obstruction, although the top integral is positive.

The selecting potential satisfies $\varphi \geq \pi ^*\psi $. Its pole set is the disjoint union of the sets $E_ i\times \{ o\} $. Near either of them the other summand is smooth, and

\[ \varphi =\log (|z|^4+|w|^6)+O(1),\qquad E_ i=\{ z=0\} ,\quad o=\{ w=0\} . \]

Thus $c_ i=p_ i=2$, $b_{i1}=3$ and $a_ i=2/3$. By theorem 27.10.10, corollary 27.10.14, $W_ m=H^0(B,A^ m(-2m(E_1+E_2)))$, and $V_ m$ is contained in $H^0(B,A^ m(-(2m-1)(E_1+E_2)))$; for an integer $\ell $, the strict local integrability condition $\ell +1>2m-2/3$ means $\ell \geq 2m-1$. Conversely, by lemma 27.10.5, divisibility by these orders gives integrability in every chart of the pole set, and off the pole set the selecting potential is locally bounded. This proves equality for $V_ m$.

Dividing a section of $A^ m$ by $s_1^ ms_2^ m$ gives a section of $\mathcal O(2m)$ away from the two centers, which extends across them by Hartogs' theorem since $q\geq 2$. The pullback of the extension, multiplied by $s_1^ ms_2^ m$, agrees with the original section everywhere, by the identity theorem. If the extended section vanishes to order $j_ i$ at $x_ i$, the corresponding section of $A^ m$ vanishes to order $m+j_ i$ generically along $E_ i$. The two divisorial conditions therefore give exactly the vanishing orders at $x_1$ and $x_2$ displayed in the statement. Since the two point ideals have disjoint supports, their product equals their intersection.

Apply lemma 27.10.17 with $d=2m$ and orders $(m,m)$ or $(m-1,m-1)$; in both cases their sum is at most $d+1$. This gives

\[ \begin{aligned} \dim W_ m& =\binom {2m+q}{q}-2\binom {m+q-1}{q},\\ \dim V_ m& =\binom {2m+q}{q}-2\binom {m+q-2}{q}. \end{aligned} \]

Pascal's identity gives the exact defect, and the leading terms give both growth laws. For $m=1$ the vanishing orders for $V_1$ are zero, and the defect is already two. For $q=2$ the dimensions are $\dim W_ m=(m+1)^2$ and $\dim V_ m=m^2+4m+1$. $\square$

In this example the residual $\beta $ has zero integral over the curve $C$, so no smooth change of gauge makes it positive, and yet its top integral is positive. Thus the bound $O(m^{q-1})$ of corollary 27.10.14 cannot be improved to $o(m^{q-1})$ for semipositive residuals either. As in the previous examples, the selecting potential is finite on most of each fiber over $E_ i$ and equals $-\infty $ at the point $o$ of that fiber, so it does not descend, even up to a bounded function.

27.11 Horizontal poles over projective space

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

When the base divisor is a hyperplane in projective space, the descended vanishing conditions of theorem 27.10.2 turn the kernels into binomial tail probabilities. The large-deviation rate of these tails is the domination envelope of the original singularity upstairs, and the concentration of evaluation sections identifies the limiting measure for every smooth integration volume. In this setting the weak limit of corollary 27.10.4 improves to convergence in total variation.

We specialize section 27.10 to $B=\mathbb P^ q$ with homogeneous coordinates $[Z_0:\cdots :Z_ q]$, $A=\mathcal O_ B(k)$ and $Z=\{ Z_0=0\} $, where $q,r,k$ are positive integers and $0<c<k$.
