Let $\omega _ B$ be the Fubini–Study form on $B$, normalized by $\int _ B\omega _ B^ q=1$, and give $A$ the Fubini–Study metric, whose curvature is $\theta =k\omega _ B$. We keep the submersion $\pi :X\to B$ with connected $r$-dimensional fibers, and the selecting potential $\varphi $ with the singularity (27.33) along the section $Y$ over $Z$. Write

\[ R=\frac{|Z_0|^2}{\sum _{i=0}^ q|Z_ i|^2},\qquad a=\frac ck,\qquad D_ c=\{ R\geq a\} ,\qquad C_ c=\frac{(k-c)^ q}{q!}. \]

Fix a smooth positive probability volume $\nu _ X$ on $X$. Fiber integration gives $\nu _ B=\pi _*\nu _ X=w\, \omega _ B^ q$ with a smooth function $w>0$; put $M=\max _ Bw$. Let $B_ m^ B$ be the kernel of the descended space $V_ m$ for the norm on $B$ with zero continuous weight and volume $\nu _ B$, and let $B_ m^ X$ be the kernel of the pulled-back space for the norm on $X$ with zero continuous weight and volume $\nu _ X$. We shall see that $B_ m^ X=B_ m^ B\circ \pi $.

The selecting potential $\varphi $ need not be $\pi ^*\theta $-plurisubharmonic. Its domination envelope in this curvature background is

\[ \mathcal P(\varphi )= \left(\sup \{ u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\pi ^*\theta ):u\leq 0,\ u\preceq \varphi \} \right)^*, \]

where $u\preceq \varphi $ means that $u\leq \varphi +C$ for some constant $C$ (definition 1.12.1). The limit will be described by the function of $R$ defined by

27.50
\begin{equation} u_ c(R)= \begin{cases} -k\left(a\log \dfrac aR+(1-a)\log \dfrac {1-a}{1-R}\right),& 0<R<a,\\ 0,& a\leq R\leq 1,\\ -\infty ,& R=0. \end{cases} \end{equation}

Theorem 27.11.1. The envelope satisfies $\mathcal P(\varphi )=u_ c\circ \pi $. With $\ell _ m=\lfloor mc\rfloor $ and $t_ m=\max \{ \ell _ m-r,0\} $,

\[ N_ m=\dim V_ m=\binom {km-t_ m+q}{q} =C_ cm^ q+O(m^{q-1}). \]

Thus the section spaces grow like $m^ q$, and $q<\dim X$. For every $m\geq 1$, in the extended-value sense,

27.54
\begin{equation} \frac1m\log B_ m^ X\geq \mathcal P(\varphi )-\frac{\log M}{m}. \end{equation}

The functions $m^{-1}\log B_ m^ X$ converge to $\mathcal P(\varphi )$ in $L^1$ for every smooth positive volume on $X$, and locally uniformly on $X\setminus \pi ^{-1}(Z)$. In the normalization of section 27.1, the kernel measures converge in total variation:

27.55
\begin{equation} \begin{split} \frac{q!}{m^ q}B_ m^ B\nu _ B& \longrightarrow \mu _ c:=\mathbf1_{D_ c}\theta ^ q,\\ \frac{q!}{m^ q}B_ m^ X\nu _ X& \longrightarrow \Lambda _{\nu _ X}:=\frac{k^ q\mathbf1_{D_ c}\circ \pi }{w\circ \pi }\nu _ X. \end{split} \end{equation}

Both limits have mass $(k-c)^ q$, and $\pi _*\Lambda _{\nu _ X}=\mu _ c$. Dividing the unnormalized kernel measures by $N_ m$ instead gives the probability limits $\mu _ c/(k-c)^ q$ and $\Lambda _{\nu _ X}/(k-c)^ q$, while normalization by $m^{q+r}$ gives zero in total variation.

Let $W_ m$ be the reference spaces of theorem 27.10.2. For every sequence of intermediate spaces $W_ m\subseteq U_ m\subseteq V_ m$, equipped with the restricted norms, the lower bound and all these convergences remain valid. The dimensions of these spaces are $C_ cm^ q+O(m^{q-1})$, and the dimension normalization then uses $\dim U_ m$.

27.11.1 The domination envelope

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We first compute the envelope on the base, using the affine chart $Z_0\ne 0$. Set $z_ i=Z_ i/Z_0$, $s=|z|^2$, $l=k-c$, $s_0=l/c$ and $C=k\log k-c\log c-l\log l$. In this chart the reference weight of $A$ is $\phi _ A=k\log (1+s)$. Put

\[ F_0(z)= \begin{cases} k\log (1+s),& s\leq s_0,\\ l\log s+C,& s\geq s_0. \end{cases} \]

As a function of $\log s$, $F_0$ is increasing and convex: its derivative increases to $l$ and then stays constant. Hence $F_0$ is plurisubharmonic. The two branches match to first order at $s=s_0$, and the tangent-line inequality gives $F_0\leq \phi _ A$. On a chart $Z_ j\ne 0$ with $j\geq 1$, write $x=Z_0/Z_ j$ and denote the remaining ratios by $\zeta $. Near $Z$ the corresponding weight is

\[ F_ j=c\log |x|^2+l\log (1+|\zeta |^2)+C. \]

Therefore $F_0$ and the $F_ j$ define a global plurisubharmonic metric weight $F$ on $A$, with a divisor pole of coefficient $c$ along $Z$ and a smooth remainder.

Let $v\leq \phi _ A$ be a competing plurisubharmonic weight, that is, $v=\phi _ A+u$ with $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(B,\theta )$ and $u\leq 0$, and suppose that $v\leq c\log |x|^2+O(1)$ near $Z$. Write $v_0$ for its expression in the chart $Z_0\neq 0$. The obstacle gives $v_0\leq F_0$ on $\{ s\leq s_0\} $. On each complex line $z=t\xi $ with $|\xi |=1$, either $v_0(t\xi )\equiv -\infty $, or $v_0(t\xi )-l\log |t|^2$ is subharmonic on $|t|>\sqrt{s_0}$ and bounded above near infinity. In the coordinate $1/t$ the puncture at infinity is then removable, and the maximum principle bounds this difference by its boundary bound $C$. Varying $\xi $ proves $v\leq F$ on $B$. Consequently $F=\phi _ A+u_ c$ is the largest such weight, and its contact set $\{ F=\phi _ A\} $ is $D_ c$.

We now return to $X$. By proposition 1.22.3, every $\pi ^*\theta $-plurisubharmonic function has the form $u\circ \pi $ for a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function $u$ on $B$. Restriction to the local section $w_1=\cdots =w_ r=0$ shows that $u\circ \pi \preceq \varphi $ implies $u\leq c\log |x|^2+O(1)$ near $Z$. Conversely, this divisorial bound and the inequality $|x|^2\leq |x|^2+\sum _ j|w_ j|^2$ give the domination $u\circ \pi \leq \varphi +O(1)$ near $Y$. A finite cover of $Y$ gives one constant there. On the compact complement of a smaller neighborhood of $Y$, the function $\varphi $ is bounded below and $u\leq 0$, so one constant works on all of $X$. The two competitor families therefore correspond to each other, which proves $\mathcal P(\varphi )=u_ c\circ \pi $. The pole of this envelope fills $\pi ^{-1}(Z)$, although $\varphi $ is finite on $\pi ^{-1}(Z)\setminus Y$.

27.11.2 Binomial kernels

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For a fixed integer $j\geq 0$, set $d=km$, $t_{m,j}=\max \{ \ell _ m-j,0\} $ and $\mathcal H_ m^{(j)}=H^0(B,\mathcal O(d)(-t_{m,j}Z))$. The choices $j=0$ and $j=r$ give $W_ m$ and $V_ m$. Give these spaces the reference norm defined by the Fubini–Study metric, zero continuous weight and the volume $\nu _0=\omega _ B^ q$. For a binomial random variable $\operatorname {Bin}(d,R)$ with $d$ trials and success probability $R$, their kernels are

27.60
\begin{equation} B_{m,j}^0(R)=A_ dQ_{m,j}(R),\qquad A_ d=\binom {d+q}{q},\qquad Q_{m,j}(R)=\mathbb P\{ \operatorname {Bin}(d,R)\geq t_{m,j}\} . \end{equation}

To see this, apply the monomial norm formula (27.30) in degree $d$. Invariance under the coordinate circle actions makes the monomials orthogonal, and the selection keeps exactly those in which $Z_0$ has exponent at least $t_{m,j}$. Summing over the other exponents with the multinomial identity gives (27.60). Counting the selected monomials gives $\dim \mathcal H_ m^{(j)}=\binom {d-t_{m,j}+q}{q}=C_ cm^ q+O(m^{q-1})$.

We next bound the tails from below. Write $D(v\mid R)=v\log (v/R)+(1-v)\log ((1-v)/(1-R))$ for the relative entropy, and put $b=\lfloor da\rfloor =\ell _ m$. If $R\geq b/d$, the binomial mode $\min \{ d,\lfloor (d+1)R\rfloor \} $ is at least $b$. Since $t_{m,j}\leq b$, the tail above $t_{m,j}$ contains this mode and is therefore at least $1/(d+1)$. If $0<R<b/d$, the mass at $b$ for the parameter $v=b/d$ is a largest mass, hence at least $1/(d+1)$. For the parameter $R$, the same mass is at least $(d+1)^{-1}\exp (-dD(v\mid R))$. Since

\[ \frac{\partial D(v\mid R)}{\partial v} =\log \frac{v(1-R)}{R(1-v)}\geq 0\quad (v\geq R) \]

and $v\leq a$, it is at least $(d+1)^{-1}\exp (-dD(a\mid R))$. Together with $u_ c\leq 0$, these cases give $Q_{m,j}\geq (d+1)^{-1}\exp (mu_ c)$, including the cases $b=0$ and $R=1$. Since $A_ d\geq d+1$, the reference kernels satisfy $m^{-1}\log B_{m,j}^0\geq u_ c$; at $R=0$ this holds in the extended sense.

For the matching upper asymptotics, fix $0<R<a$. Stirling's formula at the first selected index $t_{m,j}$ gives the logarithmic rate $-kD(a\mid R)$. Since $t_{m,j}/d\to a>R$, the ratios of successive binomial masses above that index are eventually bounded by a number less than one, so the whole tail has the same logarithmic rate. For $R>a$, the binomial variance estimate gives $Q_{m,j}\to 1$. At $R=a$, the lower bound by the mode and $Q_{m,j}\leq 1$ give the logarithmic limit zero. The functions $m^{-1}\log Q_{m,j}(R)$ increase with $R$, and their limit $u_ c$ is continuous on each interval $[\delta ,1]$ with $\delta >0$. On a finite partition of such an interval on which the oscillation of $u_ c$ is small, monotonicity upgrades pointwise convergence to uniform convergence.

The event that the first $t_{m,j}$ trials succeed gives

\[ c\log R\leq \frac{t_{m,j}}m\log R \leq \frac1m\log Q_{m,j}(R)\leq 0. \]

Since $\log R$ is integrable against every smooth volume, dominated convergence proves global $L^1$ convergence of the functions $m^{-1}\log Q_{m,j}$, and the additional term $m^{-1}\log A_ d$ tends to zero. For the measures, the variance estimate gives $Q_{m,j}\to \mathbf1_{D_ c}$ away from $R=a$. The pushforward of $\nu _0$ by $R$ is $q(1-R)^{q-1}\, \mathrm{d}R$, by the radial computation in the proof of theorem 27.9.2 applied to $1-R$. In particular the level set $\{ R=a\} $ has zero volume. Since $q!A_ d/m^ q\to k^ q$ and these constants are bounded, dominated convergence proves total-variation convergence of $q!m^{-q}B_{m,j}^0\nu _0$ to $\mu _ c$, and

\[ \mu _ c(B)=k^ q\int _ a^1q(1-R)^{q-1}\, \mathrm{d}R=(k-c)^ q. \]

27.11.3 Changing the integration volume

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Put $\delta =\min _ Bw>0$, and write $B_{m,j}^ w$ for the kernel of $\mathcal H_ m^{(j)}$ with volume $w\nu _0$. Comparing the two norms gives

\[ M^{-1}B_{m,j}^0\leq B_{m,j}^ w\leq \delta ^{-1}B_{m,j}^0. \]

Together with the preceding subsection, this proves the lower bound (27.54) on the base and the convergence of the functions $m^{-1}\log B_{m,j}^ w$. Comparability alone does not identify the measure limit, however.

Let $\Pi _ m$ be the orthogonal projection onto $\mathcal H_ m^{(j)}$ for the reference $L^2$ norm. In a unit frame at $x$, write $F_{m,x}$ for the evaluation vector of $H^0(B,\mathcal O(d))$ at $x$, and put $K_{m,x}=\Pi _ mF_{m,x}$. Formula (27.60) gives

\[ \begin{gathered} \| F_{m,x}\| _0^2=A_ d,\qquad \| K_{m,x}\| _0^2=A_ dQ_{m,j}(R(x)),\\ \| F_{m,x}-K_{m,x}\| _0^2=A_ d(1-Q_{m,j}(R(x))). \end{gathered} \]

The full evaluation section satisfies

\[ |F_{m,x}(y)|_{h_ A^ m}^2=A_ d^2\tau (x,y)^ d,\qquad \tau ([Z],[W])=\frac{|\langle Z,W\rangle |^2}{|Z|^2|W|^2}. \]

Hence $A_ d^{-1}|F_{m,x}|^2\nu _0$ is a probability measure concentrating at $x$: outside any neighborhood of $x$ we have $\tau \leq \rho $ for some $\rho <1$, so its mass there is at most $A_ d\rho ^ d\to 0$. Continuity of $w$ therefore gives $\| (w-w(x))F_{m,x}\| _0=o(\sqrt{A_ d})$ for each fixed $x$.

On $\mathcal H_ m^{(j)}$, let $T_ ms=\Pi _ m(ws)$. The quadratic form of $T_ m$ is the weighted norm, so $\delta I\leq T_ m\leq MI$, and

\[ B_{m,j}^ w(x)=\langle T_ m^{-1}K_{m,x},K_{m,x}\rangle _0. \]

For $R(x)>a$, the preceding norm identities give $\| F_{m,x}-K_{m,x}\| _0=o(\sqrt{A_ d})$. Since $(T_ m-w(x)I)K_{m,x}=\Pi _ m\bigl ((w-w(x))K_{m,x}\bigr )$, it follows that $\| (T_ m-w(x)I)K_{m,x}\| _0=o(\sqrt{A_ d})$. Using $\| T_ m^{-1}\| \leq \delta ^{-1}$, we obtain

\[ B_{m,j}^ w(x)=w(x)^{-1}B_{m,j}^0(x)+o(A_ d) \qquad (R(x)>a). \]

For $R(x)<a$, the upper comparison of kernels gives $m^{-q}B_{m,j}^ w(x)\to 0$. Finally, $0\leq q!m^{-q}wB_{m,j}^ w\leq (M/\delta )q!A_ d/m^ q$ is uniformly bounded. Dominated convergence away from the null set $\{ R=a\} $ proves that $q!m^{-q}B_{m,j}^ w w\nu _0\to \mu _ c$ in total variation.

Proof of theorem 27.11.1. The identification of the selected spaces is theorem 27.10.2, and the dimension formula follows from the monomial count. The envelope computation and the kernel calculations for the two endpoint spaces $W_ m,V_ m$ prove the assertions on the base. Isometric descent gives $B_ m^ X=B_ m^ B\circ \pi $. For $f_ c=k^ q\mathbf1_{D_ c}/w$, fiber integration gives

\[ \int _ X\left|\frac{q!}{m^ q}B_ m^ X-f_ c\circ \pi \right|\nu _ X =\int _ B\left|\frac{q!}{m^ q}B_ m^ B-f_ c\right|\nu _ B. \]

This proves the total-variation limit on $X$ and the identity $\pi _*\Lambda _{\nu _ X}=\mu _ c$. The same fiber integration transfers the $L^1$ convergence, and every other smooth positive volume on the compact manifold $X$ is comparable to $\nu _ X$. Local uniform convergence pulls back to $X\setminus \pi ^{-1}(Z)$. The dimension asymptotics give the probability limits, and $N_ m/m^{q+r}=O(m^{-r})$ proves the zero limit in the ambient degree.

For $W_ m\subseteq U_ m\subseteq V_ m$, monotonicity of the kernels gives $B_{W,m}\leq B_{U,m}\leq B_{V,m}$. By lemma 27.8.1, the positive difference of the $q$-normalized $U_ m$ and $W_ m$ measures has total mass at most $q!(\dim V_ m-\dim W_ m)/m^ q=O(m^{-1})$. The lower bound and the local uniform convergence for $U_ m$ follow from those for the two endpoint spaces by sandwiching. The $L^1$ errors of the endpoint functions bound the error of the intermediate one, which proves global convergence as well. $\square$

The limit on the base is independent of $\nu _ X$, but its lift is not. If $\nu _ X(\mathrm{d}b,\mathrm{d}x)=\nu _ B(\mathrm{d}b)\lambda _ b(\mathrm{d}x)$ is the disintegration of $\nu _ X$ along the fibers, then $\Lambda _{\nu _ X}(\mathrm{d}b,\mathrm{d}x)=\mu _ c(\mathrm{d}b)\lambda _ b(\mathrm{d}x)$. Thus the lifted limit keeps exactly the conditional fiber probabilities over $D_ c$. Comparing with corollary 27.10.4 for $v=0$, we see that $\mu _ c=\mathrm{MA}_\theta (E(0))$, so $\Lambda _{\nu _ X}$ is the quotient equilibrium measure of section 27.7.1 for the fiber probabilities $\lambda _ b$. Conversely, equality of two lifts implies equality of these probabilities for $\mu _ c$-almost every $b$: test against $(g\circ \pi )f$, with $g\in C(B)$ and $f$ in a countable dense subset of $C(X)$, and take one common exceptional null set. The reverse implication follows directly by integration.

This dependence occurs in the product example of example 27.10.3. Keep its selecting potential $\varphi =c\log (R_ B+R_ F)$ and its integer $b\geq c$, and let $\eta _ f=k\omega _ B+bf\omega _ F$, where $f>0$ is a smooth function on $\mathbb P^ r$ with $b\min f>c$. Then $\eta _ f+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq (k-c)\omega _ B+(bf-c)\omega _ F>0$. The normalized volume of $\eta _ f$ and the corresponding limit are

\[ \nu _{X,f}=\omega _ B^ q\otimes \frac{f^ r\omega _ F^ r}{\int _{\mathbb P^ r}f^ r\omega _ F^ r}, \qquad \Lambda _{\nu _{X,f}}=\mu _ c\otimes \frac{f^ r\omega _ F^ r}{\int _{\mathbb P^ r}f^ r\omega _ F^ r}. \]

A nonconstant $f$ changes the lift. For $r\geq 2$, it also makes $\eta _ f$ nonclosed: $\mathrm{d}\eta _ f=b\, \mathrm{d}f\wedge \omega _ F$, and wedging with $\omega _ F^{r-2}$ reduces the vanishing of this form to the injectivity of $\xi \mapsto \xi \wedge \omega _ F^{r-1}$ on one-forms. For $r=1$ this derivative vanishes for degree reasons.

The spaces, the envelope and the kernels do not change when the admissible Hermitian background varies while $\pi ,A,h_ A,\varphi $ and $\nu _ X$ stay fixed. Moving the fiber point of $Y=Z\times \{ [1:0:\cdots :0]\} $ to another point of $\mathbb P^ r$ does not change them either. For $mc\geq r+1$, however, the constant germ fails to be integrable at the old center and becomes integrable there after the move. Thus the kernels in all degrees together need not determine the multiplier-ideal type of the selecting potential.

27.12 Degenerate curvature and growing vanishing orders

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

In the compact models above, the vanishing orders are proportional to the tensor degree. To see the transition when the order grows more slowly, we pass to a model space of entire functions. There the local metric fixes the scale of the full kernel, while the selecting ideal creates a second, larger radius. Grouping the monomials according to the residues of their degrees expresses the transition at that radius through Poisson tails.

In this section $r$ and $q$ denote new integers. Fix an integer $r\geq 1$. On $\mathbb C$, with coordinate $z=x+\mathrm{i}y$ and area measure $\mathrm{d}A=\mathrm{d}x\, \mathrm{d}y$, put $\phi (z)=|z|^{2r}$. For a real $m>0$, let $H_{m,r}$ be the Hilbert space of entire functions with norm

\[ \| f\| _{m,r}^2=\int _{\mathbb C}|f|^2\mathrm{e}^{-m\phi }\, \mathrm{d}A. \]

For an integer $q\geq 0$, let $H_{m,r}^{(q)}$ consist of the functions vanishing to order at least $q$ at zero. Since the weight is bounded below on each fixed disk, the local submean and Cauchy inequalities bound point and jet evaluations, so this subspace is closed. Write $K_{m,r}^{(q)}(z,w)$ for its reproducing kernel, holomorphic in $z$ and antiholomorphic in $w$, and put

\[ B_{m,r}^{(q)}(z)=\mathrm{e}^{-m\phi (z)}K_{m,r}^{(q)}(z,z). \]

Thus the ideal $(z^ q)$ selects the functions, while the smooth weight determines the norm. We use $\Gamma (a)=\int _0^\infty t^{a-1}\mathrm{e}^{-t}\, \mathrm{d}t$ for $a>0$.

Proposition 27.12.1. For every $m>0$ and integer $q\geq 0$,

27.61
\begin{equation} K_{m,r}^{(q)}(z,w)=\frac r\pi \sum _{k=q}^\infty \frac{m^{(k+1)/r}(z\bar w)^ k}{\Gamma ((k+1)/r)}. \end{equation}

The series and all its derivatives converge uniformly on compact sets. For $\zeta \in \mathbb C$,

27.62
\begin{equation} m^{-1/r}B_{m,r}^{(q)}(m^{-1/(2r)}\zeta ) =\frac r\pi \mathrm{e}^{-|\zeta |^{2r}} \sum _{k=q}^\infty \frac{|\zeta |^{2k}}{\Gamma ((k+1)/r)}. \end{equation}

Consequently, if $m$ tends to infinity through positive integers and $q=q_ m\to \infty $, the left side tends to zero uniformly on compact $\zeta $-sets.

Proof. The monomials are orthogonal by rotational invariance. For every integer $k\geq 0$, polar integration with radial variable $\rho $ and the substitution $t=m\rho ^{2r}$ give

\[ \| z^ k\| _{m,r}^2=2\pi \int _0^\infty \rho ^{2k+1}\mathrm{e}^{-m\rho ^{2r}}\, \mathrm{d}\rho =\frac\pi r m^{-(k+1)/r}\Gamma ((k+1)/r). \]

For an entire function $f(z)=\sum _{k\geq 0}a_ kz^ k$, Parseval's identity on circles and Tonelli's theorem yield $\| f\| _{m,r}^2=\sum _{k\geq 0}|a_ k|^2\| z^ k\| _{m,r}^2$. Thus the normalized monomials form an orthonormal basis, and those with $k\geq q$ form an orthonormal basis of the selected subspace. This proves (27.61). By Stirling's formula, the reciprocal Gamma coefficients decay faster than every fixed geometric progression, even after multiplication by any fixed power of $k$; this gives uniform convergence on compact sets together with all derivatives. Substituting $z=w=m^{-1/(2r)}\zeta $ gives (27.62). On a disk $|\zeta |\leq R$ of fixed radius, the series in (27.62) is bounded by the tail, from the index $q$ on, of the convergent series with $|\zeta |$ replaced by $R$. This tail tends to zero as $q\to \infty $, proving the last assertion. $\square$

For $r=1$ and $q=0$ the density is the constant $m/\pi $. For general $r$ and $q=0$ its value at zero is $rm^{1/r}/(\pi \Gamma (1/r))$, whereas it vanishes at zero when $q>0$. The scale $m^{-1/(2r)}$ therefore describes fixed vanishing orders, but a growing order requires a further rescaling.

27.12.1 A uniform tail estimate

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Grouping the monomial degrees by their residues modulo $r$ expresses the kernel through weighted Poisson tails. The estimate of Gamma ratios below removes those weights, with an error independent of the vanishing threshold.

Write $\mathbb P$ and $\mathbb E$ for probability and expectation. For $T>0$, let $N_ T$ be a Poisson random variable with parameter $T$, so that $\mathbb P(N_ T=\ell )=\mathrm{e}^{-T}T^\ell /\ell !$ for integers $\ell \geq 0$. For a real $0<\alpha \leq 1$, define

\[ w_{\alpha ,T}(\ell )=T^{\alpha -1}\frac{\Gamma (\ell +1)}{\Gamma (\ell +\alpha )}. \]

All constants in the next lemma may depend on $\alpha $, but not on $T$.

Lemma 27.12.2. There is a constant $C_\alpha >0$ such that, for $T\geq 1$,

\[ \mathbb E\bigl |w_{\alpha ,T}(N_ T)-1\bigr |\leq C_\alpha T^{-1/2}. \]

Proof. Recall that $\mathbb E N_ T=T$ and $\operatorname {Var}(N_ T)=T$. Stirling's formula, applied to the ratio of Gamma functions, gives

\[ \begin{aligned} \frac{\Gamma (\ell +1)}{\Gamma (\ell +\alpha )} & =\ell ^{1-\alpha }(1+O_\alpha (\ell ^{-1}))& & \quad (\ell \to \infty ),\\ \frac{\Gamma (\ell +1)}{\Gamma (\ell +\alpha )} & \leq C_\alpha (\ell +1)^{1-\alpha }& & \quad (\ell \geq 0). \end{aligned} \]

The second bound follows from the first after the constant is increased to account for finitely many small integers. Since $0\leq 1-\alpha <1$, it implies

\[ w_{\alpha ,T}(\ell )\leq C_\alpha \left(1+\frac{\ell +1}{T}\right), \qquad \mathbb E(w_{\alpha ,T}(N_ T)+1)^2\leq C_\alpha \quad (T\geq 1). \]

For $T\geq 2$, consider the event $E_ T=\{ |N_ T-T|\leq T/2\} $. On this event, the expansion of the Gamma ratio and the bounded derivative of $s\mapsto s^{1-\alpha }$ on $[1/2,3/2]$ give

\[ |w_{\alpha ,T}(N_ T)-1| \leq C_\alpha \left(\frac{|N_ T-T|}{T}+\frac1T\right). \]

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, its expectation is at most $C_\alpha T^{-1/2}$. On the complement, Chebyshev's inequality gives $\mathbb P(E_ T^ c)\leq 4/T$, and the preceding second-moment bound together with the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives the same estimate for the contribution of $E_ T^ c$. Enlarging the constant handles $1\leq T\leq 2$. $\square$

For integers $q\geq 0$ and $0\leq s<r$, put

\[ L_ s(q)=\left\lceil \frac{q-s}{r}\right\rceil \geq 0. \]

If a monomial degree is written as $k=r\ell +s$, the condition $k\geq q$ becomes $\ell \geq L_ s(q)$.

Proposition 27.12.3. There is a constant $C_ r>0$, depending only on $r$, such that for $m>0$, $z\ne 0$ and $T=m|z|^{2r}\geq 1$,

27.65
\begin{equation} \left|\frac{\pi B_{m,r}^{(q)}(z)}{mr^2|z|^{2r-2}} -\frac1r\sum _{s=0}^{r-1}\mathbb P\{ N_ T\geq L_ s(q)\} \right| \leq C_ r T^{-1/2} \quad (q\geq 0). \end{equation}

In particular, the error is uniform in the vanishing order $q$.

Proof. Let $\rho =|z|>0$ and put $\alpha _ s=(s+1)/r$ for integers $0\leq s<r$. Grouping the positive series (27.61) by the residues $s$ and using $T=m\rho ^{2r}$, we obtain the exact identity

\[ \begin{split} B_{m,r}^{(q)}(z) & =\frac r{\pi \rho ^2}\sum _{s=0}^{r-1} \mathrm{e}^{-T}\sum _{\ell \geq L_ s(q)} \frac{T^{\ell +\alpha _ s}}{\Gamma (\ell +\alpha _ s)}\\ & =\frac{rT}{\pi \rho ^2}\sum _{s=0}^{r-1} \mathbb E\bigl [w_{\alpha _ s,T}(N_ T) \mathbf1_{\{ N_ T\geq L_ s(q)\} }\bigr ]. \end{split} \]

Divide by $mr^2\rho ^{2r-2}/\pi =r^2T/(\pi \rho ^2)$ and apply lemma 27.12.2 to each of the finitely many residues. Since the indicators are bounded by one independently of their thresholds, the resulting error is uniform in $q$. $\square$

27.12.2 The limiting envelope and the transition scale

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A vanishing order proportional to $m$ removes the kernel density inside a fixed circle. Inside that circle the limit of the potentials is logarithmic, and outside it agrees with the smooth weight. Throughout this subsection, $m$ tends to infinity through positive integers. For $\lambda >0$, set $q_ m=\lfloor m\lambda \rfloor $ and $R_\lambda =(\lambda /r)^{1/(2r)}$. Define

\[ V_\lambda (z)= \begin{cases} |z|^{2r},& |z|\geq R_\lambda ,\\ \lambda \log (|z|^2/R_\lambda ^2)+\lambda /r,& 0<|z|<R_\lambda ,\\ -\infty ,& z=0. \end{cases} \]

In the following theorem, $\delta _0$ denotes the unit point mass at zero. Recall that in dimension one the non-pluripolar measure of a subharmonic function discards the mass carried by its polar set.

Theorem 27.12.4. One has

\[ \frac1m\log K_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}(z,z)\longrightarrow V_\lambda (z) \]

pointwise with extended values and in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb C)$. The kernel measures converge locally in total variation:

27.71
\begin{equation} \frac1m B_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}\, \mathrm{d}A \longrightarrow \mathbf1_{\{ |z|>R_\lambda \} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi . \end{equation}

The function $V_\lambda $ is the largest subharmonic function $v$ on $\mathbb C$ such that $v\leq \phi $ and $v(z)\leq \lambda \log |z|^2+C$ near zero for some constant $C$. Its curvature current is

27.72
\begin{equation} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_\lambda =\lambda \delta _0+ \mathbf1_{\{ |z|>R_\lambda \} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi . \end{equation}

Thus the limit in (27.71) is the non-pluripolar measure of this envelope, rather than its full distributional Laplacian.

Proof. For fixed $z\ne 0$, put $\rho =|z|$ and $T=m\rho ^{2r}$. Then $L_ s(q_ m)/T\to \lambda /(r\rho ^{2r})$, and this limit differs from one when $\rho \neq R_\lambda $. Chebyshev's inequality and (27.65) therefore give

27.73
\begin{equation} \frac1m B_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}(z)\longrightarrow \begin{cases} 0,& 0<\rho <R_\lambda ,\\ r^2\rho ^{2r-2}/\pi ,& \rho >R_\lambda . \end{cases} \end{equation}

The selected density is at most the full density, that is, the density with $q=0$. When $T\geq 1$, the same comparison with $q=0$ bounds it by $C_ r m\rho ^{2r-2}$; when $T\leq 1$, (27.62) with $q=0$ bounds it by $C_ r m^{1/r}\leq C_ r m$. Thus $m^{-1}B_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}$ is uniformly bounded on each compact set. Since the circle $\rho =R_\lambda $ and the origin have zero area, dominated convergence and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi =(r^2/\pi )|z|^{2r-2}\mathrm{d}A$ prove (27.71).

To compute the limit of the potentials inside the circle, fix $0<\rho <R_\lambda $ and write

\[ d_{m,k}=\frac{m^{(k+1)/r}\rho ^{2k}}{\Gamma ((k+1)/r)} \quad (k\geq q_ m). \]

The estimate of Gamma ratios from Stirling's formula gives

\[ \frac{d_{m,k+1}}{d_{m,k}} \leq \left(\frac{rm\rho ^{2r}}{q_ m+1}\right)^{1/r} \left(1+\frac{C_ r}{q_ m+1}\right) \quad (k\geq q_ m) \]

for all sufficiently large $m$. The right side tends to $(r\rho ^{2r}/\lambda )^{1/r}<1$. Hence the sum of these terms lies between $d_{m,q_ m}$ and a fixed multiple of that term, and Stirling's formula yields

\[ \frac1m\log K_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}(z,z) \longrightarrow \lambda \log \rho ^2+ \frac\lambda r\bigl (1-\log (\lambda /r)\bigr ) =V_\lambda (z). \]

For $\rho >R_\lambda $, the limit of the densities in (27.73) is positive. Hence $m^{-1}\log B_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}(z)\to 0$, and therefore $m^{-1}\log K_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}(z,z)$ tends to $\phi (z)$.

At $\rho =R_\lambda $, put $k_ m=\max \{ q_ m,\lfloor rm\rho ^{2r}\rfloor \} $. Then $k_ m/m\to r\rho ^{2r}=\lambda $. The single monomial of degree $k_ m$ and Stirling's formula give $\liminf m^{-1}\log K_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}(z,z)\geq \phi (z)$, and the bound for the full kernel already proved gives the reverse inequality for the upper limit. At zero the selected kernel vanishes for all sufficiently large $m$. This proves pointwise convergence everywhere.

For local $L^1$ convergence, the bounds for the full kernel give a uniform upper bound for $m^{-1}\log K_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}(z,z)$ on each compact set. The single selected monomial of degree $q_ m$ gives the lower bound

\[ \frac1m\log K_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}(z,z) \geq c_ m+\frac{q_ m}{m}\log |z|^2, \]

where $c_ m$ is bounded as $m\to \infty $, again by Stirling's formula. Since $0\leq q_ m/m\leq \lambda $, the negative part on a fixed compact set is bounded by a constant plus $2\lambda |\log |z||$ near zero, which is locally integrable. Dominated convergence proves the $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ assertion.

We turn to the envelope. The two radial expressions for $V_\lambda $ and their first radial derivatives agree at $R_\lambda $, because $rR_\lambda ^{2r}=\lambda $. Away from zero and that circle, direct differentiation proves (27.72). Since the first derivatives match, the circle carries no measure, and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log |z|^2=\delta _0$ gives the atom at zero. The right side is positive, so $V_\lambda $ is subharmonic. For $t=|z|^{2r}/(\lambda /r)<1$, the inequality $\log t+1\leq t$ proves $V_\lambda \leq \phi $ inside the circle; outside it there is equality.

Finally, let $v$ be subharmonic with $v\leq \phi $ and $v\leq \lambda \log |z|^2+C$ near zero. On the punctured disk $0<|z|<R_\lambda $, the function $v-\lambda \log |z|^2$ is subharmonic and bounded above near zero, so it extends subharmonically across zero. On the boundary circle it is at most $\lambda /r-\lambda \log R_\lambda ^2$, since $v\leq \phi =\lambda /r$ there. The maximum principle gives $v\leq V_\lambda $ inside the disk, and outside the disk this follows from $v\leq \phi $. Since $V_\lambda $ itself satisfies the conditions, it is the largest such function. Removing the atom at the pole gives the last assertion. $\square$

The measures in this theorem have infinite total mass on $\mathbb C$, so their convergence is asserted only on compact sets. The weighted potentials $m^{-1}\log B_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}$ converge to $V_\lambda -\phi $. The radius $R_\lambda $ is created by the selecting ideal even when $r=1$, whereas $r>1$ also reflects the curvature zero at the origin.

For any integer sequence $q_ m\to \infty $, the transition at the boundary circle is governed by the central limit theorem for the same Poisson tails. Let $\Phi $ be the standard Gaussian distribution function,

\[ \Phi (\tau )=\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi }}\int _{-\infty }^{\tau } \mathrm{e}^{-s^2/2}\, \mathrm{d}s \quad (\tau \in \mathbb R). \]

Proposition 27.12.5. Fix a real $\tau $. Choose $z_ m\ne 0$ with

\[ m|z_ m|^{2r}=\frac{q_ m}{r}+\tau \sqrt{\frac{q_ m}{r}}, \]

which is positive for all sufficiently large $m$. Then

\[ \frac{\pi B_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}(z_ m)}{mr^2|z_ m|^{2r-2}} \longrightarrow \Phi (\tau ). \]

In particular, this applies both when $q_ m/m\to \lambda >0$ and when $q_ m/m\to 0$.

Proof. We first recall the central limit theorem for Poisson variables in the form used here. For a fixed real $M>0$, Stirling's formula for $\ell !$ gives, uniformly over integers $\ell $ with $|\ell -T|\leq M\sqrt T$,

\[ \mathbb P(N_ T=\ell )=\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi T}} \exp \left(-\frac{(\ell -T)^2}{2T}\right) (1+O_ M(T^{-1/2}))\quad (T\to \infty ). \]

Indeed, insert the expansion of the factorial in $\mathrm{e}^{-T}T^\ell /\ell !$ and expand the logarithm in powers of $(\ell -T)/T$; the cubic remainder is $O_ M(T^{-1/2})$. Riemann sums over bounded intervals therefore converge to Gaussian integrals, and Chebyshev's bound $\mathbb P(|N_ T-T|>M\sqrt T)\leq M^{-2}$ then allows $M\to \infty $. Consequently, if real thresholds $a_ T$ satisfy $(a_ T-T)/\sqrt T\to a\in \mathbb R$, then $\mathbb P(N_ T\geq a_ T)\to 1-\Phi (a)$; rounding changes the normalized threshold by at most $T^{-1/2}$, and $\Phi $ is continuous.

Set $T_ m=m|z_ m|^{2r}$. Then $T_ m\to \infty $ and, for every integer $0\leq s<r$,

\[ \frac{L_ s(q_ m)-T_ m}{\sqrt{T_ m}}\longrightarrow -\tau . \]

Apply the preceding Poisson limit in (27.65). The uniform error there tends to zero, and each of the finitely many probabilities tends to $1-\Phi (-\tau )=\Phi (\tau )$. $\square$

For a sequence $q_ m\to \infty $, put $R_ m=(q_ m/(rm))^{1/(2r)}$. At fixed $\tau $, the radii in proposition 27.12.5 satisfy

\[ |z_ m|=R_ m\left(1+\frac{\tau }{2\sqrt{rq_ m}}+O_{r,\tau }(q_ m^{-1})\right). \]

If $q_ m=o(m)$, these radii tend to zero, while their ratio to the scale $m^{-1/(2r)}$ of the full kernel tends to infinity; when $r>1$, they shrink to a curvature zero. To transfer this transition to a compact manifold, both the local comparison of norms and the error of the global extension must be uniform in the growing vanishing order. The next section establishes both estimates on a curve.

27.13 Localization on curves

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now transfer the transition of proposition 27.12.5 to a compact curve. The selected sections concentrate on a shrinking disk around the distinguished point. When the remainder of the metric weight is negligible on that disk, the norm there can be compared directly with the model norm, and restriction to the disk gives an upper bound for the kernel. For the lower bound, positivity of the curvature away from the distinguished point permits a global $L^2$ correction of localized model sections that preserves the growing vanishing order.

Let $C$ be a connected compact Riemann surface, let $x\in C$, and let $(L,h)$ be a holomorphic line bundle on $C$ with a smooth Hermitian metric. Fix an integer $r\geq 1$. Suppose that in a holomorphic coordinate $z$ centered at $x$ and a holomorphic frame $e$,

\[ |e|_ h^2=\mathrm{e}^{-\phi },\qquad \phi (z)=|z|^{2r}+O(|z|^{2r+1}). \]

Let $\nu $ be a smooth positive volume form on $C$, written as $a(z)\, \mathrm{d}A$ in this coordinate, where $\mathrm{d}A$ is Euclidean area and $a(0)=1$. For positive integers $m$, choose nonnegative integers $q_ m$ such that

\[ q_ m\longrightarrow \infty ,\qquad q_ m=o(m^{1/(2r+1)}). \]

Write $\mathfrak m_ x$ for the ideal sheaf of $x$, and equip

\[ V_ m=H^0(C,L^ m\otimes \mathfrak m_ x^{q_ m}) \]

with the norm $\| s\| _ m^2=\int _ C|s|_{h^ m}^2\, \mathrm{d}\nu $. Its density is $B_ m=\sum _ j|s_{m,j}|_{h^ m}^2$ for any orthonormal basis $(s_{m,j})$, and $B_ m=0$ if $V_ m=0$. As in section 27.12, $\Phi $ denotes the standard Gaussian distribution function.

Theorem 27.13.1. For a fixed real $\tau $, let $x_ m$ lie in the coordinate neighborhood and satisfy

\[ T_ m:=m|z(x_ m)|^{2r}=q_ m/r+\tau \sqrt{q_ m/r}. \]

Then

27.76
\begin{equation} \limsup _{m\to \infty } \frac{\pi B_ m(x_ m)}{mr^2|z(x_ m)|^{2r-2}}\leq \Phi (\tau ). \end{equation}

If the curvature of $(L,h)$ is semipositive on $C$ and strictly positive on $C\setminus \{ x\} $, this ratio converges to $\Phi (\tau )$.

Proof. We compare $B_ m$ with the model kernels of section 27.12 on a shrinking disk, which gives the upper bound. For the lower bound, we cut off model polynomials concentrated near $x$ and correct them to global sections by an $L^2$ estimate. Put $z_ m=z(x_ m)$, $\rho _ m=|z_ m|$, $R_ m=(q_ m/(rm))^{1/(2r)}$ and

\[ S_ m=\frac{mr^2\rho _ m^{2r-2}}\pi . \]

Then $R_ m\to 0$ and $\rho _ m/R_ m\to 1$, and $T_ m>0$ for all sufficiently large $m$. Positive constants denoted by $M$, with subscripts when needed, may change between estimates. They are independent of $m$ and of the argument of $z_ m$; they may depend on the fixed geometric data, $r$, $\tau $, and fixed auxiliary cutoffs or radii. We write $q=q_ m$ when no confusion is possible. The model densities $B_{m,r}^{(q)}$ are those of section 27.12.

Step 1: the kernel on a shrinking disk. Fix a real $A>1$ and set $D_ m=\{ |z|<A R_ m\} $. On $D_ m$,

\[ m|\phi (z)-|z|^{2r}|\leq M_ A mR_ m^{2r+1} =M_ A\frac{q_ mR_ m}{r}=o(1),\qquad \sup _{D_ m}|a-1|=o(1). \]

The first limit follows from $q_ mR_ m=r^{-1/(2r)}(q_ m^{2r+1}/m)^{1/(2r)}\to 0$. Hence, on $D_ m$, the norm defined by $h^ m$ and $\nu $ and the radial norm with weight $\mathrm{e}^{-m|z|^{2r}}\, \mathrm{d}A$ differ by a factor $1+o(1)$, uniformly over all holomorphic functions on $D_ m$. The same comparison holds for the corresponding pointwise weights at $z_ m$.

For integers $k\geq 0$, let $I_{m,k}$ and $J_{m,k}$ be the squared norms of $z^ k$ on $D_ m$ and on the whole plane, respectively, with this radial weight. Polar integration gives

\[ \begin{aligned} I_{m,k}& =\frac\pi r m^{-(k+1)/r}\gamma ((k+1)/r,U_ m), & U_ m& =m(A R_ m)^{2r},\\ J_{m,k}& =\frac\pi r m^{-(k+1)/r}\Gamma ((k+1)/r), & \gamma (b,U)& =\int _0^ U t^{b-1}\mathrm{e}^{-t}\, \mathrm{d}t. \end{aligned} \]

Here $b,U>0$. By Parseval's identity on circles and Tonelli's theorem, the normalized monomials form a complete orthonormal system in the space of holomorphic functions on $D_ m$ with finite radial norm: the squared norm of a Taylor series is the sum of the squared norms of its monomials, so its partial sums converge in that norm. Therefore the density of the subspace of functions vanishing to order at least $q$ is

\[ B_ m^{D}(z_ m)=\mathrm{e}^{-T_ m}\sum _{k\geq q}\frac{\rho _ m^{2k}}{I_{m,k}}. \]

Point evaluation is continuous by the local submean inequality, so this sum is also the supremum of $\mathrm{e}^{-T_ m}|f(z_ m)|^2$ over the unit ball of that subspace.

Choose fixed real numbers $1<c<d<A^{2r}$ and put $k_ m=\lfloor rcT_ m\rfloor $. For large $m$, $k_ m\geq q$ and $U_ m\geq dT_ m$. Uniformly for $0\leq k\leq k_ m+1$,

27.79
\begin{equation} \frac{I_{m,k}}{J_{m,k}}=1+O(\mathrm{e}^{-\eta T_ m}) \end{equation}

for some fixed $\eta >0$. Indeed, let $G_ b$ be a random variable with Gamma density $t^{b-1}\mathrm{e}^{-t}/\Gamma (b)$ on $(0,\infty )$. A change of variable gives $\mathbb E\mathrm{e}^{sG_ b}=(1-s)^{-b}$ for $0<s<1$. With $b=(k+1)/r\leq cT_ m+O_ r(1)$, Markov's inequality yields

\[ 1-\frac{\gamma (b,U_ m)}{\Gamma (b)} \leq \mathrm{e}^{-sU_ m}(1-s)^{-b}. \]

Since $d>c$, choose $s>0$ small enough that $sd+c\log (1-s)>0$. The right side is at most $M\mathrm{e}^{-\eta T_ m}$, as required.

The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for the positive radial measure gives $I_{m,k+1}^2\leq I_{m,k}I_{m,k+2}$, so $I_{m,k+1}/I_{m,k}$ increases with $k$. By (27.79) and the asymptotics of Gamma ratios,

\[ \rho _ m^2\frac{I_{m,k_ m}}{I_{m,k_ m+1}} =(1+o(1))T_ m^{1/r} \frac{\Gamma ((k_ m+1)/r)}{\Gamma ((k_ m+2)/r)} \longrightarrow c^{-1/r}<1. \]

Choose $\kappa \in (c^{-1/r},1)$. For all large $m$, the ratio of consecutive terms $\rho _ m^{2k}/I_{m,k}$ is at most $\kappa $ for every $k\geq k_ m$. Consequently,

\[ \mathrm{e}^{-T_ m}\sum _{k>k_ m}\frac{\rho _ m^{2k}}{I_{m,k}} \leq \frac{\kappa }{1-\kappa } \mathrm{e}^{-T_ m}\frac{\rho _ m^{2k_ m}}{I_{m,k_ m}} =o(S_ m). \]

For the last equality, replace $I_{m,k_ m}$ by $J_{m,k_ m}$ using (27.79). The resulting term is one of the terms of the series for $B_{m,r}^{(k_ m)}(z_ m)$. In (27.65) for this kernel, each residue threshold divided by $T_ m$ tends to $c>1$, so Chebyshev's inequality makes all the Poisson tails tend to zero, and the comparison error is $O(T_ m^{-1/2})$. Hence $B_{m,r}^{(k_ m)}(z_ m)=o(S_ m)$.

For the terms with $q\leq k\leq k_ m$, we use (27.79). Since $I_{m,k}\leq J_{m,k}$ for every $k$, we obtain

\[ B_{m,r}^{(q)}(z_ m)\leq B_ m^{D}(z_ m) \leq (1+o(1))B_{m,r}^{(q)}(z_ m)+o(S_ m). \]

By proposition 27.12.5,

27.80
\begin{equation} B_ m^{D}(z_ m)/S_ m\longrightarrow \Phi (\tau ). \end{equation}

The global norm of a selected section dominates its norm on $D_ m$. Restriction to $D_ m$ and the preceding comparisons of norms therefore give $B_ m(x_ m)\leq (1+o(1))B_ m^ D(z_ m)$, also when $V_ m=0$. This proves (27.76).

Step 2: polynomials with almost all their norm near the point. From now on we assume the curvature hypotheses of the last assertion. Fix a real $c>1$ and put $K_ m=\lfloor cq_ m\rfloor $. Define

\[ C_ m=\sum _{k=q_ m}^{K_ m}\frac{\rho _ m^{2k}}{J_{m,k}},\qquad p_ m(z)=C_ m^{-1/2}\sum _{k=q_ m}^{K_ m} \frac{\bar z_ m^{\, k}z^ k}{J_{m,k}}. \]

For large $m$ these are well defined, $\| p_ m\| _{m,r}=1$, and

27.81
\begin{equation} \mathrm{e}^{-T_ m}|p_ m(z_ m)|^2 =B_{m,r}^{(q_ m)}(z_ m)-B_{m,r}^{(K_ m+1)}(z_ m) =(\Phi (\tau )+o(1))S_ m. \end{equation}

Here the first equality holds because $p_ m$ is the normalized evaluation vector at $z_ m$ of the span of $z^{q_ m},\ldots ,z^{K_ m}$. The omitted kernel $B_{m,r}^{(K_ m+1)}(z_ m)$ is $o(S_ m)$ by (27.65), since its Poisson thresholds divided by $T_ m$ tend to $c>1$; the second equality then follows from proposition 27.12.5.

Choose $\delta \in (0,1/2)$ so that the coordinate disk $|z|<\delta $ satisfies $\phi (z)\geq \beta |z|^{2r}$ for a fixed $\beta \in (1/2,1)$, and such that $a$ is bounded above there. For a sufficiently large fixed $A>1$,

27.82
\begin{equation} \int _{A R_ m<|z|<\delta }|p_ m|^2\mathrm{e}^{-m\phi }a\, \mathrm{d}A=o(1). \end{equation}

To see this, write $p_ m(z)=\sum _{k=q_ m}^{K_ m}b_{m,k}z^ k$, so that $\sum _ k|b_{m,k}|^2J_{m,k}=1$. The lower bound for $\phi $, the orthogonality of monomials on circles and polar integration bound the left side by a fixed constant times

\[ \sum _{k=q_ m}^{K_ m}|b_{m,k}|^2J_{m,k}\, \beta ^{-(k+1)/r} \mathbb P\{ G_{(k+1)/r}\geq \beta A^{2r}q_ m/r\} . \]

The exponential moment of $G_ b$ at $s=1/2$ gives, for $b,U>0$,

\[ \beta ^{-b}\mathbb P\{ G_ b\geq U\} \leq \exp \bigl (-U/2+b\log (2/\beta )\bigr ). \]

Choose $A$ so large that $\beta A^{2r}/2>c\log (2/\beta )+1$. Since $(k+1)/r\leq (cq_ m+1)/r$, the last factor in the sum is $O(\mathrm{e}^{-q_ m/r})$, uniformly in $k$. This proves (27.82). On $|z|<A R_ m$, the norm defined by $h^ m$ and $\nu $ and the radial model norm differ by a factor $1+o(1)$, as shown in Step 1, and the model norm of $p_ m$ on this disk is at most $1$.

Fix a smooth cutoff function $\chi $ with $0\leq \chi \leq 1$, compactly supported in $|z|<\delta $ and equal to one near zero, and extend $\chi p_ m e^{\otimes m}$ by zero to $C$. The preceding estimates give

27.83
\begin{equation} \| \chi p_ m e^{\otimes m}\| _ m^2\leq 1+o(1). \end{equation}

The numbers $\delta ,\beta ,A$ and the cutoff $\chi $ are fixed before $m$ tends to infinity.

Step 3: a global correction with the same vanishing condition. Let $f_ m=\bar\partial (\chi p_ m e^{\otimes m})$. Its support is contained in a fixed annulus on which $\phi $ has a positive lower bound. The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the identity $\sum _ k|b_{m,k}|^2J_{m,k}=1$ give, for $|z|<\delta <1$,

\[ |p_ m(z)|^2\leq \sum _{k=q_ m}^{K_ m}\frac{|z|^{2k}}{J_{m,k}} \leq M(K_ m+1)m^{(K_ m+1)/r}. \]

Here we used that $\Gamma (b)$ has a positive lower bound for $b\geq 1/r$. Since $q_ m\log m=o(m)$, the factor $\mathrm{e}^{-m\phi }$ on the annulus dominates this growth of the coefficients.

Regard $\nu $ as a Kähler form on the curve; it defines pointwise norms of forms and the induced metric $h_{K_ C^{-1}}$ on the anticanonical bundle $K_ C^{-1}$. Choose a smooth cutoff function $\eta $ with $0\leq \eta \leq 1$, supported in the coordinate chart and equal to one near $x$; shrink its support if necessary. For a sufficiently small fixed $t_0>0$, the global smooth functions

\[ \ell _ t=\eta \log (|z|^2+t^2),\qquad 0<t\leq t_0, \]

extended by zero outside the chart, are nonpositive. Their complex Hessians are nonnegative where $\eta =1$, and elsewhere they are bounded below independently of $t$, because $\eta =1$ near $x$.

Choose a fixed smooth real function $\psi $ that equals a sufficiently large positive multiple of $|z|^2$ near $x$ and is cut off inside the chart, so that

\[ \mathrm{i}\Theta (K_ C^{-1})+\mathrm{i}\partial \bar\partial \psi \geq 2\nu \]

on a neighborhood of $x$ where $\eta =1$. Here $\mathrm{i}\Theta $ denotes the Chern curvature form, without the factor $1/(2\pi )$, so that the weight $\mathrm{e}^{-\psi }$ contributes $\mathrm{i}\partial \bar\partial \psi $. For all large $m$ and all $0<t\leq t_0$, equip $E_ m=L^ m\otimes K_ C^{-1}$ with the smooth metric

\[ H_{m,t}=(h^ m\otimes h_{K_ C^{-1}})\, \mathrm{e}^{-\psi -q_ m\ell _ t}. \]

This metric satisfies

27.84
\begin{equation} \mathrm{i}\Theta (E_ m,H_{m,t})\geq \nu . \end{equation}

Indeed, near $x$ the two terms involving $h$ and $\ell _ t$ are nonnegative, and the other two together are at least $2\nu $. On the complement of a fixed such neighborhood, compactness and strict positivity give a lower bound $c_0m\nu $ for $m\mathrm{i}\Theta (L,h)$, with $c_0>0$. Since $q_ m=o(m)$, this term absorbs all remaining negative terms, which are bounded by $M(q_ m+1)\nu $.

On the support of $f_ m$, the functions $\ell _ t$ are bounded below uniformly in $t$. The polynomial estimate, the bounded derivatives of $\chi $, and $q_ m\log m=o(m)$ therefore give a constant $a_0>0$ such that, for all large $m$,

\[ \int _ C|f_ m|_{h^ m,\nu }^2 \mathrm{e}^{-\psi -q_ m\ell _ t}\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq \mathrm{e}^{-a_0m}\qquad (0<t\leq t_0). \]

On a curve, $K_ C=\Lambda ^{1,0}$, so an $L^ m$-valued $(0,1)$-form is the same as an $E_ m$-valued $(1,1)$-form, and the induced metrics give it the same pointwise norm; the anticanonical factor in $E_ m$, whose curvature enters (27.84), accounts for this change of bidegree. In bidegree $(1,1)$ on a curve, (27.84) makes the curvature operator at least the identity. Since the curve is compact and every $(1,1)$-form on it is $\bar\partial $-closed, the $L^2$ existence theorem for complete Kähler manifolds [Theorem 5.1, DemAM11] applies. It gives an $L^ m$-valued weak solution $u_{m,t}$ with

\[ \bar\partial u_{m,t}=f_ m,\qquad \int _ C|u_{m,t}|_{h^ m}^2 \mathrm{e}^{-\psi -q_ m\ell _ t}\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq \mathrm{e}^{-a_0m}. \]

Now fix $m$ and let $t\downarrow 0$. Since $\ell _ t\leq 0$ and $\psi $ is bounded, the solutions $u_{m,t}$ are bounded in the original $L^2$ norm, and a subsequence converges weakly to some $u_ m$. Continuity of $\bar\partial $ in the sense of distributions gives $\bar\partial u_ m=f_ m$. For fixed $t'>0$ and $t<t'$, we have $\ell _ t\leq \ell _{t'}$, so the same bound holds in the smooth weighted space with weight $\mathrm{e}^{-\psi -q_ m\ell _{t'}}$, whose norm is equivalent to the original one for this fixed $m$ and $t'$. Weak lower semicontinuity, followed by monotone convergence as $t'\downarrow 0$, gives

\[ \int _ C|u_ m|_{h^ m}^2\mathrm{e}^{-\psi -q_ m\ell }\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq \mathrm{e}^{-a_0m},\qquad \ell =\eta \log |z|^2. \]

In particular, $\| u_ m\| _ m^2\leq M\mathrm{e}^{-a_0m}$. Near $x$ the right side $f_ m$ vanishes, so the Cauchy–Riemann equation, in the sense of distributions, makes $u_ m$ holomorphic in the frame $e^{\otimes m}$. The smooth factors in the weight are bounded above and below near $x$ for each fixed $m$, so the coefficient of $u_ m$ is square integrable against $|z|^{-2q_ m}$. By lemma 1.16.1 in dimension one, this forces vanishing order at least $q_ m$ at $x$.

Since $p_ m$ also vanishes to order at least $q_ m$ at zero, it follows that $s_ m=\chi p_ m e^{\otimes m}-u_ m$ is a global holomorphic section in $V_ m$. By (27.83), $\| s_ m\| _ m\leq 1+o(1)$.

Step 4: evaluation at the moving point. For large $m$, the disk $D_ m$ lies in the region where $\chi =1$ and $f_ m=0$. Apply the local comparison of norms and the extremal property of $B_ m^ D$ to the holomorphic function representing $u_ m$ on $D_ m$, which vanishes to order at least $q_ m$. By (27.80),

\[ |u_ m(x_ m)|_{h^ m}^2 \leq (1+o(1))B_ m^ D(z_ m)\| u_ m\| _ m^2 \leq M S_ m\mathrm{e}^{-a_0m}. \]

The pointwise factor $\mathrm{e}^{-m\phi (z_ m)}$ differs from $\mathrm{e}^{-T_ m}$ by a factor $1+o(1)$. Since $\Phi (\tau )>0$, (27.81) and the triangle inequality give

\[ |s_ m(x_ m)|_{h^ m}^2\geq (\Phi (\tau )+o(1))S_ m. \]

In particular, these sections are nonzero for large $m$. The extremal characterization of $B_ m$ and $\| s_ m\| _ m\leq 1+o(1)$ yield the matching lower bound, which proves the limit. $\square$

The hypothesis $q_ m=o(m^{1/(2r+1)})$ enters only through $mR_ m^{2r+1}=o(1)$, which makes the first remainder term of the metric weight negligible throughout the disk carrying the norm. For larger orders, the curvature quadrature domains of section 27.13.1 absorb the harmonic part of this error and determine where the transition occurs. Theorem 27.13.4 gives the corrected transition for all sublinear orders, and theorem 27.13.5 determines the optimal range in which the uncorrected law holds for every metric. On a fibration over a curve, the kernel identities of section 27.7 transfer these transitions whenever the descended spaces carry the same vanishing conditions and the induced norm.

27.13.1 Curvature quadrature domains

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The disk comparison above requires the error of the metric weight to be small throughout the disk carrying the norm. A larger range of orders is obtained by absorbing the harmonic part of this error into the holomorphic sections. The transition then takes place on the boundary of the region where the remaining obstacle potential detaches from the weight with first-order contact, and a quadrature identity for the curvature determines that boundary.

Keep $C,x,(L,h),r,z,e$ and $\nu =a(z)\, \mathrm{d}A$ from section 27.13, and assume that the curvature of $(L,h)$ is semipositive on $C$ and strictly positive on $C\setminus \{ x\} $. We now allow integers $q=q_ m\to \infty $ with $q=o(m)$. Write $V_{m,q}=H^0(C,L^ m\otimes \mathfrak m_ x^ q)$, and let $B_{m,q}$ be its density for the norm of section 27.13. Put

\[ \rho =\phi _{z\bar z},\qquad t=q/m,\qquad R=(t/r)^{1/(2r)}. \]

Write $\mathbb D$ for the unit disk.

Lemma 27.13.2. Let $b>1$, and let $Q(R,\zeta )$ be a real function, jointly smooth for small real $R$ and $|\zeta |<b$, with $Q(0,\zeta )=|\zeta |^{2r}$. Write $Q_ R=Q(R,\cdot )$, and suppose that every $Q_ R$ is subharmonic and that $Q_{R,\zeta \bar\zeta }>0$ on a fixed neighborhood of the unit circle. For small $R$, let $U_ R$ be the largest subharmonic function on $\{ |\zeta |<b\} $ such that $U_ R\leq Q_ R$ and $U_ R\leq 2r\log |\zeta |+O(1)$ near zero. Then the noncontact set $\Omega _ R=\{ U_ R<Q_ R\} $ is connected, and its boundary is a radial graph near the unit circle. Let $F_ R:\mathbb D\to \Omega _ R$ be the conformal map with $F_ R(0)=0$ and $F_ R'(0)>0$. For every fixed $k$ and all sufficiently small $R$, depending on $k$, the map $F_ R$ and the harmonic function $(U_ R-2r\log |\zeta |)\circ F_ R$ depend smoothly on $R$ in the Hölder spaces $C^{k,\alpha }(\overline{\mathbb D})$, and they differ from their values at $R=0$ by $O(R)$.

The arguments below use only finitely many of these norms, so the dependence of the range of $R$ on $k$ is harmless.

Proof. Fix $k\geq 3$ and $0<\alpha <1$. Let $A^{k,\alpha }$ be the real Banach space of functions that are holomorphic on $\mathbb D$, of class $C^{k,\alpha }$ on its closure, and real at zero. For $h\in A^{k,\alpha }$ near zero set $f(u)=u(1+h(u))$. For small $h$, the map $f$ is univalent, with $f(0)=0$ and $f'(0)>0$. On $|u|=1$ define

\[ K(R,h)=f(u)(Q_ R)_\zeta (f(u))-r, \qquad T(R,h)=(P_-K,\operatorname {Re}[K]_0), \]

where $P_-$ is the projection onto the strictly negative Fourier modes and $[\cdot ]_0$ is the constant Fourier coefficient.

The Cauchy projections are bounded on these Hölder spaces. This follows from the Hilbert kernel on the circle: subtract the value at the evaluation point, split the integral at twice the length of the increment, and use the Hölder bound for differences on the inner part and the bound for the differentiated kernel on the outer part. This gives the $C^{0,\alpha }$ bound, and commuting angular derivatives with the projections gives the $C^{k,\alpha }$ bound. The superposition operator $h\mapsto (Q_ R)_\zeta \circ f$ involves no derivatives of $h$ and is smooth. Thus $T$ is a smooth map into the product of the space of negative-mode functions with $\mathbb R$. At $(R,h)=(0,0)$ we have $T=0$ and

\[ D_ hK(0,0)v=r^2(v+\bar v),\qquad D_ hT(0,0)v=(r^2\overline{v-v(0)},\, 2r^2v(0)). \]

This is a bounded real-linear isomorphism, so the implicit function theorem in Banach spaces gives a unique normalized branch $f_ R$ of solutions of $T(R,h)=0$, depending smoothly on $R$ in the fixed space, with $\| f_ R-\mathrm{id}\| =O(R)$. The branches obtained for larger $k$ agree with this one by uniqueness in the smaller space. For each fixed order, this gives the spatial estimates and the expansions in $R$ needed below, on a parameter interval that depends on the order.

The equation $T=0$ prescribes only the real part of $[K]_0$; we show that the imaginary part vanishes as well. Since $P_-K=0$, the function $K$ extends holomorphically to $\mathbb D$, and $K(0)=[K]_0$ is purely imaginary. On the other hand, Stokes' formula gives

\[ \frac1{2\pi \mathrm{i}}\int _{|u|=1}(Q_ R)_\zeta (f_ R(u))f_ R'(u)\, \mathrm{d}u =\frac1\pi \int _{f_ R(\mathbb D)}(Q_ R)_{\zeta \bar\zeta }\, \mathrm{d}A\in \mathbb R. \]

On the circle the integrand equals $(r+K)f_ R'/f_ R$, whose residue at zero is $r+K(0)$. Hence $K(0)$ is real as well, and therefore zero. Consequently

\[ S_ R(\zeta )=\frac{r+K(f_ R^{-1}(\zeta ))}{\zeta } =r/\zeta +H_ R(\zeta ) \]

with $H_ R$ holomorphic on $\Omega _ R=f_ R(\mathbb D)$, and $S_ R=(Q_ R)_\zeta $ on the boundary of $\Omega _ R$. Let

\[ U_ R=2r\log |\zeta |+2\operatorname {Re}\int _0^\zeta H_ R(w)\, \mathrm{d}w+C_ R. \]

Its complex gradient agrees with that of $Q_ R$ on the boundary, so the difference $Q_ R-U_ R$ is constant there; choose the constant $C_ R$ to make it zero. Then the difference and its gradient vanish on the boundary. Since the tangential second derivative of the difference also vanishes there, its inward normal second derivative equals $\Delta Q_ R>0$. Hence $Q_ R-U_ R>0$ in an inner collar of uniform width. At $R=0$,

\[ U_0=1+2r\log |\zeta |,\qquad |\zeta |^{2r}-1-2r\log |\zeta |>0\quad (0<|\zeta |<1). \]

By uniform convergence, this gap keeps a positive minimum on the remaining compact subsets of the interior for small $R$, and the logarithmic term controls a neighborhood of zero.

Set $U_ R=Q_ R$ outside $\Omega _ R$. Since values and gradients match across the boundary, the Laplacian of the patched function carries no measure on the boundary. Its curvature is $r\delta _0$ inside $\Omega _ R$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}Q_ R\geq 0$ outside, so it is an admissible minorant. If $v$ is any competitor, then $v-U_ R$ is subharmonic on $\Omega _ R\setminus \{ 0\} $, bounded above near zero and nonpositive on the boundary. Removability of the singularity at zero and the maximum principle give $v\leq U_ R$ in $\Omega _ R$, and outside $\Omega _ R$ this follows from $v\leq Q_ R$. This identifies the envelope and shows that there are no further noncontact components. The radial graph property and the estimates for the harmonic part follow from the normalized map and its dependence on the parameter. We henceforth write $F_ R=f_ R$. $\square$

We apply the lemma to the given weight. By Taylor's formula with integral remainder, for small real $R$,

\[ Q_ R(\zeta )=R^{-2r}\phi (R\zeta ) =|\zeta |^{2r}+R E(R,\zeta ) \]

with a jointly smooth function $E$. Choose the holomorphic primitive with $G_ R(0)$ real, and write the envelope in $\Omega _ R$ as

\[ U_ R=2r\log |\zeta |+2\operatorname {Re} G_ R(\zeta ),\qquad G_ R=1/2+O(R). \]

Set $D_ t=R\Omega _ R$. In the original coordinate $z$, the obstacle potential is $\phi _ t(z)=R^{2r}U_ R(z/R)$ inside $D_ t$ and $\phi $ outside. Replacing $\phi $ by $\phi _ t$ in this chart and keeping $h$ elsewhere defines a global singular metric on $L$. Its local potential is $C^1$ across the boundary, bounded above by $\phi $, and has the pole $2t\log |z|+O(1)$. On the boundary, $\phi _ z=t/z+g_ t'$ for a holomorphic function $g_ t$. Green's formula therefore gives the quadrature identity

27.85
\begin{equation} \int _{D_ t}\rho (z)f(z)\, \mathrm dA=\pi t f(0) \end{equation}

for every function $f$ holomorphic near $\overline{D_ t}$. The curvature of the singular metric is $t\delta _ x$ inside $D_ t$ and the original curvature outside. Finally, we write the boundary as $\Gamma _ t=\{ R c_ R(\theta )\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta }\} $ with a positive periodic function $c_ R$, where $c_ R-1=O(R)$ in each fixed norm used below.

27.13.2 Gaussian transitions at curvature boundaries

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

To prove the transition at the boundary $\Gamma _ t$, we again correct localized sections by an $L^2$ estimate. The singular metric of section 27.13.1, whose curvature at $x$ is the point mass $t\delta _ x$, enforces the growing vanishing order of the correction. Its curvature vanishes on the rest of $D_ t$, so a bounded function varying on the scale $R$ must first restore strict positivity there.

Lemma 27.13.3. There are constants $c,C>0$ with the following properties, for all sufficiently large $m$. There is a smooth function $b_ R$, bounded independently of $m$, such that the metric on $L^ m\otimes K_ C^{-1}$ induced by the singular local potential $\psi _ m=m\phi _ t+b_ R$ on $L^ m$ and by the anticanonical metric of $\nu $ has curvature at least $(c/R^2)\nu $ on $C$. On annuli outside $D_ t$ whose radii are comparable to $R$, the lower bound improves to $(cq/R^2)\nu $. If an $L^ m$-valued $(0,1)$-form $\alpha $ is supported on such an annulus, the equation $\bar\partial v=\alpha $ has a solution $v$, depending linearly on $\alpha $, with

27.86
\begin{equation} \| v\| _{h^ m}^2\leq C\frac{R^2}{q}\| \alpha \| _{h^ m,\nu }^2, \end{equation}

where both sides are the $L^2$ norms defined by $h^ m$ and $\nu $. If $\alpha $ vanishes near $x$, then $v$ is holomorphic near $x$ and vanishes to order at least $q$ there.

Proof. Take $b_ R=|z|^2/R^2$ on a disk containing $D_ t$, and cut it off before the radius $3R$, so that its negative curvature is supported outside $D_ t$. This function is uniformly bounded and its Hessian is $O(R^{-2})$. On the support of its negative curvature, the curvature $m\rho $ is comparable to $q/R^2$; since $q\to \infty $, it absorbs the negative part. Inside $D_ t$, the function $b_ R$ contributes the curvature $c/R^2$. Elsewhere in a fixed coordinate disk, the original curvature is bounded below by a fixed multiple of $m|z|^{2r-2}$, and off that disk it is at least $cm$. Since $mR^2\to \infty $, all these bounds dominate $c/R^2$ and the bounded Ricci term of $\nu $.

We use the $L^2$ estimate for $\bar\partial $ with a pointwise lower bound for the curvature of a singular metric, in the form described in section 34.14. As in the proof of theorem 27.13.1, we regard $L^ m$-valued $(0,1)$-forms as $(1,1)$-forms with values in $L^ m\otimes K_ C^{-1}$ and sections of $L^ m$ as $(1,0)$-forms with values in the same bundle; this identification preserves the pointwise norms and brings in the Ricci term of $\nu $. The estimate then reads as follows: if $H$ is a singular metric on $L^ m$ and the curvature of $L^ m\otimes K_ C^{-1}$ is at least $\lambda \nu $ for a continuous function $\lambda >0$, then

\[ \int _ C|v|_ H^2\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq \int _ C\lambda ^{-1}|\alpha |_{H,\nu }^2\, \mathrm{d}\nu . \]

On the support of $\alpha $ we have $\lambda ^{-1}=O(R^2/q)$, $\phi _ t=\phi $, and $b_ R$ is bounded. Elsewhere $\phi _ t\leq \phi $ gives $\mathrm{e}^{-m\phi }\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\psi _ m}$. These comparisons yield (27.86). Choosing the solution of minimal norm in the singular weighted Hilbert space makes the correction linear in its source.

The estimate requires a continuous lower bound $\lambda $. We take $\lambda $ equal to $c/R^2$ inside $D_ t$ and to $cq/R^2$ on the exterior annulus, interpolating across a thin exterior collar of $\Gamma _ t$. For the cutoff sources used below, whose supports are separated from $\Gamma _ t$, the collar can be chosen to miss the source. For an exterior source whose support accumulates on $\Gamma _ t$, we let the collar shrink to $\Gamma _ t$; since $\Gamma _ t$ has zero area, dominated convergence in the integrals of $\lambda ^{-1}|\alpha |^2$ and weak compactness in the fixed weighted Hilbert space give the same estimate. The singular metric is handled by approximation, which gives the same estimate for a weak solution. Where the source vanishes, the Cauchy–Riemann equation in the sense of distributions makes the solution holomorphic. Near $x$, the weight is $|z|^{-2q}$ times a smooth positive factor. A holomorphic term $z^ j$ is square integrable against it precisely when $j\geq q$, so the solution has the required vanishing order. $\square$

Theorem 27.13.4. Let $D_ t$ and $\Gamma _ t$ be as in section 27.13.1. Let $d_ t(z)$ be the signed Euclidean distance from $z$ to $\Gamma _ t$, positive outside $D_ t$, and let $p_ t(z)$ be the nearest point of $\Gamma _ t$. For every fixed $M>0$, uniformly over points $z$ with $|d_ t(z)|\leq MR/\sqrt q$,

27.87
\begin{equation} \frac{\pi a(z)B_{m,q}(z)}{m\rho (z)} -\Phi \bigl (2\sqrt{m\rho (p_ t(z))}\, d_ t(z)\bigr )\longrightarrow 0. \end{equation}

If $d_ t(z)=o(R)$ and $\sqrt q\, d_ t(z)/R$ tends to $-\infty $ or $+\infty $, the same normalized kernel tends to $0$ or $1$, respectively. Thus, for every sublinear order, the transition is centered on $\Gamma _ t$ and has length $1/(2\sqrt{m\rho })$.

Proof. We pass to logarithmic coordinates adapted to $\Gamma _ t$, in which the norm of a section of order $q$ becomes Gaussian in the distance to the boundary. Restriction to a thin ring gives the upper bound, and localized trial sections corrected by lemma 27.13.3 give the lower bound. Finally we compare with the full kernel to obtain saturation. Throughout the proof, $c$ and $C$ denote positive constants independent of $m$, which may change from line to line.

Step 1: the effective potential. Define on the unit disk

\[ \begin{aligned} A_ R(w)& =G_ R(F_ R(w))+r\log (F_ R(w)/w),\\ \Psi _ R& =Q_ R(F_ R(w))-2\operatorname {Re} A_ R(w)-2r\log |w|, \end{aligned} \]

where the logarithm uses the branch near one. Then $A_ R=1/2+O(R)$ and $F_ R=w+O(R)$ in every fixed norm used below. The effective potential $\Psi _ R=(Q_ R-U_ R)\circ F_ R$ is positive inside the disk, and its value and gradient vanish on the boundary. For $w=\mathrm{e}^{v+\mathrm{i}\theta }$ it has the expansion

27.90
\begin{equation} \Psi _ R=(2r^2+O(R))v^2+O(v^3), \end{equation}

and the exact quadratic coefficient is $2(Q_ R)_{\zeta \bar\zeta }(F_ R(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta }))|F_ R'(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta })|^2$.

The maps need not extend holomorphically across the circle. To obtain charts that also cover a neighborhood outside the circle, we truncate their Taylor series at the degree $K=\lfloor q^{1/16}\rfloor $ and denote the truncations by $F_ K,A_ K$. The Taylor coefficients of a function bounded in a sufficiently high fixed Hölder norm decay faster than a large fixed power of their index, so the truncation error is $O(q^{-10})$, together with the fixed number of derivatives needed here. Fix a small $c_0>0$. On $|w|\leq 1+c_0/K$ the derivatives of the truncations remain bounded and $F_ K-w,A_ K-1/2=O(R)$, because the factor $(1+c_0/K)^ K\leq \mathrm{e}^{c_0}$ controls the sums. In particular, $F_ K$ is univalent there for small $R$. Put $\delta =c_0/K$ and

\[ \Psi _ K=Q_ R(F_ K)-2\operatorname {Re} A_ K-2r\log |w|. \]

The errors in the value and the first derivative of $\Psi _ K$ on the circle are $O(q^{-10})$, and uniformly for $|v|\leq \delta $,

27.91
\begin{equation} \Psi _ K=(2r^2+O(R)+O(|v|))v^2+O(q^{-10})(1+|v|). \end{equation}

Inside the disk, the effective potential has uniform positive quadratic growth near the circle, a positive lower bound on compact annuli farther inside, and logarithmic growth at zero.

In the coordinate $z=RF_ K(w)$, a local holomorphic section of order at least $q$ at $x$ is uniquely represented, in the original frame, as

27.92
\begin{equation} s=w^ q\exp ((q/r)A_ K(w))f(w),\qquad |s|_{h^ m}^2=|f(w)|^2\mathrm{e}^{-(q/r)\Psi _ K(w)}, \end{equation}

with $f$ holomorphic near zero. The volume in this chart is $R^2(1+O(R))\, \mathrm{d}A_ w$. Put $\eta =\sqrt{rq}\, v$. On each band $|\eta |\leq N$ with $N$ fixed,

\[ (q/r)\Psi _ K=2\eta ^2+o(1). \]

Step 2: the upper bound. Let the observation point have coordinate $\eta _0$ in a fixed bounded interval. Restricting the norm to the band $|\eta |\leq N$ bounds the kernel from above by the kernel of the restricted space. For $f(w)=\sum _{j\geq 0}b_ jw^ j$, the limiting diagonal norms of the monomials on this band are

\[ n_{j,N}=\frac{2\pi R^2}{\sqrt{rq}}I_ N(j/\sqrt{rq}),\qquad I_ N(s)=\int _{-N}^ N \mathrm{e}^{2s\eta -2\eta ^2}\, \mathrm{d}\eta . \]

After normalization by $rq/(\pi R^2)$, the limiting upper kernel is

27.93
\begin{equation} \frac12\mathrm{e}^{-2\eta _0^2}\int _0^\infty \frac{\mathrm{e}^{2s\eta _0}}{I_ N(s)}\, \mathrm{d}s. \end{equation}

The passage from sums to integrals uses Riemann sums with mesh $1/\sqrt{rq}$. To control their tails, bound $I_ N(s)$ from below by its integral over a fixed subinterval of $[-N,N]$ lying beyond every observation point $\eta _0$; this gives an exponential bound uniform in the mesh. Thus we may first pass to the Riemann integral and then let $N\to \infty $. Since $I_\infty (s)=\sqrt{\pi /2}\, \mathrm{e}^{s^2/2}$, the expression (27.93) tends to

27.94
\begin{equation} \frac1{\sqrt{2\pi }}\int _0^\infty \mathrm{e}^{-(s-2\eta _0)^2/2}\, \mathrm{d}s =\Phi (2\eta _0). \end{equation}

Step 3: the lower bound. Fix $T>0$, and use (27.92) with $f$ in the span of the monomials $w^ j$, $0\leq j\leq T\sqrt{rq}$. For $f=\sum _ jb_ jw^ j$, put $\| s\| _{\mathrm{trial}}^2=\sum _ j|b_ j|^2n_ j$, with $n_ j$ given by (27.96) below. Let $\chi $ be a radial cutoff function in the $w$-plane, supported in $|w|\leq 1+\delta $ and equal to one on $|w|\leq 1+\delta /2$. The annulus on which $\chi $ is not constant lies outside $D_ t$. Equations (27.91) and (27.92) bound the squared norm of the source, as a quadratic form on the entire span, by

\[ \| \bar\partial (\chi s)\| _{h^ m,\nu }^2\leq C(R\delta )^{-2}\mathrm{e}^{-cq\delta ^2}\| s\| _{\mathrm{trial}}^2; \]

the linear term $2jv$ in the exponent is absorbed because $\sqrt q\, \delta \to \infty $. Lemma 27.13.3 gives a correction $g_ s$, depending linearly on $s$, with $\bar\partial g_ s=\bar\partial (\chi s)$ and

27.95
\begin{equation} \| g_ s\| _{h^ m}^2\leq C(q\delta ^2)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-cq\delta ^2}\| s\| _{\mathrm{trial}}^2. \end{equation}

The constants here depend on $m$ only through $R$ and $q$. The factor $R^2$ in (27.86) cancels the factor $R^{-2}$ coming from the derivative of the cutoff; this cancellation is needed even when $q$ grows more slowly than every power of $m$.

On this span, the Gram form of the global norm of $\chi s$ converges, in the sense of relative operator norm, to the diagonal form

27.96
\begin{equation} n_ j=\frac{2\pi R^2}{\sqrt{rq}}\sqrt{\frac\pi 2} \exp (j^2/(2rq)). \end{equation}

Indeed, the ratio of the weights is uniformly close to one on each fixed band in $\eta $. Outside such a band, the radial bound for the weight and Parseval's identity on circles bound arbitrary combinations by diagonal tails $\mathrm{e}^{-c\eta ^2+2T|\eta |}$. We first pass to the limit on a fixed band and then enlarge it. The positive lower bound of the effective potential on compact annuli and its logarithmic singularity at zero control the remaining part of the disk. This argument controls the whole quadratic form, without a factor depending on the dimension of the span.

To evaluate the corrections we need a pointwise bound. Let $u$ be a section holomorphic on a disk of radius $R/\sqrt q$ centered at a point $z$ with $|z|$ comparable to $R$. Then a local submean estimate gives

27.97
\begin{equation} |u(z)|_{h^ m}^2\leq C(q/R^2)\| u\| _{h^ m}^2. \end{equation}

To prove it, multiply $u$ by the exponential of a holomorphic polynomial that removes the harmonic part of the linear and quadratic Taylor terms of $m\phi $ at $z$. After rescaling the disk to unit size, the remaining quadratic coefficient is bounded and the third-order error is $O(q^{-1/2})$, which proves the estimate. It applies to the corrections, because their sources lie on the cutoff annulus, away from the observation points. Together with (27.95), it shows that the kernel of the corrected global trial sections differs by $o(q/R^2)$ from the local trial kernel. After normalization, the lower limit is the integral in (27.94) with upper limit $T$ instead of infinity, and letting $T\to \infty $ gives the matching lower bound.

Step 4: return to the original coordinate. Let $p$ be the nearest boundary point and $\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta }$ its preimage under $F_ R$. Then

\[ \begin{aligned} v& =\frac{d_ t(z)}{R|F_ R'(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta })|} +O(d_ t(z)^2/R^2)+o(q^{-1/2}),\\ m\rho (p)R^2|F_ R'(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta })|^2& =rq(1+O(R)). \end{aligned} \]

Moreover, $m\rho (z)/a(z)=(rq/R^2)(1+o(1))$ in the band $|d_ t(z)|\leq MR/\sqrt q$. These identities convert $2\eta _0$ into the argument in (27.87) and give the normalization of the density there. This proves (27.87).

Step 5: the full density and saturation. Let $B_ m^{\rm full}$ be the density of the full space $H^0(C,L^ m)$. At points with $|z|\asymp R$, it satisfies

27.100
\begin{equation} B_ m^{\rm full}(z)=\frac{m\rho (z)}{\pi a(z)}(1+o(1)), \end{equation}

uniformly in an $o(R)$-neighborhood of $\Gamma _ t$. We give a direct localization argument, which also yields the saturation statements of the theorem. Remove the harmonic Taylor terms of $m\phi $ at the observation point and rescale by $(m\rho (z))^{-1/2}$. On every fixed rescaled disk, the remaining weight tends to $|u|^2$, where $u$ is the rescaled coordinate, and the volume density tends to the constant $a(z)$. The Taylor estimate is uniform, because the third derivatives of $\phi $ at radius $R$ are $O(R^{2r-3})$, so the normalized third-order error is $O(q^{-1/2})$. Restriction to such disks bounds the evaluation norm from above, and the Gaussian norm of the constant function on them tends to $\pi $ as their radius grows. This proves the upper bound in (27.100).

For the lower bound, take the holomorphic exponential canceling the harmonic Taylor terms, and cut it off on a disk of radius $\varepsilon R$ centered at the observation point, where $\varepsilon \to 0$ and $\sqrt q\, \varepsilon \to \infty $. Its local squared norm is $(\pi a(z)/(m\rho (z)))(1+o(1))$ times its squared weighted value at the center, which is one. The quadratic lower bound for the weight bounds the source on the cutoff annulus by an exponential factor $\mathrm{e}^{-cq\varepsilon ^2}$. Add the same bounded function of scale $R$ to the original metric and use the pointwise $\bar\partial $ estimate. The relative squared norm of the correction is bounded by $C(q\varepsilon ^2)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-cq\varepsilon ^2}=o(1)$, after decreasing $c$ if necessary, and its value at the center is $o(1)$ by (27.97). This proves the matching lower bound.

For saturation inside $D_ t$, use the polynomial chart above and put $u=1-|w|>0$. Restrict the norm in (27.92) to the exterior half-band $0\leq v\leq c/\sqrt q$. On this half-band every monomial $w^ j$, $j\geq 0$, has norm at least $cR^2/\sqrt q$. Parseval's identity on circles and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality give

\[ B_{m,q}(z)\leq C\frac{\sqrt q}{R^2}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-cqu^2}}{1-|w|^2}. \]

If $u\sqrt q\to \infty $, this bound divided by $q/R^2$ tends to zero. The error $O(q^{-10})$ of the chart changes neither the hypothesis nor the conclusion.

For saturation outside $D_ t$, cut off the localized Gaussian section at $z$ on a disk of radius

\[ \varepsilon R,\qquad \varepsilon =\min \{ d_ t(z)/(3R),q^{-1/4}\} . \]

This disk lies entirely outside $D_ t$, and again $\varepsilon \to 0$ and $\sqrt q\, \varepsilon \to \infty $. Apply lemma 27.13.3 with the singular obstacle metric, which equals the original metric on the support of the source. The correction has negligible norm and value, and it vanishes to order $q$ at $x$. Thus a member of $V_{m,q}$ asymptotically attains the full Gaussian density, and (27.100) gives the reverse inequality. This proves saturation to one. $\square$

27.13.3 Growth ranges and compact examples

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

In the coordinate $z$, the quadrature boundary $\Gamma _ t$ differs from the model circle $|z|=R$ by $O(R^2)$. Comparing this displacement with the transition width $R/\sqrt q$ gives the range of orders in which the uncorrected law holds for every metric. A holomorphic change of coordinate produces a compact example in which the displacement has exactly this size.

Theorem 27.13.5. Keep the setting of section 27.13.1, fix the integer $r\geq 1$ and a real $\tau $, and consider observation points with coordinates $z_ m$ and the law

27.101
\begin{equation} \frac{\pi B_{m,q}(z_ m)}{mr^2|z_ m|^{2r-2}} \longrightarrow \Phi (\tau ),\qquad m|z_ m|^{2r}=q/r+\tau \sqrt{q/r}, \end{equation}

where the second relation prescribes the radii of the observation points. If $q=o(m^{1/(r+1)})$, this law holds for every choice of the geometric data and every sequence of arguments of $z_ m$. This range is optimal: for every sequence $q\to \infty $ with $q=o(m)$ that is not $o(m^{1/(r+1)})$, there is a compact example satisfying the same hypotheses in which the law fails along a subsequence.

For fixed geometric data, write $\Gamma _ t$ as $\{ |z|=R c_ R(\theta )\} $. Then (27.101) holds for every fixed $\tau $ and every sequence of arguments if and only if

27.102
\begin{equation} \sqrt q\, \| c_ R-1\| _\infty \longrightarrow 0. \end{equation}

Proof. By lemma 27.13.2, $c_ R-1=O(R)$ in every fixed norm required here. The observation points in (27.101) satisfy

\[ |z_ m|/R=1+\tau /(2\sqrt{rq})+O(q^{-1}). \]

Since the slope of the boundary graph tends to zero, the argument of $\Phi $ in theorem 27.13.4 is $\tau +o(1)$ whenever $\sqrt q R\to 0$, and this condition is equivalent to $q=o(m^{1/(r+1)})$. Moreover, $a(z)=1+o(1)$ and $\rho (z)=r^2|z|^{2r-2}(1+o(1))$ at these points, so the two normalizations agree. Thus theorem 27.13.4 proves the law for all data in this range.

The same reasoning shows that (27.102) is sufficient. For necessity, suppose that (27.102) fails. On a subsequence, choose an angle realizing the maximal boundary displacement and observe at $|z|=R$ with $\tau =0$. A nonzero finite subsequential limit of the scaled displacement gives a nonzero Gaussian argument, and an infinite one gives saturation to zero or one. Radial and normal distances have relative error tending to zero, because the graph has vanishing slope. In either case the limit differs from $1/2$.

It remains to construct the compact examples. On $\mathbb P^1$ choose a radial metric on $\mathcal O(d)$ whose local potential $f(u)$, $u=|w|^2$, equals $u^ r$ near zero, such that $g(u)=uf'(u)$ is strictly increasing on $(0,\infty )$ and tends to $d$, and such that $g(u)=d-\epsilon /u$ for large $u$, with a constant $\epsilon >0$. Such a smooth interpolation exists for a sufficiently large integer $d$: prescribe a positive function $g'$, equal to $r^2u^{r-1}$ near zero and to $\epsilon /u^2$ near infinity, with total integral $d$. Then $f=d\log u+\mathrm{constant}+\epsilon /u$ near infinity, so the potential in the frame at infinity is smooth with strictly positive curvature. Choose a smooth radial volume form equal to $\mathrm{d}A_ w$ near zero.

For sufficiently small $t$, the hole $D_ t$ lies in the region where $f(u)=u^ r$ and equals the disk $|w|<R$: rotational invariance and uniqueness make it a disk, and its curvature mass is $rR^{2r}=t$. Hence the transition and saturation statements of theorem 27.13.4 apply to this compact example.

Now use instead the coordinate $z$ defined by $w=z+cz^2$, with a constant $c>0$. In this coordinate the same compact metric satisfies $\phi (z)=|z|^{2r}+O(|z|^{2r+1})$, the volume form still has $a(0)=1$, and vanishing orders are unchanged. At the uncorrected observation point $z=R$ with $\tau =0$,

\[ 2\sqrt{mr^2R^{2r-2}}\, (|w|-R) =2c\sqrt r\, \sqrt q R, \]

and the normalizing factors computed in the two coordinates have ratio tending to one. If $q$ is not $o(m^{1/(r+1)})$, there is a subsequence on which $\sqrt q R$ tends to a positive finite number or to infinity. Theorem 27.13.4, together with its saturation statement when the argument diverges, then gives a limit strictly larger than $1/2$. This proves that the range is sharp. $\square$

27.13.4 Corrections from the smooth weight

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The boundary $\Gamma _ t$ can be expanded in powers of $R$, and the leading terms of this expansion give explicit corrections to the uncorrected law. Write

\[ \phi (z)=|z|^{2r}+P_{2r+1}(z,\bar z)+O(|z|^{2r+2}),\qquad P(\theta )=P_{2r+1}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta },\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\theta }), \]

where $P_{2r+1}$ is a real homogeneous polynomial of degree $2r+1$ in $(z,\bar z)$. Let $\Lambda $ be the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator of the unit circle, $\Lambda \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} n\theta }=|n|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} n\theta }$. The first boundary coefficient is given by

27.103
\begin{equation} c_ R=1+Rb_1+O(R^2),\qquad b_1=-\frac{((2r+1)-\Lambda )P}{4r^2}. \end{equation}

To derive this, write the interior obstacle, to first order in $R$ and in polar coordinates $(s,\theta )$ for $\zeta $, as $1+2r\log s+R H$ with $H$ harmonic. Matching values on the boundary $s=1+Rb_1$ gives $H|_{s=1}=P$; the terms involving $b_1$ cancel there, since the radial model already matches first derivatives. Matching radial derivatives then gives $4r^2b_1+(2r+1)P-\Lambda P=0$. If $P_{2r+1}$ is harmonic, then $\Lambda P=(2r+1)P$ and $b_1=0$, as it must be, since such a term can be removed by a change of frame.

For $q=O(m^{1/(r+1)})$, the argument of $\Phi $ at the uncorrected observation point of (27.101) is

27.104
\begin{equation} \tau +\frac{\sqrt q R}{2r^{3/2}}((2r+1)-\Lambda )P(\theta )+o(1), \end{equation}

uniformly in $\theta $, and the normalized kernel differs from $\Phi $ of this argument by $o(1)$. The remainder is small because $\sqrt q R^2\to 0$ in this range. For $w=z+cz^2$ we have $P=2r\operatorname {Re}(c\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta })$, and the shift is $2\sqrt{rq}R\operatorname {Re}(c\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta })$, in agreement with the compact example above.

For every fixed integer $J\geq 1$, the parameter construction of lemma 27.13.2 gives

\[ c_ R=1+\sum _{j=1}^ J R^ j b_ j+O(R^{J+1}) \]

in every fixed norm needed. The coefficients satisfy an explicit recursion, obtained by expanding the interior harmonic obstacle in powers of $R$. At order $j$, matching values determines the boundary data of the new harmonic term from the Taylor term of degree $2r+j$ of $\phi $ and all preceding coefficients, and harmonic extension determines the term itself. Matching derivatives then gives an equation $4r^2b_ j+\text{(known terms)}=0$; the new coefficient $b_ j$ does not enter the value equation of order $j$. For $j\geq 2$, nonlinear contributions of lower-order terms also appear.

Replacing $\Gamma _ t$ by the curve given by the truncated expansion moves the center of the transition by $O(R^{J+2})$. This curve can therefore be used in place of $\Gamma _ t$ whenever

\[ \sqrt q R^{J+1}\to 0, \quad \hbox{equivalently}\quad q=o(m^{(J+1)/(r+J+1)}). \]

If $b_1=\cdots =b_{j-1}=0$ but $b_ j\not\equiv 0$, the criterion (27.102) shows that, for this fixed weight, the uncorrected law holds exactly in the range $q=o(m^{j/(r+j)})$, and this range is sharp when all directions of observation are allowed. Indeed, the higher terms are relatively $O(R)$ at an angle where $b_ j\ne 0$.

In the radial case $\phi =f(u)$ with $u=|z|^2$, the hole is a disk by uniqueness and rotational invariance. By (27.85), its boundary circle $\{ u=u_*\} $ is determined by

\[ m u_*f'(u_*)=q. \]

With $\rho (u)=f'(u)+uf''(u)$, the asymptotic variance of the dominant monomial degree is $m u\rho (u)$, and the corresponding Gaussian argument is

27.105
\begin{equation} \frac{m u f'(u)-q}{\sqrt{m u\rho (u)}}. \end{equation}

For bounded arguments, Taylor expansion at $u_*$ identifies it with $2\sqrt{m\rho (u_*)}(\sqrt u-\sqrt{u_*})+o(1)$. Hence theorem 27.13.4 proves this radial formula for all sublinear orders. For $f=u^ r+c u^{r+1}+\cdots $, the first shift at the uncorrected observation point is $c(r+1)r^{-3/2}\sqrt q R^2$, so the critical growth is $m^{2/(r+2)}$. This larger radial threshold does not apply in arbitrary coordinates.

No fixed finite Taylor jet of the weight determines the correction for every $q=o(m)$. For example, near zero take

\[ f(u)=u^ r+c u^ r \mathrm{e}^{-1/\sqrt u},\qquad c>0, \]

with the second term extended by zero at $u=0$, and interpolated globally as above. It has the same Taylor jet as $u^ r$ at zero to every finite order, but at $u=R^2$ its relative contribution to $uf'(u)$ is $c \mathrm{e}^{-1/R}(1+1/(2rR))$.
