For $q=\lfloor m/\log m\rfloor $, we have $\sqrt q \mathrm{e}^{-1/R}/R\to \infty $. Hence the uncorrected observation point with $\tau =0$ lies outside the true boundary circle by many transition widths, and the normalized kernel tends to one. The quadrature-domain correction includes this flat contribution.

27.14 A logarithmic kernel scale in two dimensions

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

In the curve models above, the metric weight has a single homogeneous leading term. In two dimensions, a mixed monomial in the weight can compete with the pure monomials and introduce a logarithm into the scale of the kernel. For an explicit metric on $\mathbb P^2$, the relevant global section norm can be computed directly.

Write $\mathrm dA$ for Euclidean area on $\mathbb C$. We first isolate the integral responsible for the logarithm.

Lemma 27.14.1. Set $W(a,b)=ab+a^3+b^3$ on $[0,\infty )^2$. As $t\to \infty $,

\[ \begin{gathered} J(t):=\int _0^\infty \int _0^\infty \mathrm e^{-tW(a,b)}\, \mathrm da\, \mathrm db =\frac{\log t}{3t}+O(t^{-1}), \\ \int _0^\infty \int _0^\infty (a+b)\mathrm e^{-tW(a,b)} \, \mathrm da\, \mathrm db=O(t^{-4/3}). \end{gathered} \]

Proof. Put $A=t^{-2/3}$ and $B=t^{-1/3}$; the logarithm comes from the interval $A\leq a\leq B$. For $a>0$, the substitution $u=tab$ gives

\[ H_ t(a):=\int _0^\infty \mathrm e^{-t(ab+b^3)}\, \mathrm db =\frac1{ta}\int _0^\infty \mathrm e^{-u-u^3/(t^2a^3)}\, \mathrm du. \]

Since $0\leq 1-\mathrm e^{-v}\leq v$ for $v\geq 0$,

\[ 0\leq \frac1{ta}-H_ t(a)\leq \frac6{t^3a^4}. \]

Over $A\leq a\leq B$, the integral of this error is $O(t^{-1})$. Replacing $\mathrm e^{-ta^3}$ by one in $\int _ A^ B\mathrm e^{-ta^3}/(ta)\, \mathrm da$ costs at most $\int _ A^ B a^2\, \mathrm da=O(t^{-1})$ as well. Thus the middle interval contributes

\[ \int _ A^ B\mathrm e^{-ta^3}H_ t(a)\, \mathrm da =\frac1t\log \frac BA+O(t^{-1}) =\frac{\log t}{3t}+O(t^{-1}). \]

For $a<A$, the bound $H_ t(a)\leq \int _0^\infty \mathrm e^{-tb^3}\, \mathrm db=O(t^{-1/3})$ gives a contribution $O(t^{-1})$. For $a>B$, use $H_ t(a)\leq 1/(ta)$ and substitute $v=ta^3$ to obtain the bound

\[ \int _ B^\infty \frac{\mathrm e^{-ta^3}}{ta}\, \mathrm da =\frac1{3t}\int _1^\infty \frac{\mathrm e^{-v}}v\, \mathrm dv =O(t^{-1}). \]

Finally, $aH_ t(a)\leq 1/t$ gives

\[ \int _0^\infty \int _0^\infty a\mathrm e^{-tW(a,b)} \, \mathrm da\, \mathrm db \leq \frac1t\int _0^\infty \mathrm e^{-ta^3}\, \mathrm da =O(t^{-4/3}). \]

Symmetry proves the same bound for $b$. $\square$

On $L=\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^2}(1)$, define a Hermitian metric by

\[ |S|_ h^2([Z])= \frac{|S(Z)|^2}{\bigl (|Z_0|^6+3|Z_0Z_1Z_2|^2+3|Z_1|^6+3|Z_2|^6\bigr )^{1/3}} \qquad (S\text{ a linear form}). \]

The denominator is positive away from zero and homogeneous of degree two in $|Z|$, so the metric is well defined and smooth. Its local weight is one third of the logarithm of a sum of squared absolute values of holomorphic cubic functions, so its curvature is semipositive. In the chart $Z_0\ne 0$, put $z_ j=Z_ j/Z_0$ and $p=[1:0:0]$. The frame $e=Z_0$ has

27.106
\begin{equation} |e|_ h^2=\mathrm e^{-\phi },\qquad \phi (z)=\tfrac 13\log \bigl (1+3W(|z_1|^2,|z_2|^2)\bigr ). \end{equation}

In particular the curvature vanishes at $p$.

Proposition 27.14.2. Let $\nu $ be a smooth positive probability volume form on $\mathbb P^2$, invariant under the rotations of $z_1$ and of $z_2$, and write

\[ \nu =\rho (z)\, \mathrm dA(z_1)\, \mathrm dA(z_2),\qquad \rho _0=\rho (0)>0 \]

in the chart above. Give $H^0(\mathbb P^2,L^ m)$ the norm $\int _{\mathbb P^2}|s|_{h^ m}^2\, \mathrm d\nu $, and let $B_ m$ be its kernel density. Then

\[ B_ m(p)=\frac{3}{\pi ^2\rho _0}\frac m{\log m} \left(1+O((\log m)^{-1})\right). \]

For the Fubini–Study probability volume, the leading constant is $3/2$.

Proof. The homogeneous monomials form an orthogonal basis, because the metric and the volume are invariant under the two independent rotations and distinct monomials transform by distinct characters. At $p$, only $Z_0^ m$ has a nonzero value, equal to one in the frame $e^{\otimes m}$. Consequently $B_ m(p)=I_ m^{-1}$, where

\[ I_ m=\int _{\mathbb P^2}|Z_0^ m|_{h^ m}^2\, \mathrm d\nu =\int _{\mathbb C^2}(1+3W(|z_1|^2,|z_2|^2))^{-m/3} \rho (z)\, \mathrm dA(z_1)\, \mathrm dA(z_2). \]

Here the omitted projective line $\{ Z_0=0\} $ has zero volume. By rotational invariance, the first derivatives of $\rho $ vanish at zero, so $\rho (z)=\rho _0+O(|z_1|^2+|z_2|^2)$ near zero. In the variables $a=|z_1|^2$ and $b=|z_2|^2$, polar integration has Jacobian $\pi ^2\, \mathrm da\, \mathrm db$.

Choose a fixed small $\delta >0$. On $W\leq \delta $, set $\ell (W)=\tfrac 13\log (1+3W)$. For constants $c,C>0$ independent of $m$,

\[ cW\leq \ell (W)\leq W,\qquad 0\leq \mathrm e^{-m\ell (W)}-\mathrm e^{-mW} \leq CmW^2\mathrm e^{-cmW} \leq \frac Cm\mathrm e^{-cmW/2}. \]

The first estimate of lemma 27.14.1 bounds the integral of this error in the weight by $O(m^{-2}\log m)$, and the second bounds the error from replacing $\rho $ by $\rho _0$ by $O(m^{-4/3})$. On the complement $W>\delta $, the contribution to $I_ m$ is at most $(1+3\delta )^{-m/3}$, because $\nu $ has mass one, and the corresponding tail of the exponential model is at most $\mathrm e^{-m\delta /2}J(m/2)$. Therefore

\[ I_ m=\pi ^2\rho _0J(m)+O(m^{-4/3})+O(m^{-2}\log m) =\frac{\pi ^2\rho _0}{3m}\log m+O(m^{-1}). \]

Inverting proves the first assertion. The Fubini–Study probability volume has the local expression

\[ \nu =\frac2{\pi ^2}(1+|z_1|^2+|z_2|^2)^{-3} \, \mathrm dA(z_1)\, \mathrm dA(z_2). \]

It has mass one, since $2\int _0^\infty \int _0^\infty (1+a+b)^{-3}\, \mathrm da\, \mathrm db=1$, and its density at zero is $2/\pi ^2$. The leading constant is therefore $3/2$. $\square$

Thus the full kernel, without any selection, already has a logarithmic factor at this fixed curvature zero, and its coefficient depends on the volume density at $p$. The calculation does not give a transition for growing vanishing ideals; that would require control of the norms of the selected monomials on the moving scale, as in the curve case.

27.15 Anisotropic point jets and isolated poles on fibrations

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Vanishing to a given order at a point couples the variables through the total degree, even when the metric weight splits as a sum. On a compact product of curves, this coupling becomes a half-space condition on independent monomial indices, and the transition on each curve factor determines the local law in higher dimensions. An isolated pole on a submersion with connected fibers gives a further application: its multiplier ideal does not descend to the base, although the condition it imposes on pulled-back sections does.

Let $q\geq 2$. For $1\leq j\leq q$, choose integers $r_ j\geq 2$, at least two of them unequal, and numbers $\varepsilon _ j>0$. Put

\[ Y=(\mathbb P^1)^ q,\qquad x=(0,\ldots ,0),\qquad d_ j=r_ j+1,\qquad L=\boxtimes _ j\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(d_ j). \]

In the affine coordinates $z_ j$ and the product frame $e$, put $t_ j=|z_ j|^2$ and define the smooth metric and volume by

\[ \begin{gathered} |e|_ h^2=\mathrm e^{-\phi },\qquad \phi =\sum _ j\psi _ j(t_ j),\qquad \psi _ j(t)=\log (1+t^{r_ j}+\varepsilon _ jt^{r_ j+1}),\\ \nu _ Y=a(t)\prod _ j\mathrm dA_ j,\qquad a(t)=\prod _ j a_ j(t_ j),\qquad a_ j(t)=(1+t)^{-2}, \end{gathered} \]

where $\mathrm dA_ j$ is Euclidean area in $z_ j$. In particular $\nu _ Y(Y)=\pi ^ q$ and $a(0)=1$. Define

\[ H_ j(t)=t\psi _ j'(t),\qquad G_ j(t)=tH_ j'(t),\qquad g_ j(t)=G_ j(t)/t,\qquad \theta =c_1(L,h). \]

The metric extends across infinity: there its weight, in terms of $u=|z_ j|^{-2}$, is $\log (\varepsilon _ j+u+u^{d_ j})$, which has positive curvature at infinity. For $t>0$, $H_ j(t)$ and $G_ j(t)$ are the mean and the variance of the exponents $0,r_ j,d_ j$ taken with probabilities proportional to $1,t^{r_ j},\varepsilon _ jt^{d_ j}$. Hence $G_ j>0$, and $H_ j$ increases from $0$ to $d_ j$. Near zero,

27.107
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} H_ j(t)& =r_ jt^{r_ j}+(r_ j+1)\varepsilon _ jt^{r_ j+1}+O(t^{2r_ j}),\\ G_ j(t)& =r_ j^2t^{r_ j}+(r_ j+1)^2\varepsilon _ jt^{r_ j+1}+O(t^{2r_ j}). \end{aligned} \end{equation}

Thus $\theta $ is semipositive, and its rank drops along the coordinate divisors $\{ z_ j=0\} $.

For integers $k_ m\to \infty $ with $k_ m=o(m)$, let $B_ m$ be the density of

\[ V_ m=H^0(Y,L^ m\otimes \mathfrak m_ x^{k_ m}),\qquad \| s\| ^2=\int _ Y|s|_{h^ m}^2\nu _ Y. \]

The ideal $\mathfrak m_ x^{k_ m}$ of the point $x$ imposes vanishing of total order $k_ m$, rather than separate orders in the individual variables. As before, $\Phi $ is the standard Gaussian distribution function.

Theorem 27.15.1. Let $z=z^{(m)}$ be points of the affine chart, with $t_ j=|z_ j|^2$, such that $t_ j\to 0$ and $\mu _ j:=mH_ j(t_ j)$ satisfies $\mu _ j/k_ m\to \alpha _ j>0$, where $\sum _ j\alpha _ j=1$. Set $V=\sum _ jmG_ j(t_ j)$. If $(\sum _ j\mu _ j-k_ m)/\sqrt V\to \tau \in \mathbb R$, then

27.110
\begin{equation} \frac{\pi ^ q a(t)B_ m(z)}{m^ q\prod _ jg_ j(t_ j)}\longrightarrow \Phi (\tau ). \end{equation}

The arguments of the coordinates $z_ j$ are arbitrary. For all sufficiently large $m$,

27.111
\begin{equation} N_ m:=\dim V_ m=\prod _ j(d_ jm+1)-\binom {k_ m+q-1}{q}, \qquad N_ m/m^ q\longrightarrow D:=\prod _ jd_ j. \end{equation}

Moreover $m^{-q}B_ m\nu _ Y\to \theta ^ q/q!$ in total variation.

Proof. The proof reduces the kernel to a probability for a sum of independent variables, one for each factor, and applies the one-variable transition of section 27.13 to each of them. In each variable, the affine coefficient of a global section of $L^ m$ is entire, and its expression in the frame at infinity must be holomorphic, so it is a polynomial of degree at most $d_ jm$. Hence the monomials $z^\ell e^ m$, $0\leq \ell _ j\leq d_ jm$, form a basis of the global sections. A polynomial vanishes to order at least $k_ m$ at $x$ exactly when all its monomials have total degree at least $k_ m$, so the monomials with $\sum _ j\ell _ j\geq k_ m$ form a basis of $V_ m$. Once $k_ m\leq \min _ j(d_ jm)+1$, which holds for large $m$, all monomials of total degree below $k_ m$ are global, and counting them gives (27.111).

Invariance under the rotation of each coordinate makes these monomials orthogonal. Their squared norms are $\prod _ jJ_{j,m,\ell _ j}$, where

\[ J_{j,m,\ell }=\pi \int _0^\infty t^\ell \mathrm e^{-m\psi _ j(t)}a_ j(t)\, \mathrm dt, \qquad w_{j,m,\ell }(t)=\frac{t^\ell \mathrm e^{-m\psi _ j(t)}}{J_{j,m,\ell }}. \]

These integrals converge even for $\ell =d_ jm$. Write $K_{j,m}=\sum _{\ell =0}^{d_ jm}w_{j,m,\ell }$, and let $\mathsf L_1,\ldots ,\mathsf L_ q$ be independent random variables with $\Pr \{ \mathsf L_ j=\ell \} =w_{j,m,\ell }(t_ j)/K_{j,m}(t_ j)$ for $0\leq \ell \leq d_ jm$. Then

27.112
\begin{equation} B_ m(z)=\prod _ jK_{j,m}(t_ j)\, \Pr \left\{ \sum _ j\mathsf L_ j\geq k_ m\right\} . \end{equation}

Each factor $(\mathbb P^1,\mathcal O(d_ j))$, with the metric $\mathrm e^{-\psi _ j(|z_ j|^2)}$ and the volume $a_ j\, \mathrm dA_ j$, satisfies the hypotheses of theorem 27.13.4, and $K_{j,m}$ is its full density. Put $V_ j=mG_ j(t_ j)$. Since $\partial _{z_ j}\partial _{\bar z_ j}\psi _ j(|z_ j|^2)=g_ j(t_ j)$, the full density (27.100) gives $K_{j,m}=mg_ j(t_ j)/(\pi a_ j(t_ j))(1+o(1))$. For any fixed $y\in \mathbb R$, apply the radial formula (27.105) to the integer order

\[ b_{j,m}=\lfloor \mu _ j+y\sqrt{V_ j}\rfloor +1. \]

This order tends to infinity and is sublinear. The selected kernel of the factor, divided by its full kernel, is $\Pr \{ \mathsf L_ j\geq b_{j,m}\} $, and it tends to $\Phi (-y)$. Hence

\[ \frac{\mathsf L_ j-\mu _ j}{\sqrt{V_ j}}\ \Longrightarrow \ N(0,1). \]

That is, these normalized variables converge in distribution to a standard Gaussian variable. Here $\mu _ j$ and $\sqrt{V_ j}$ serve only as centering and scale; we do not need the exact mean or variance of $\mathsf L_ j$. Since $V_ j/V\to r_ j\alpha _ j/\sum _ i r_ i\alpha _ i>0$, independence gives a standard Gaussian limit for $(\sum _ j\mathsf L_ j-\sum _ j\mu _ j)/\sqrt V$. To see this, use the product of the limiting Gaussian laws on bounded rectangles, and then tightness and the fact that the boundary of a half-space has zero Gaussian measure. Consequently the probability in (27.112) tends to $\Phi (\tau )$, which proves the local assertion for every sequence $k_ m\to \infty $ with $k_ m=o(m)$.

For the global assertion we also need the full kernel at a fixed $t>0$, where the formula for shrinking radii does not apply. We suppress the index of the factor and set $F(u)=\psi (\mathrm e^ u)$. Then $F'(u)=H(\mathrm e^ u)$ and $F''(u)=G(\mathrm e^ u)>0$. For $\ell /m$ in a compact subinterval of $(0,d)$, the saddle $s=H^{-1}(\ell /m)$ stays compact, and Laplace expansion gives uniformly

\[ J_{m,\ell }=\pi a(s)s\sqrt{\frac{2\pi }{mG(s)}} \mathrm e^{\ell \log s-m\psi (s)}(1+o(1)). \]

Indeed, the phase in the logarithmic variable has a uniformly positive second derivative there, and outside a fixed neighborhood of the saddle point, convexity and the slopes at the endpoints bound the tails exponentially. For $\ell =mH(t)+O(\sqrt m)$, evaluation yields

\[ w_{m,\ell }(t)=\frac{\sqrt{mG(t)}}{\pi a(t)t\sqrt{2\pi }} \mathrm e^{-(\ell -mH(t))^2/(2mG(t))}(1+o(1)). \]

For indices with saddle points in a compact interval, the loss in the phase, as a function of $\ell $, has second derivative $1/(mG(s))\geq c/m$, which gives summable Gaussian tails. For the remaining indices, fix small numbers $\delta ,\sigma >0$, and bound the norm $J_{m,\ell }$ from below by the integral over an interval of length $1/m$ about $t\mathrm e^{-\sigma }$ or $t\mathrm e^{\sigma }$. If $|\ell /m-H(t)|\geq \delta $, the appropriate choice gains at least $m(\delta \sigma -C\sigma ^2)\geq cm$ in the exponent. Each such weight $w_{m,\ell }(t)$ is then at most $Cm\mathrm e^{-cm}$, and there are $dm+1$ indices. Gaussian summation therefore gives $K_ m(t)/m\to g(t)/(\pi a(t))$.

In the product chart, $\theta ^ q/q!=\prod _ j(g_ j(t_ j)/\pi )\mathrm dA_ j$, and its mass is $D$ because $\int _0^\infty g_ j(t)\, \mathrm dt=H_ j(\infty )-H_ j(0)=d_ j$. The normalized full density $m^{-q}\prod _ jK_{j,m}$ therefore tends to the density of $\theta ^ q/q!$ with respect to $\nu _ Y$ almost everywhere; the coordinate divisors have zero volume. The total mass of the normalized full kernel measure is $m^{-q}\prod _ j(d_ jm+1)\to D$. For the nonnegative densities $f_ m,f$ of these measures with respect to $\nu _ Y$, dominated convergence applied to $\min (f_ m,f)$ gives

\[ \int |f_ m-f|\nu _ Y =\int f_ m\nu _ Y+\int f\nu _ Y-2\int \min (f_ m,f)\nu _ Y\longrightarrow 0. \]

Finally, the difference between the full and the selected kernel is nonnegative and has integral $\binom {k_ m+q-1}{q}=o(m^ q)$. This proves the total-variation convergence. $\square$

27.15.1 Corrected and homogeneous centers

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The transition in theorem 27.15.1 is centered at the radii determined by the functions $H_ j$. Replacing them by the radii of the homogeneous leading terms of the weight introduces a shift, which is negligible exactly in a range of orders determined by the largest exponent $r_ j$.

Fix positive numbers $\alpha _ j$ with sum one and a real $\tau $, and set

27.113
\begin{equation} R=\sum _ j\alpha _ jr_ j,\qquad T_ m=k_ m+\tau \sqrt{Rk_ m},\qquad t_ j=H_ j^{-1}(\alpha _ jT_ m/m). \end{equation}

Then $\sum _ j\mu _ j=T_ m$ and $V\sim Rk_ m$, so (27.110) holds for every sublinear growing order. The radii $|z_ j|=\sqrt{t_ j}$ are asymptotic to $(\alpha _ jk_ m/(r_ jm))^{1/(2r_ j)}$.

Proposition 27.15.2. Keep $\alpha _ j,\tau ,R,T_ m$ as above, but use instead the homogeneous radii given by $t_ j=(\alpha _ jT_ m/(r_ jm))^{1/r_ j}$, and put $r_*:=\max _ jr_ j$. For the metrics of this section, the law

\[ \frac{\pi ^ qB_ m(z)}{m^ q\prod _ jr_ j^2|z_ j|^{2r_ j-2}}\longrightarrow \Phi (\tau ) \]

holds for every fixed $\tau $ if and only if $k_ m=o(m^{2/(r_*+2)})$. If $k_ m/m^{2/(r_*+2)}\to c\in (0,\infty )$, the left side instead tends to

\[ \Phi \bigl (\tau +C_\alpha c^{(r_*+2)/(2r_*)}\bigr ),\qquad C_\alpha =\frac1{\sqrt R}\sum _{j:r_ j=r_*} (r_*+1)\varepsilon _ j(\alpha _ j/r_*)^{1+1/r_*}. \]

If that ratio tends to infinity while $k_ m=o(m)$, the limit is $1$.

Proof. By (27.107), $\sum _ j\mu _ j=T_ m+\Delta _ m$ with

\[ \Delta _ m=\sum _ j(r_ j+1)\varepsilon _ jmt_ j^{r_ j+1} +O\left(\sum _ jmt_ j^{2r_ j}\right),\qquad V\sim Rk_ m. \]

Since $r_ j\geq 2$, each error term is negligible compared with the corresponding positive correction. Consequently

\[ \frac{\Delta _ m}{\sqrt{Rk_ m}}=(1+o(1))\sum _ j \frac{(r_ j+1)\varepsilon _ j(\alpha _ j/r_ j)^{1+1/r_ j}}{\sqrt R} k_ m^{1/2+1/r_ j}m^{-1/r_ j}. \]

At the critical scale only the indices with $r_ j=r_*$ contribute in the limit. Below it every term tends to zero, and above it a positive term diverges. The Gaussian limit of the normalized sum proved above applies in all three cases; in the last case its tightness gives probability one. Moreover, $a(t)\to 1$ and $g_ j(t_ j)/(r_ j^2t_ j^{r_ j-1})\to 1$, so the two normalizations agree. If $k_ m/m^{2/(r_*+2)}$ does not tend to zero, a subsequence has a positive finite or infinite limit, and the law fails at $\tau =0$. This proves both directions of the equivalence for these metrics. $\square$

27.15.2 An isolated selecting potential on a submersion

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Let $\pi :X\to Y$ be a surjective holomorphic submersion from a compact complex manifold $X$, with connected fibers of dimension $f\geq 1$, and put $N=q+f=\dim X$. Fix a Hermitian form $\omega _ X$ on $X$. Use $E=\pi ^*L$ with the metric $h_ E=\pi ^*h$, and a smooth positive volume form $\nu _ X$ satisfying

27.114
\begin{equation} \pi _*\nu _ X=\nu _ Y. \end{equation}

Choose $p\in \pi ^{-1}(x)$ and submersion coordinates $(z,w)$ centered at $p$, in which $\pi (z,w)=z$. Let $\chi _ p$ be a function equal to $\log (|z|^2+|w|^2)$ near $p$, smooth on $X\setminus \{ p\} $, and zero outside a larger coordinate ball. Define the selecting potentials, which depend on $m$,

\[ \varphi _ m=\frac{k_ m+N-1}{m}\chi _ p,\qquad \mathcal I_ m=\mathcal I(m\varphi _ m). \]

As in definition 1.12.3, $\mathcal I_ m$ consists of the germs $F$ for which $|F|^2\mathrm e^{-m\varphi _ m}$ is locally integrable. The selected sections are measured with the smooth norm defined by $h_ E^ m$ and $\nu _ X$.

Theorem 27.15.3. For all large $m$, $\varphi _ m\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega _ X)$ and $\mathcal I_ m=\mathfrak m_ p^{k_ m}$. Neither $\varphi _ m$ nor $\mathcal I_ m$ is pulled back from $Y$, but pullback gives an isometry of Hilbert spaces and an identity of densities

\[ H^0(X,E^ m\otimes \mathcal I_ m)=\pi ^*V_ m,\qquad B_{X,m}=B_ m\circ \pi . \]

Thus the local laws above hold for every sequence of points of $X$ whose projections satisfy their hypotheses. Moreover $\log N_ m/\log m\to q<\dim X$, and

27.115
\begin{equation} m^{-q}B_{X,m}\nu _ X\longrightarrow \left(\frac{\theta ^ q/q!}{\nu _ Y}\circ \pi \right)\nu _ X \quad \hbox{in total variation}. \end{equation}

Normalizing by $N_ m$ instead gives the limit divided by $D$, a probability measure.

Proof. The function $\chi _ p$ satisfies $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi _ p\geq -C\omega _ X$, because it is plurisubharmonic near its pole and smooth elsewhere. Since its coefficient $(k_ m+N-1)/m$ tends to zero, $\varphi _ m$ is $\omega _ X$-plurisubharmonic for large $m$. A germ of order $\ell $ at $p$ has a nonzero initial homogeneous term, so the integral of its squared modulus over the sphere of radius $s$ is $cs^{2\ell }(1+o(1))$ with $c>0$. Local integrability is therefore equivalent to

\[ \int _0^{s_0}s^{2\ell -2(k_ m+N-1)+2N-1}\, \mathrm ds =\int _0^{s_0}s^{2\ell -2k_ m+1}\, \mathrm ds<\infty , \]

that is, to $\ell \geq k_ m$. Away from $p$ the ideal is the unit ideal. This proves $\mathcal I_ m=\mathfrak m_ p^{k_ m}$. The ideal is proper at $p$ but trivial at the other points of the same fiber, which is impossible for an ideal pulled back from $Y$; likewise, the pole of $\varphi _ m$ occurs only at $p$.

By the maximum modulus principle, every section of $\pi ^*L^ m$, written in a trivialization pulled back from the base, is constant on each compact connected fiber. Local holomorphic sections of the submersion show that the resulting coefficient on the base is holomorphic, and constancy along the fibers makes these coefficients glue. In the submersion coordinates, a pulled-back coefficient depends only on $z$, so its order at $p$ equals its order at $x$. This proves the identity of the selected spaces. Fiber integration and (27.114) give

\[ \int _ X|\pi ^*s|_{h_ E^ m}^2\nu _ X=\int _ Y|s|_{h^ m}^2\nu _ Y. \]

Pulling back an orthonormal basis proves the identity of densities. If $b_ m$ and $b$ are the densities, with respect to $\nu _ Y$, of the measures in the total-variation limit of theorem 27.15.1, then the corresponding densities on $X$ are $b_ m\circ \pi $ and $b\circ \pi $, and their $L^1(\nu _ X)$ distance equals $\int _ Y|b_ m-b|\nu _ Y$. This proves (27.115). The dimension formula (27.111) gives $\log N_ m/\log m\to q$ and the final normalization. $\square$

Volume forms satisfying (27.114) exist on every such submersion. For any smooth positive volume form $\mu _ X$, write $\pi _*\mu _ X=b\nu _ Y$. Submersion charts and a finite partition of unity express $b$ as a sum of integrals of smooth compactly supported densities in the fiber variables, and differentiation under the integral sign shows that $b$ is smooth. Since the fibers are nonempty, $b>0$. Thus $\nu _ X=\mu _ X/(b\circ \pi )$ satisfies (27.114). For a product $X=Y\times F$, the fiber $F$ may be any compact connected complex manifold, Kähler or not. On a product, both the volumes $\nu _ Y\otimes \nu _ F$ with $\nu _ F(F)=1$ and the nonproduct volumes $W(y,u)\nu _ Y\nu _ F$, with $W>0$ and $\int _ FW(y,u)\nu _ F=1$, satisfy (27.114).

For an arbitrary smooth positive volume $\nu '_ X$, the pulled-back norm becomes the norm on the base for the volume $\pi _*\nu '_ X$, whereas the local laws above were proved for the specific volume $\nu _ Y$. Since the selecting potentials vary with $m$, theorem 27.15.3 does not settle 33.9.2 for fixed singularities. Weights with mixed or angular remainder terms, observation points with some $\alpha _ j=0$, and other ideals on the base are not treated here. The lifted measure keeps the chosen conditional fiber volume; it is not a Monge–Ampère measure in the dimension of $X$.

27.16 Newton selection and moment-shell limits

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On a product of projective lines, selecting the monomials whose exponents lie in the dilates of a fixed polytope gives a graded family of section spaces for which the lower bound for the potentials can be proved directly, without a general approximation theorem. The limit of the kernel measures depends on the dimension of the polytope. When this dimension is smaller than that of the manifold, the limit is carried by a family of real tori and differs from the mixed curvature measures of the envelope.

Let $X=(\mathbb P^1)^ n$ with $n\geq 1$, and let $L=\mathcal O(k_1,\ldots ,k_ n)$, where each $k_ j$ is a positive integer. Equip $L$ with its product Fubini–Study metric $h$. On the torus $T=(\mathbb C^*)^ n$, in the standard frame $e$, write

\[ r_ j=\log |z_ j|^2,\qquad \varphi _0(r)=\sum _ jk_ j\log (1+\mathrm e^{r_ j}),\qquad \theta =c_1(L,h)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _0. \]

Put $P=\prod _ j[0,k_ j]$. Fix a nonempty rational polytope $Q$ contained in the interior of $P$ whose affine span has the form

\[ \operatorname {aff}Q=b+W,\qquad b\in \mathbb Z^ n, \]

with $W\subseteq \mathbb R^ n$ a rational linear subspace. Set $d=\dim Q$ and $\Lambda =W\cap \mathbb Z^ n$. Let $\mathrm dq_\Lambda $ be the Lebesgue measure on $b+W$ normalized so that a fundamental domain of the lattice $\Lambda $ has volume one; when $d=0$, it is the unit point mass at $b$. Write $\operatorname {vol}_\Lambda (Q)$ for the volume of $Q$ with respect to $\mathrm dq_\Lambda $. In this section the selected spaces are defined directly by monomials, without a selecting potential: we select exactly the monomials with exponents in $mQ$,

27.116
\begin{equation} V_ m=\operatorname {span}\{ z^\alpha e^ m: \alpha \in mQ\cap \mathbb Z^ n\} . \end{equation}

Each of these monomials is a global section of $L^ m$, since $0\leq \alpha _ j\leq mk_ j$. Since $Q$ is convex, products of selected sections are selected, so these spaces form a graded family.

Choose a smooth real torus-invariant function $w$ on $X$ such that the function $F=\varphi _0+w$ of $r$ has positive definite Hessian $D^2F(r)$ for every $r\in \mathbb R^ n$. Choose a smooth strictly positive torus-invariant probability measure $\nu $ on $X$. On $T$ we then have $\mathrm d\nu =a(r)\, \mathrm dr\, \mathrm d\tau $, where $a>0$, $\int a\, \mathrm dr=1$, and $\mathrm d\tau $ is the Haar probability measure on $(S^1)^ n$ in the angular variables. Give $V_ m$ the Hilbert norm $\int _ X|s|_{h^ m}^2\mathrm e^{-mw}\, \mathrm d\nu $, and for an orthonormal basis put

\[ \mathcal B_ m=\sum _ j|s_{m,j}|_{h^ m}^2,\qquad B_ m=\mathrm e^{-mw}\mathcal B_ m,\qquad U_ m=m^{-1}\log \mathcal B_ m. \]

As in section 27.1, the density $B_ m$ includes the continuous weight.

27.16.1 The growth constraint and its envelope

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Let $H_ Q(r)=\max _{q\in Q}q\cdot r$ be the support function of $Q$, and define

\begin{align*} \mathcal C_ Q& =\{ v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ): v+\varphi _0\leq H_ Q+C_ v\text{ on }T\text{ for some constant }C_ v\} ,\\ E_ Q& =\left(\sup \{ v\in \mathcal C_ Q: v\leq w\text{ quasi-everywhere on }X\} \right)^*. \end{align*}

This growth condition replaces the multiplier-ideal condition of section 27.3; we do not identify $E_ Q$ with an ideal envelope.

Lemma 27.16.1. The gradient $\nabla F:\mathbb R^ n\to \operatorname {int}P$ is a smooth diffeomorphism. With $r(q)=(\nabla F)^{-1}(q)$ and $F^*(q)=q\cdot r(q)-F(r(q))$, set

\[ G_ Q(r)=\max _{q\in Q}\{ q\cdot r-F^*(q)\} . \]

Then $E_ Q=G_ Q-\varphi _0$ on $T$, and $E_ Q=-\infty $ on $X\setminus T$. Moreover, a global section $s$ of $L^ m$ belongs to $V_ m$ exactly when

27.117
\begin{equation} |s/e^ m|^2\leq C_ s\mathrm e^{mH_ Q(r)}\quad \text{on }T \quad \text{for some finite }C_ s. \end{equation}

Proof. In the charts at infinity one sees that every global section of $L^ m$ is a polynomial of degree at most $mk_ j$ in each variable $z_ j$. If $s\in V_ m$, the growth bound follows from the triangle inequality, since $|z^\alpha |^2=\mathrm e^{\alpha \cdot r}\leq \mathrm e^{mH_ Q(r)}$ for $\alpha \in mQ$. Conversely, averaging the growth bound over the angles gives $\sum _\alpha |c_\alpha |^2\mathrm e^{\alpha \cdot r} \leq C_ s\mathrm e^{mH_ Q(r)}$ for the coefficients $c_\alpha $ of $s$. If some $c_\alpha \neq 0$ had $\alpha \notin mQ$, a vector $u$ with $\alpha \cdot u>mH_ Q(u)$, which exists by separation, would contradict this bound along the ray $r=tu$, $t\to \infty $. Thus the growth condition controls each monomial separately, even though the monomials of $s$ may cancel pointwise.

For $p\in \operatorname {int}P$, put $\delta _ p=\min _ j(p_ j,k_ j-p_ j)>0$. Since $w$ is bounded,

27.118
\begin{equation} F(r)-p\cdot r\geq \delta _ p\sum _ j|r_ j|-\| w\| _\infty . \end{equation}

By strict convexity, $F-p\cdot r$ therefore has a unique minimizer, at which $\nabla F=p$. Conversely, every gradient of $F$ lies in $P$: compare the supporting hyperplanes of the convex function $F$ with the asymptotic slopes of $\varphi _0$. By the inverse function theorem the image of $\nabla F$ is open, so it is contained in the interior of $P$. This proves that $\nabla F$ is a diffeomorphism onto $\operatorname {int}P$. On a compact neighborhood of $Q$ in $\operatorname {int}P$, the inverse gradient and the first and second derivatives of $F^*$ are bounded, and the Hessian of $F^*$ is positive definite.

We next show that $G_ Q-\varphi _0$ is a competitor. In a product chart in which some of the coordinates are inverted, the local potential of $G_ Q-\varphi _0$ is

\[ \max _{q\in Q}\left\{ \sum _ j c_ j(q)\log |\zeta _ j|^2-F^*(q)\right\} , \qquad c_ j(q)=q_ j\text{ or }k_ j-q_ j. \]

All $c_ j$ are bounded below by a fixed positive number. The expression is locally bounded above, tends uniformly to $-\infty $ at the coordinate hyperplanes when the other coordinates stay bounded, and is locally integrable by comparison with one fixed summand. At points where it is finite, a maximizing summand gives the submean inequality for plurisubharmonic functions; at points where it equals $-\infty $, this inequality is automatic. These local expressions glue. Boundedness of $F^*$ on $Q$ gives $G_ Q-H_ Q=O(1)$, and Fenchel's inequality gives $G_ Q\leq F$, that is, $G_ Q-\varphi _0\leq w$. Thus $G_ Q-\varphi _0$, extended by $-\infty $ on $X\setminus T$, belongs to $\mathcal C_ Q$ and satisfies the obstacle condition.

Now let $v$ be any competitor. The obstacle condition holds everywhere: the exceptional pluripolar set has Lebesgue measure zero, and submean values over shrinking balls carry the inequality with the continuous obstacle to every point. Consider the maximum over the angles, $M(r)=\max _\tau \{ v(\mathrm e^{r/2}\tau )+\varphi _0(r)\} $, where $\mathrm e^{r/2}\tau $ denotes the point with coordinates $\mathrm e^{r_ j/2}\tau _ j$. By compactness, $M$ is upper semicontinuous. Rotating a point at which the maximum is attained gives the submean inequality for the invariant function $z\mapsto M(\log |z_1|^2,\ldots ,\log |z_ n|^2)$, which is therefore plurisubharmonic on $T$. It extends across the affine coordinate hyperplanes by the local upper bound. If $M(r)=-\infty $ for some $r$, successive one-variable maximum principles would force this function to be $-\infty $ on the corresponding polydisk, contradicting $v\not\equiv -\infty $. Thus $M$ is finite. In logarithmic coordinates this invariant function depends only on the real parts, so its positive complex Hessian shows that $M$ is convex.

We have $M\leq F$ and $M\leq H_ Q+C_ v$. Comparing along rays shows that every supporting slope of $M$ lies in $Q$. At any $r$, choose such a slope $p$. Since $M\leq F$ gives $M^*\geq F^*$, convex duality yields $M(r)=p\cdot r-M^*(p)\leq p\cdot r-F^*(p)\leq G_ Q(r)$. Hence $v\leq G_ Q-\varphi _0$ on $T$. Submean values over shrinking balls and the uniform decay of $G_ Q-\varphi _0$ near $X\setminus T$ show that $v=-\infty $ on $X\setminus T$. Since $G_ Q-\varphi _0$, extended by $-\infty $, is already upper semicontinuous, this proves the identity for the envelope after upper regularization. $\square$

27.16.2 Norms, lattice growth and the lower bound

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Theorem 27.16.2. The norms are nondegenerate and satisfy the Bernstein–Markov inequality of section 27.2 for the weight $w$. Writing $N_ m=\dim V_ m$, we have

\[ N_ m=m^ d\operatorname {vol}_\Lambda (Q)+O(m^{d-1})\quad (d\geq 1), \qquad N_ m=1\quad (d=0). \]

For all sufficiently large $m$,

27.119
\begin{equation} |U_ m-E_ Q|\leq \frac{C}{m} \left(1+\sum _ j|r_ j|+\log m\right)\quad \text{on }T. \end{equation}

Consequently $U_ m\to E_ Q$ locally uniformly on $T$ and in $L^ p$ for every finite $p$ with respect to smooth volume forms on $X$, and the closed positive currents $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U_ m$ converge weakly to $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}E_ Q$. Both $U_ m$ and $E_ Q$ equal $-\infty $ on $X\setminus T$.

Proof. Since the volume form is smooth and positive, the norm is positive on every nonzero section. Orthogonality of the angular Fourier modes diagonalizes it:

\begin{align*} A_ m(\alpha )& =\| z^\alpha e^ m\| _ m^2 =\int _{\mathbb R^ n}\mathrm e^{\alpha \cdot r-mF(r)}a(r)\, \mathrm dr,\\ \mathcal B_ m(r)& =\sum _{\alpha \in mQ\cap \mathbb Z^ n} \frac{\mathrm e^{\alpha \cdot r-m\varphi _0(r)}}{A_ m(\alpha )}. \end{align*}

For $q=\alpha /m$, the function $F(r)-q\cdot r$ has the unique minimum value $-F^*(q)$, attained at $r(q)$. Taylor's formula on a ball of radius $m^{-1/2}$ about $r(q)$, with uniform bounds on a compact neighborhood of $r(Q)$, yields

27.120
\begin{equation} c m^{-n/2}\mathrm e^{mF^*(q)}\leq A_ m(\alpha ) \leq \mathrm e^{mF^*(q)}. \end{equation}

The upper bound uses only the minimum and $\int a\, \mathrm dr=1$.

Since $b\in \mathbb Z^ n$, we have $(m\operatorname {aff}Q)\cap \mathbb Z^ n=mb+\Lambda $. Counting fundamental cells of $\Lambda $ in $m(Q-b)$ gives the dimension estimate: the cells meeting the relative boundary of $m(Q-b)$ lie in a boundary layer of fixed width, and there are $O(m^{d-1})$ of them. The same cells give convergence of the lattice Riemann sums on $Q$. Every $q\in Q$ lies within $O(m^{-1})$ of $Q_ m=Q\cap (b+m^{-1}\Lambda )$: move $q$ by $O(m^{-1})$ towards a fixed relative interior point so that a ball larger than the covering radius of the lattice fits inside $Q$, and then round to a lattice point inside that ball. This works for all sufficiently large $m$, and for a singleton it is exact.

Put $G_{Q,m}=\max _{q\in Q_ m}(q\cdot r-F^*(q))$. The approximation by the grid gives $0\leq G_ Q-G_{Q,m}\leq C(1+\sum _ j|r_ j|)/m$. The formula for $\mathcal B_ m$ and (27.120) give

\[ G_{Q,m}-\varphi _0\leq U_ m\leq G_{Q,m}-\varphi _0+ \frac{(n/2)\log m+\log N_ m-\log c}{m}. \]

This proves (27.119). The selected monomials vanish on every component of $X\setminus T$. The functions $\log |z_ j|^2$ have finite $L^ p$ norms for each finite $p$ with respect to smooth volume forms, so (27.119) gives the global convergence, and differentiation in the sense of distributions gives the convergence of the currents.

Fenchel's inequality $\alpha \cdot r-mF(r)\leq mF^*(\alpha /m)$ and (27.120) also give $\sup _ X B_ m\leq C N_ m m^{n/2}$. By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in the orthonormal basis, $\sup _ X|s|_{h^ m}\mathrm e^{-mw/2} \leq C m^{d/2+n/4}\| s\| _ m$ for $s\in V_ m$. The finitely many initial degrees can be absorbed in $C$, and a polynomial bound implies the Bernstein–Markov inequality with any positive exponential loss. $\square$

27.16.3 The limit measure and the ambient metric

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For $q\in \operatorname {int}P$ and $\tau \in (S^1)^ n$, let $\Psi (q,\tau )=\mathrm e^{r(q)/2}\tau $ be the point of $T$ with coordinates $\mathrm e^{r_ j(q)/2}\tau _ j$. Thus $\Psi (q,\cdot )$ parametrizes the real torus $\{ r=r(q)\} $, and we call the image of $Q\times (S^1)^ n$ the moment shell of $Q$. Here and below, $\langle S^ k\rangle $ denotes the non-pluripolar product of a closed positive $(1,1)$-current $S$; in top degree it is the non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère measure of theorem 6.1.2.

Theorem 27.16.3. Against every continuous test function on $X$,

27.121
\begin{equation} \frac{d!}{m^ d}B_ m\nu \rightharpoonup \mu _ Q=\Psi _*(d!\, \mathrm dq_\Lambda \, \mathrm d\tau ), \qquad \Psi (q,\tau )=\mathrm e^{r(q)/2}\tau . \end{equation}

The mass of $\mu _ Q$ is $d!\operatorname {vol}_\Lambda (Q)>0$. If $d=0$, then $\mu _ Q$ is the Haar probability measure on one real $n$-torus. If $d<n$, then $\mu _ Q$ is singular with respect to smooth volume forms on $X$, and

\[ \left\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}E_ Q)^{d+1}\right\rangle =0. \]

If $d=n$, then $\mu _ Q=\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}E_ Q)^ n\rangle $.

Proof. For $\alpha \in mQ\cap \mathbb Z^ n$ and $q=\alpha /m$, the normalized monomial $z^\alpha e^ m$ gives the probability measure $\mathrm dP_{m,q}=A_ m(\alpha )^{-1} \mathrm e^{m(q\cdot r-F(r))}a(r)\, \mathrm dr\, \mathrm d\tau $ on $T$. For every $\eta >0$ there is $c_\eta >0$ such that $F(r)-q\cdot r+F^*(q)\geq c_\eta $ whenever $|r-r(q)|\geq \eta $, uniformly for $q\in Q$: on a large compact set this follows from the uniqueness of the minima, and outside it from (27.118), which is uniform on $Q$. Together with (27.120), this gives $P_{m,q}\{ |r-r(q)|\geq \eta \} \leq C m^{n/2}\mathrm e^{-mc_\eta }$. Hence, for every continuous function on $X$, its integral against $P_{m,q}$ differs from its average over the torus $\{ r=r(q)\} $ by $o(1)$, uniformly in $q\in Q$. The identity $d!m^{-d}B_ m\nu =d!m^{-d}\sum _\alpha P_{m,\alpha /m}$ and the lattice Riemann sums prove (27.121). When $d<n$, the measure $\mu _ Q$ is carried by the moment shell, which has real dimension $n+d<2n$, so it is singular with respect to smooth volume forms.

For the curvature, note that $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}E_ Q=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ Q$ on $T$. By uniform strong convexity of $F^*$ on $Q$, the slope $q(r)$ maximizing $q\cdot r-F^*(q)$ over $Q$ is unique. Adding the variational inequalities at $r$ and $s$ gives $(r-s)\cdot (q(r)-q(s))\geq c|q(r)-q(s)|^2$. Thus $q$ is Lipschitz, and comparison of the maximal values gives $\nabla G_ Q=q$. Hence $G_ Q$ is $C^{1,1}$, and its Hessian has rank at most $d$ almost everywhere, since $q(r)\in b+W$. In logarithmic coordinates, convex mollifications of $G_ Q$ converge in $C^1$, their Hessians stay bounded and converge in $L^ p_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for every finite $p$, and their minors converge in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Induction on the degree in the Bedford–Taylor definition of products of bounded plurisubharmonic functions, together with integration by parts, identifies the powers of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ Q$ on $T$ with the measures given by these minors. The non-pluripolar product agrees with them on $T$ and puts no mass on the pluripolar set $X\setminus T$. The bound on the rank therefore proves the vanishing assertion.

When $d=n$, a maximizer in the interior of $Q$ satisfies $r=\nabla F^*(q)$, so $G_ Q=F$ on the set where $\nabla F(r)\in \operatorname {int}Q$. Elsewhere the maximizing slope lies in one of the finitely many facets of $Q$. On the set where it lies in a given facet, its component normal to that facet is constant, so the derivative of this component vanishes at almost every point of the set: at a point of differentiability and of density, a nonzero derivative would force a cone of positive density outside the set. Consequently $\det D^2G_ Q=\boldsymbol 1_{\{ \nabla F(r)\in Q\} }\det D^2F$ almost everywhere. In our normalization, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F)^ n=n!\det D^2F(r)\, \mathrm dr\, \mathrm d\tau $. The change of variables $q=\nabla F(r)$ gives the asserted identity for the top product. $\square$

For any Hermitian form $\omega $ on $X$, closed or not, the map $\Psi $ embeds $\operatorname {relint}Q\times (S^1)^ n$ onto a real $(n+d)$-dimensional submanifold $S_ Q=\Psi (\operatorname {relint}Q\times (S^1)^ n)$; for $d=0$ this is a single torus. Let $g_\omega (u,v)=\omega (u,Jv)$ be the associated Riemannian metric, and define the function $\mathcal J_\omega >0$ by $\Psi ^*\mathrm d\operatorname {vol}_{\omega ,S_ Q} =\mathcal J_\omega \, \mathrm dq_\Lambda \, \mathrm d\tau $, where $\mathrm d\operatorname {vol}_{\omega ,S_ Q}$ is the Riemannian volume of $S_ Q$ for $g_\omega $. Since the relative boundary has measure zero,

\[ \mathrm d\mu _ Q=\frac{d!}{\mathcal J_\omega \circ \Psi ^{-1}} \, \mathrm d\operatorname {vol}_{\omega ,S_ Q}. \]

If $d<n$, the mixed measure $\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}E_ Q)^ d\rangle \wedge \omega ^{n-d}$, where the zeroth power is interpreted as $1$ when $d=0$, has a locally bounded density on $T$ and gives $S_ Q$ zero mass. Hence it differs from $\mu _ Q$. The limit $\mu _ Q$ does not depend on the invariant probability measure $\nu $, although the norms in each degree do.

27.16.4 A rank-one selection with nonclosed sampling

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Example 27.16.4. On $(\mathbb P^1)^2$ take $L=\mathcal O(4,4)$, $w=0$, and $Q=\{ (2+t,2-t):-1/2\leq t\leq 1/2\} $. Then $\Lambda =\mathbb Z(1,-1)$, $\mathrm dq_\Lambda =\mathrm dt$, and

\[ V_ m=\operatorname {span}\{ z_1^{2m+j}z_2^{2m-j}e^ m: j\in \mathbb Z,\ |j|\leq m/2\} , \qquad N_ m=2\lfloor m/2\rfloor +1. \]

The limit $\mu _ Q$ has mass one: it is the average, over $-1/2\leq t\leq 1/2$, of the Haar probability measures on the real tori $\{ |z_1|^2=(2+t)/(2-t),\ |z_2|^2=(2-t)/(2+t)\} $; indeed, these equations describe the inverse gradient map. The moment shell is a compact three-dimensional part of the hypersurface $|z_1z_2|=1$. Writing $c(p)=p\log (p/4)+(4-p)\log ((4-p)/4)$, we have $F^*(2+t,2-t)=2c(2+t)$, which gives the envelope explicitly.

The quotient of two adjacent selected monomials is $z_1/z_2$. In homogeneous coordinates $z_ j=v_ j/u_ j$, the corresponding map $[v_1u_2:u_1v_2]$ to $\mathbb P^1$ is undefined at $(0,0)$ and $(\infty ,\infty )$. Thus the limit arises from radial concentration rather than from a globally defined submersion as in section 27.7.

Let $\omega _0$ be the sum of the two Fubini–Study forms, and set $f=c|z_1|^2/(1+|z_1|^2)$ with a constant $c\ne 0$. Then $\omega =\mathrm e^ f\omega _0$ is a torus-invariant Hermitian form, and $\mathrm d\omega =\mathrm e^ f\mathrm df\wedge \omega _0\ne 0$ wherever $\mathrm df\ne 0$. Sampling with $\nu =\omega ^2/\int _ X\omega ^2$ gives the same limit of mass one, while the curvature remains $\theta =4\omega _0$.

The assumption $b\in \mathbb Z^ n$ excludes a parity obstruction: if $Q=\{ b\} $ with $b$ half-integral, then $mQ$ contains no lattice point for odd $m$. Under our assumptions, theorem 27.16.2 shows that $V_ m\neq 0$ for all sufficiently large $m$. Polytopes touching the boundary of $P$, weights or sampling measures that are not torus-invariant, and general multiplier-ideal families require additional arguments. In particular, the growth condition defining $E_ Q$ does not by itself identify the ideal envelope of section 27.3.

27.17 Rounding in multiplier-ideal kernel families

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The growth degree $\lim _ m\log N_ m/\log m$ of the selected spaces does not by itself determine a limit of the kernel measures. At a critical logarithmic coefficient, the rounding in multiplier ideals can produce different leading section counts in even and odd degrees, while the potentials still converge to the ideal envelope. In the following product example the growth degree is positive.

Fix $q\geq 1$ and put $X=\mathbb P^1\times Y$, where $Y=(\mathbb P^1)^ q$. Write $p_0$ for the projection onto the first factor. For $0\leq j\leq q$, let $\sigma _ j$ be the Fubini–Study form on the $j$th factor, normalized to have integral one, where $j=0$ refers to the first factor. Use

\[ L=\mathcal O(1,\ldots ,1),\qquad \theta =c_1(L,h)=\sigma _0+\theta _ Y,\qquad \theta _ Y=\sum _{j=1}^ q\sigma _ j, \]

where $h$ is the product Fubini–Study metric and the forms $\sigma _ j$ are pulled back to $X$. We use the smooth positive probability measure $\nu =\sigma _0\otimes \nu _ Y$, where $\nu _ Y=\sigma _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \sigma _ q=\theta _ Y^ q/q!$, and the continuous weight zero.

On the affine line of the first factor define

\[ \psi _0(z)=\tfrac 12\log |z|^2-\log (1+|z|^2),\qquad \psi =\psi _0\circ p_0. \]

In the coordinate $1/z$ at infinity, $\psi _0$ has the same expression. It equals $-\infty $ at $0$ and $\infty $, and

27.122
\begin{equation} \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =\tfrac 12[D_0]+\tfrac 12[D_\infty ]+\theta _ Y\geq 0, \qquad D_0=\{ 0\} \times Y,\quad D_\infty =\{ \infty \} \times Y. \end{equation}

We use $\psi $ as selecting potential, so that the selected spaces are $V_ m=H^0(X,L^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\psi ))$, with the norm $\int _ X|s|_{h^ m}^2\, \mathrm d\nu $. For an orthonormal basis $(s_{m,j})$, write $\mathcal B_ m=\sum _ j|s_{m,j}|_{h^ m}^2$ and $U_ m=m^{-1}\log \mathcal B_ m$. Let $\varsigma $ be the Haar probability measure on the circle $\{ |z|=1\} \subset \mathbb P^1$.

Theorem 27.17.1. Put $a_ m=1$ for even $m$ and $a_ m=2$ for odd $m$. Then

\[ N_ m=\dim V_ m=a_ m(m+1)^ q,\qquad \lim _{m\to \infty }\frac{\log N_ m}{\log m}=q. \]

The partial envelope (27.4) with $K=X$ and obstacle zero is

27.123
\begin{equation} E_{\theta ,X}[\psi ]_{\mathcal I}(0)=E=\psi +\log 2. \end{equation}

The norms satisfy the Bernstein–Markov inequality, and $U_ m\to E$ locally uniformly on $X\setminus (D_0\cup D_\infty )$ and in $L^ p$ for every finite $p$ with respect to smooth volume forms; moreover, the currents $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U_ m$ converge weakly to $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}E$. However,

\begin{align*} \frac{q!}{(2k)^ q}\mathcal B_{2k}\nu & \rightharpoonup q!\, \varsigma \otimes \nu _ Y,\\ \frac{q!}{(2k+1)^ q}\mathcal B_{2k+1}\nu & \rightharpoonup 2q!\, \varsigma \otimes \nu _ Y. \end{align*}

Thus the kernel measures normalized by $q!/m^ q$ do not converge as $m\to \infty $ through all integers. Dividing instead by the dimension gives $N_ m^{-1}\mathcal B_ m\nu \rightharpoonup \varsigma \otimes \nu _ Y$. The envelope has zero non-pluripolar top mass: $\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}E)^{q+1}\rangle =0$.

Proof. By lemma 1.16.1,

\[ \mathcal I(m\psi )= \mathcal O_ X(-r_ mD_0-r_ mD_\infty ),\qquad r_ m=\lfloor m/2\rfloor . \]

In the product affine frame, global sections are polynomials of degree at most $m$ in each variable. A global section belongs to $V_ m$ exactly when each of its monomials vanishes to order at least $r_ m$ along $D_0$ and along $D_\infty $, that is, when the exponent $\alpha _0$ of the first variable satisfies $r_ m\leq \alpha _0\leq m-r_ m$; the other exponents range from $0$ to $m$. There is one admissible value of $\alpha _0$ when $m$ is even and two when $m$ is odd. This proves the dimension formula.

To identify the envelope, let $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ be a competitor, that is, $v\preceq _{\mathcal I}\psi $ in the multiplier-ideal order of section 1.12 and $v\leq 0$ quasi-everywhere. By theorem 1.16.3, the Lelong numbers of $v$ at the points of $D_0$ and $D_\infty $ are at least $1/2$. Fix $y\in Y$ and restrict $v$ to the line $\mathbb P^1\times \{ y\} $. The restriction is either identically $-\infty $ or $\sigma _0$-plurisubharmonic. In the latter case, its Lelong numbers at the two points over $0$ and $\infty $ are at least those of $v$: the supremum over a ball in the line is at most the supremum over the corresponding ambient ball, and division by $\log r^2<0$ reverses the inequality. By the identification of Lelong numbers with atoms in lemma 1.16.11, the curvature measure of the restriction has atoms of mass at least $1/2$ at these two points. By Stokes' theorem its total mass is one, so these atoms exhaust the measure. The difference between the restriction and $\psi _0$ is therefore harmonic on the compact projective line, hence constant.

It follows that $v(z,y)=\psi _0(z)+c(y)$ off $D_0\cup D_\infty $, where $c(y)=-\infty $ on lines along which $v$ is identically $-\infty $. Restriction to $z=1$ shows that $c(y)=v(1,y)+\log 2$ is a $\theta _ Y$-plurisubharmonic function; it is not identically $-\infty $, since otherwise $v$ would be. Submean values over shrinking balls upgrade the obstacle condition to $v\leq 0$ everywhere. Since $\max \psi _0=-\log 2$, we get $c\leq \log 2$, and hence $v\leq E$. On $D_0\cup D_\infty $ both $v$ and $E$ equal $-\infty $. Conversely, $E\leq 0$ and $E$ differs from $\psi $ by a constant, so $E$ is itself a competitor. This proves (27.123).

The norms of the monomials in one factor are beta integrals. With $x=|z|^2/(1+|z|^2)$, they are

27.124
\begin{equation} \int _{\mathbb P^1}|z^\alpha e^ m|_{h^ m}^2\, \sigma _0 =\int _0^1x^\alpha (1-x)^{m-\alpha }\, \mathrm dx =\frac{\alpha !(m-\alpha )!}{(m+1)!}. \end{equation}

Orthogonality under the rotations and integration over the product diagonalize the global norm. Summing over all monomials in one factor gives the constant kernel $m+1$, by the binomial formula. Therefore the full kernel on $Y$ is $(m+1)^ q$. With $C_ k=(2k+1)\binom {2k}{k}$, the selected kernels are

27.125
\begin{align} \mathcal B_{2k}& =(2k+1)^ q C_ k\mathrm e^{2k\psi _0},\notag \\ \mathcal B_{2k+1}& =2(2k+2)^ q C_ k\mathrm e^{2k\psi _0}. \end{align}

We use the even formula for $k\geq 1$; the odd formula also holds for $k=0$. In the odd case the two monomials in the first factor have equal norms, and the sum of their squared moduli contributes the factor $1+|z|^2$, which gives the second identity. The full kernel on $X$ is $(m+1)^{q+1}$, so evaluation gives $\sup _ X|s|_{h^ m}\leq (m+1)^{(q+1)/2}\| s\| _ m$ for every selected section. This polynomial bound proves the Bernstein–Markov inequality.

The central binomial coefficient is the largest in its row. Hence $4^ k\leq C_ k\leq (2k+1)4^ k$, and $\log C_ k=2k\log 2+O(\log (k+1))$. Taking logarithms in (27.125) gives

\[ |U_ m-E|\leq \frac{C_ q}{m} \bigl (1+|\log |z|^2|+\log m\bigr ) \quad \text{off }D_0\cup D_\infty , \]

because $|\psi _0|=\log (2\cosh (\tfrac 12\log |z|^2))$ is bounded by $\log 2+\tfrac 12|\log |z|^2|$. The function $\log |z|^2$ belongs to $L^ p$ for every finite $p$ with respect to smooth volume forms. This proves the convergence of the potentials, and differentiating the $L^1$ limit proves the convergence of the currents. For every $m\geq 2$, both $U_ m$ and $E$ are $-\infty $ on $D_0\cup D_\infty $.

Let $P_ k$ be the probability measure on the first factor with density $C_ k\mathrm e^{2k\psi _0}$ with respect to $\sigma _0$. Under $P_ k$ the angle of $z$ is uniformly distributed, and $x$ has the beta distribution with parameters $(k+1,k+1)$, whose mean is $1/2$ and whose variance is $1/[4(2k+3)]$. Chebyshev's inequality and continuity of the test functions in the angle therefore give $P_ k\rightharpoonup \varsigma $. The measure identities

\[ \mathcal B_{2k}\nu =(2k+1)^ q P_ k\otimes \nu _ Y, \qquad \mathcal B_{2k+1}\nu =2(2k+2)^ q P_ k\otimes \nu _ Y \]

prove both limits along even and odd degrees and the limit after division by $N_ m$. Since the two limits have different total masses, the $q$-normalized family does not converge. Normalization by the ambient degree, $(q+1)!/m^{q+1}$, instead gives zero in total variation.

Finally, off $D_0\cup D_\infty $ the current in (27.122) is $\theta _ Y$, whose rank is $q$. The non-pluripolar product charges neither divisor, so the top product is zero. $\square$

Unlike the Newton family of section 27.16, this family of multiplier-ideal spaces is not multiplicative. For two odd degrees, the monomials of minimal order along $D_0$ are selected, but their product misses the condition in the even sum degree by one order. The Newton family has a leading term given by lattice counting in every sufficiently large degree; here the ideal envelope is identified, but the leading term oscillates. Testing against the constant function one shows that convergence of $q!N_ m/m^ q$ is necessary for the $q$-normalized kernel measures to converge. Since the envelope has zero non-pluripolar top mass, theorem 27.6.3, whose hypotheses require positive mass, does not apply to this selecting potential.

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory
27.125
\[\begin{aligned}\begin{align} \mathcal B_{2k+1}& =2(2k+2)^ q C_ k\mathrm e^{2k\psi _0}. \end{align}\end{aligned}\]

27.18 Three monomial bands from a multiplier-ideal selection

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For an isolated logarithmic pole of coefficient one on a complex surface, the multiplier ideal in degree $m$ requires vanishing of order $m-1$, whereas domination by the pole would require order $m$. At the two base points of the pencil below, the multiplier-ideal conditions select three neighboring bands of monomials. All three contribute to the limit of the kernel measures, even though the competitor classes defined by multiplier ideals and by domination coincide.

Let $X=\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$, and let $L=\mathcal O(1,1)$ carry its product Fubini–Study metric $h$, with $\theta =c_1(L,h)$. Let $[T_ j:Z_ j]$ be homogeneous coordinates on the $j$th factor, write $z_ j=Z_ j/T_ j$, and set

\[ \psi =\max \{ \log |T_1T_2|_ h^2,\log |Z_1Z_2|_ h^2\} , \qquad p_+=(0,\infty ),\quad p_-=(\infty ,0). \]

The two sections $T_1T_2$ and $Z_1Z_2$ of $L$ vanish simultaneously only at $p_\pm $, so these are the only poles of $\psi $. In local frames the potentials of $\psi $ are maxima of logarithms of squared moduli of holomorphic functions, so $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. On the torus $(\mathbb C^*)^2$ put

\[ x_ j=\log |z_ j|^2,\qquad s=x_1+x_2,\qquad \varphi _ X=\log (1+\mathrm e^{x_1})+\log (1+\mathrm e^{x_2}). \]

Then $\psi =\max (0,s)-\varphi _ X$.

In this model the norm is computed on the compact set $K=\{ x\in [-1,2]^2\} \subset (\mathbb C^*)^2$, which has nonempty interior. On $K$ let

\[ w=\frac{x_1^2+x_2^2}{2}-\varphi _ X, \qquad \mathrm d\nu =\frac19\, \mathrm dx_1\, \mathrm dx_2\, \mathrm d\tau _1\, \mathrm d\tau _2, \]

where $\mathrm d\tau _ j$ is the Haar probability measure in the angle of $z_ j$. Thus $\nu $ is a probability measure supported on $K$, not a smooth positive volume form on $X$. With $\psi $ as selecting potential, set

\[ \begin{aligned} V_ m& =H^0(X,L^ m\otimes \mathcal I(m\psi )), & N_ m& =\dim V_ m,\\ \| \sigma \| _ m^2& =\int _ K|\sigma |_{h^ m}^2\mathrm e^{-mw}\, \mathrm d\nu , & \mathcal B_ m& =\sum _ j|\sigma _{m,j}|_{h^ m}^2, \end{aligned} \]

where $(\sigma _{m,j})$ is an orthonormal basis. The norm is positive definite by the identity theorem on the interior of $K$. As in section 27.1, we use $U_ m=m^{-1}\log \mathcal B_ m$ on $X$ and $B_ m=\mathrm e^{-mw}\mathcal B_ m$ on $K$. Only the values of $w$ on $K$ enter the norm and the envelope.

Lemma 27.18.1. For every $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$,

\[ v\preceq _{\mathcal I}\psi \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad v\leq \psi +C_ v \quad \hbox{for some finite }C_ v, \]

where $\preceq _{\mathcal I}$ is the multiplier-ideal order of section 1.12. Consequently, for every obstacle, the envelopes defined with these two conditions agree. At either pole, for every real $t>0$,

\[ \mathcal I(t\psi )=\mathfrak m_{p_\pm }^{\max \{ \lfloor t\rfloor -1,0\} }. \]

Away from the poles, $\mathcal I(t\psi )$ is the full structure sheaf.

Proof. Near $p_+$ use the coordinates $\zeta _1=z_1$, $\zeta _2=1/z_2$ and the frame $T_1Z_2$. In this frame the two pencil sections $T_1T_2$ and $Z_1Z_2$ have coefficients $\zeta _2$ and $\zeta _1$, so

\[ \psi =\log \max (|\zeta _1|^2,|\zeta _2|^2) -\log (1+|\zeta _1|^2)-\log (1+|\zeta _2|^2) =\log |\zeta |^2+O(1). \]

The same calculation applies at $p_-$. The formula for the ideals is lemma 1.16.1 with normal dimension two and coefficient one, including the case of equality in the integrability condition.

Now suppose that $v\preceq _{\mathcal I}\psi $. By theorem 1.16.3, the Lelong numbers satisfy $\nu (v,p_\pm )\geq 1$; here $\nu (v,p)$ is the Lelong number of section 1.13, not the measure $\nu $. Let $\phi $ be the plurisubharmonic local potential of $v$ in a frame near $p_\pm $, and consider its radial maximum

\[ M(a)=\sup _{|\zeta |\leq \mathrm e^{a/2}}\phi (\zeta ) \]

inside a fixed coordinate ball. This function is finite and convex: by the three-circles theorem, the corresponding maximum on each complex line through the pole is convex, and $M$ is the supremum of these functions; lines on which $\phi $ is identically $-\infty $ do not affect the supremum. The limiting slope of $M$ as $a\to -\infty $ is the Lelong number. For $a<a_0$, convexity therefore gives

\[ M(a)\leq M(a_0)+\nu (v,p_\pm )(a-a_0)\leq a+C. \]

Consequently $v\leq \psi +C$ near both poles. After $C$ is increased, the same bound holds on the compact complement, because $v$ is bounded above and $\psi $ is finite and continuous there. Conversely, domination $v\leq \psi +C$ implies the inclusions of multiplier ideals at all weights, by the definition through integrability. Thus the two competitor classes coincide before any obstacle is imposed. $\square$

The pencil also describes the competitors that are not torus-invariant. Let $\omega _{\rm FS}$ be the Fubini–Study form on $\mathbb P^1$, normalized to have integral one.

Lemma 27.18.2. On $X\setminus \{ p_+,p_-\} $ set

\[ f=[T_1T_2:Z_1Z_2],\qquad \psi _{\rm FS}=\log (|T_1T_2|_ h^2+|Z_1Z_2|_ h^2). \]

Every $v$ in the class of lemma 27.18.1 has the form

\[ v=\psi _{\rm FS}+u\circ f, \qquad u\in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^1,\omega _{\rm FS}), \]

on $X\setminus \{ p_+,p_-\} $, and $v(p_\pm )=-\infty $. Conversely, for every such $u$, the function $\psi _{\rm FS}+u\circ f$, extended by $-\infty $ at the two poles, belongs to that class.

Proof. For $\xi \in \mathbb C^*$, the curve of the pencil

\[ C_\xi =\{ Z_1Z_2=\xi T_1T_2\} \]

is a smooth rational curve of bidegree $(1,1)$ through both poles, and its $\theta $-degree is two. The restriction of $v$ to this connected curve is either identically $-\infty $ or $\theta |_{C_\xi }$-plurisubharmonic. In the latter case, its curvature measure has total mass two by Stokes' theorem. The local upper bounds of lemma 27.18.1 give Lelong numbers at least one at the two poles, hence, by the identification of Lelong numbers with atoms in lemma 1.16.11, atoms of mass at least one there. These atoms exhaust the measure. The curvature of $\psi |_{C_\xi }$ consists of the same two unit atoms. Thus $v-\psi $ is harmonic, in the sense of distributions, on the compact curve $C_\xi $, and therefore constant.

On the torus use the holomorphic coordinates $(z_1,\xi =z_1z_2)$. Local integrability and Fubini's theorem show that, for almost every $\xi $, the restriction of $v$ to $C_\xi $ is not identically $-\infty $; by the preceding paragraph it is then finite off the poles. Hence the restriction of $v$ to the transverse curve $S=\{ z_1=1\} \simeq \mathbb P^1$ is not identically $-\infty $. Put $u=v|_ S+\log 2$. Since $\theta |_ S=\omega _{\rm FS}$ and $\psi _{\rm FS}|_ S=-\log 2$, the constants on the curves $C_\xi $ give the asserted identity almost everywhere on the torus. Both sides have plurisubharmonic local potentials, so they agree everywhere off the poles. The logarithmic upper bounds give the value $-\infty $ at the poles.

Conversely, $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\psi _{\rm FS}+u\circ f)=f^*(\omega _{\rm FS}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)\geq 0$ off the poles. In a chart at a pole, the local potential is bounded above by $\log (|\zeta _1|^2+|\zeta _2|^2)+C$, since $u$ is bounded above. It therefore extends across the pole as a plurisubharmonic function with value $-\infty $ there, which gives a global $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function dominated by $\psi +C$. $\square$

Theorem 27.18.3. For these $K$ and $w$, the partial envelope (27.4) is

\[ \begin{aligned} E_{\theta ,K}[\psi ]_{\mathcal I}(w)& =P=g(s)-\varphi _ X,\\ g(s)& =\sup _{0\leq t\leq 1}(ts-t^2) =\begin{cases} 0,& s\leq 0,\\ s^2/4,& 0\leq s\leq 2,\\ s-1,& s\geq 2. \end{cases}\end{aligned} \]

Here $P$ is given by this formula on the torus and extends to a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function on $X$ with $P(p_\pm )=-\infty $ and $\psi -1\leq P\leq \psi $.

Proof. For an arbitrary competitor $v$, lemma 27.18.2 gives, in terms of the affine coordinate $\xi $ of $\mathbb P^1$,

\[ \chi (\xi )=\log (1+|\xi |^2)+u(\xi ), \qquad v=\chi (z_1z_2)-\varphi _ X. \]

On the interior of $K$, the obstacle condition, which holds quasi-everywhere, becomes an inequality everywhere, by the submean inequality and the continuity of $w+\varphi _ X$. Evaluating at $x_1=x_2=s/2$, with $s=\log |\xi |^2$, gives

\[ \chi (\xi )\leq s^2/4\quad (-2<s<4) \]

for every argument of $\xi $. At $s=0$ this bounds $\chi $ by zero on $|\xi |=1$, and the maximum principle for the subharmonic function $\chi $ bounds it by zero on the whole disk $|\xi |\leq 1$. At infinity use the local potential

\[ \chi _\infty (\eta )=\chi (1/\eta )+\log |\eta |^2. \]

The obstacle at $s=2$ bounds it by $-1$ on $|\eta |=\mathrm e^{-1}$, and the same maximum principle gives $\chi (\xi )\leq s-1$ for $s\geq 2$. Together with the obstacle for $0\leq s\leq 2$, this proves $\chi \leq g$, without any invariance assumption on the competitor.

Conversely, $g$ is convex with slopes in $[0,1]$, so $g(\log |\xi |^2)-\log (1+|\xi |^2)$ is an $\omega _{\rm FS}$-plurisubharmonic function on $\mathbb P^1$. By lemma 27.18.2, it defines the global function $P$. Directly,

\[ \max (0,s)-1\leq g(s)\leq \max (0,s),\qquad g(s)\leq s^2/4\leq (x_1^2+x_2^2)/2. \]

Thus $P$ belongs to the competitor class of lemma 27.18.1 and satisfies the obstacle condition everywhere on $K$. Since it dominates every competitor, it is the envelope, including its values $-\infty $ at the poles. $\square$

Proposition 27.18.4. For every $m\geq 1$ the selected space is

\[ V_ m=\operatorname {span}\{ Z_1^ aT_1^{m-a}Z_2^ bT_2^{m-b}: 0\leq a,b\leq m,\ |a-b|\leq 1\} , \qquad N_ m=3m+1. \]

In particular, $N_ m$ grows linearly in $m$. These spaces are not multiplicative. If the sections were instead required to satisfy $|\sigma |_{h^ m}^2\leq C\mathrm e^{m\psi }$, only the monomials with $a=b$ would remain. The measure $\nu $ is Bernstein–Markov for $(K,w)$.

Proof. At $p_+$ the monomial with exponents $(a,b)$ has local exponents $(a,m-b)$, and at $p_-$ it has local exponents $(m-a,b)$. By lemma 27.18.1, both total orders must be at least $m-1$. Distinct exponent pairs cannot cancel, so these conditions select exactly the monomials with $|a-b|\leq 1$. The three bands $a=b$, $a=b+1$ and $a=b-1$ have $m+1$, $m$ and $m$ elements. The bound $|\sigma |_{h^ m}^2\leq C\mathrm e^{m\psi }$ would require total order $m$ at both poles and leave only the diagonal. The product of two sections with offset $a-b=1$ has offset two and lies outside the selected space of the sum degree; such products fail for every pair of positive degrees. Thus the linear growth of $N_ m$ is not the growth of a graded section ring.

Integration over the angles makes all global monomials orthogonal, including those outside the selected space. For $t\in [0,1]$ set

\[ I_ m(t)=\int _{-1}^2\mathrm e^{m(tx-x^2/2)}\, \mathrm dx =\mathrm e^{mt^2/2}J_ m(t),\qquad J_ m(t)=\int _{-1}^2\mathrm e^{-m(x-t)^2/2}\, \mathrm dx. \]

The squared norm of the monomial with exponents $(a,b)$ is

27.133
\begin{equation} A_ m(a,b)=\frac19 I_ m(a/m)I_ m(b/m). \end{equation}

With $c=\int _{-1}^1\mathrm e^{-y^2/2}\, \mathrm dy$, uniformly in $t\in [0,1]$,

\[ \frac c{\sqrt m}\leq J_ m(t)\leq \sqrt{\frac{2\pi }m},\qquad J_ m(t)=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi }m} \bigl (1+O(m^{-1/2}\mathrm e^{-m/2})\bigr ). \]

For the lower bound, integrate over the interval of radius $m^{-1/2}$ centered at $t$; for the last assertion, note that each of the two omitted Gaussian tails is at most $\mathrm e^{-m/2}/m$. Hence

27.134
\begin{equation} \frac{c^2}{9m}\mathrm e^{(a^2+b^2)/(2m)} \leq A_ m(a,b)\leq \frac{2\pi }{9m}\mathrm e^{(a^2+b^2)/(2m)}. \end{equation}

For $x\in [-1,2]^2$, completing squares bounds the weighted contribution of each normalized monomial by $9m/c^2$. Summing over all $(m+1)^2$ global monomials and using the evaluation inequality gives

\[ \sup _ K|\sigma |_{h^ m}\mathrm e^{-mw/2} \leq C m^{3/2}\| \sigma \| _ m \quad (\sigma \in H^0(X,L^ m)). \]

This polynomial bound proves the Bernstein–Markov property. On the selected space the same argument gives the factor $Cm$. $\square$

Theorem 27.18.5. With values in $[-\infty ,\infty )$ at the poles, the potentials satisfy

27.135
\begin{equation} U_ m\geq P+\frac{\log (9m/(2\pi ))}{m}-\frac1{4m^2}. \end{equation}

They converge to $P$ locally uniformly on $X\setminus \{ p_+,p_-\} $, in $L^ p$ with respect to a smooth volume form on $X$ for every finite $p$, at the rate $O(\log m/m)$, and pointwise on $X$ with values in $[-\infty ,\infty )$. The currents $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U_ m$ converge weakly to $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}P$. Moreover, $\mathrm e^{U_ m}\to \mathrm e^ P$ uniformly on $X$, although for each fixed $m\geq 2$ the difference $U_ m-P$ is unbounded above near either pole.

Define a probability measure $\mu $ by

\[ \int _ X f\, \mathrm d\mu =\int _0^1\int f(\mathrm e^{t/2}\tau _1,\mathrm e^{t/2}\tau _2) \, \mathrm d\tau _1\, \mathrm d\tau _2\, \mathrm dt. \]

Then, against every continuous test function on $X$,

\[ N_ m^{-1}B_ m\nu \rightharpoonup \mu ,\qquad m^{-1}B_ m\nu \rightharpoonup 3\mu . \]

The measures $2m^{-2}B_ m\nu $, normalized by the ambient dimension, tend to zero in total variation. The envelope satisfies $\langle (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}P)^2\rangle =0$.

Proof. The diagonal monomials belong to $V_ m$. The upper bound for their norms in (27.134) gives

\[ \mathcal B_ m\geq \frac{9m}{2\pi }\mathrm e^{-m\varphi _ X} \max _{0\leq j\leq m}\mathrm e^{js-j^2/m}. \]

For $0\leq s\leq 2$, the grid point $j/m$ nearest to $s/2$ loses at most $1/(4m^2)$ compared with $g(s)$; outside this interval the maximizing endpoint lies on the grid. This proves (27.135) on the torus.

For a monomial with offset one, $(a,b)=(j+1,j)$, the lower bound in (27.134) shows that the exponent of its normalized contribution, apart from $-m\varphi _ X$ and constants, is at most both

\[ js-j^2/m+x_1,\qquad (j+1)s-(j+1)^2/m+1-x_2. \]

The band with $(a,b)=(j,j+1)$ is obtained by interchanging the indices. Put

\[ R(x)=1+\min (\mathrm e^{x_1},\mathrm e^{1-x_2}) +\min (\mathrm e^{x_2},\mathrm e^{1-x_1}). \]

The lower bound for the norms and the count of monomials give

27.136
\begin{equation} U_ m\leq P+\frac{C+2\log (m+1)+\log R(x)}m. \end{equation}

The function $R$ extends continuously across the coordinate divisors away from the poles. In a chart at $p_+$, put $A=|\zeta _1|^2$ and $B=|\zeta _2|^2$. The possibly large term of $R$ satisfies

\[ \min (1/B,\mathrm e/A)\leq (\mathrm e+1)/(A+B), \]

and the other term is bounded; the same holds at $p_-$. Thus $R\leq C(1+r^{-2})$ near either pole, where $r=|\zeta |$ is the local radius, and $\log R$ belongs to $L^ p$ for every finite $p$ with respect to smooth volume forms.

For $m\geq 2$, all selected sections vanish at the poles. The two diagonal sections $T_1^ mT_2^ m$ and $Z_1^ mZ_2^ m$ have no other common zeros, so $\mathcal B_ m>0$ off the poles. Away from the poles, $P$ and $U_ m$ are continuous, so both inequalities extend from the torus to $X\setminus \{ p_+,p_-\} $. They give the asserted local uniform convergence and convergence in $L^ p$, and differentiation of the $L^1$ limit in the sense of distributions gives the weak convergence of the currents. At the poles both $U_ m$ and $P$ equal $-\infty $ for $m\geq 2$, which also proves the pointwise statement on $X$.

For a fixed $m\geq 2$, the lowest-order terms of the selected sections at a pole include every binary monomial of degree $m-1$, and the sum of their squared moduli is comparable to $r^{2(m-1)}$ in all directions. Hence

\[ U_ m=(1-1/m)\log r^2+O_ m(1),\qquad P=\log r^2+O(1), \]

so the difference $U_ m-P$ tends to $+\infty $ near each pole. Nevertheless, (27.136) gives, uniformly in $m\geq 2$ and $r<1$,

\[ \mathrm e^{U_ m}\leq Cr^{2-2/m}\leq Cr,\qquad \mathrm e^ P\leq Cr^2. \]

Combined with uniform convergence on compact subsets away from the poles, these bounds give uniform convergence of $\mathrm e^{U_ m}$.

The contribution of each normalized monomial to $B_ m\nu $ is exactly the product of two truncated Gaussian probability measures in $x_1,x_2$, centered at $(a/m,b/m)$, with the Haar probability measures in the angles. Completing squares in (27.133) and using its uniform bounds proves concentration at these centers, uniformly over all three bands; for example, the probability of $|x_1-a/m|\geq \eta $ is at most $C_\eta \sqrt m\, \mathrm e^{-m\eta ^2/2}$. Uniform continuity of a test function then reduces the sum to three lattice Riemann sums. Each band contains asymptotically one third of the $N_ m$ monomials, and in each band the centers $(a/m,b/m)$ approach the diagonal point $(t,t)$, $t\in [0,1]$. This proves both positive limits. The total mass of $2m^{-2}B_ m\nu $ is $2(3m+1)/m^2\to 0$, which proves the convergence to zero in total variation.

Off the poles, $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}P$ is the pullback by $f$ of the current on $\mathbb P^1$ defined by $g$. The function $g$ is $C^{1,1}$, and the local complex Hessian of $g(s)$ has rank at most one. Smooth convex approximations and the product of locally bounded potentials show that its square vanishes, as in the Hessian argument of section 27.16. The non-pluripolar product assigns no mass to the two poles, so the global square is zero. In particular, the positive-mass hypothesis of theorem 27.6.3 fails in this model. $\square$

Parametrize the real three-dimensional shell $S$ carrying $\mu $ by $\Psi (t,\tau _1,\tau _2)=(\mathrm e^{t/2}\tau _1,\mathrm e^{t/2}\tau _2)$, $0<t<1$. For a Hermitian form $\omega $ on $X$, which need be neither closed nor torus-invariant, write $\Psi ^*\mathrm d\operatorname {vol}_{\omega ,S}=\mathcal J_\omega \, \mathrm dt\, \mathrm d\tau _1\, \mathrm d\tau _2$, as in section 27.16. Since the boundary of $S$ has zero mass, the densities of $\mu $ and $3\mu $ with respect to $\mathrm d\operatorname {vol}_{\omega ,S}$ are $1/(\mathcal J_\omega \circ \Psi ^{-1})$ and $3/(\mathcal J_\omega \circ \Psi ^{-1})$. The mixed measure $\langle \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}P\rangle \wedge \omega $ has a locally bounded density off the poles and gives $S$ zero mass, so it cannot reproduce the positive limit of the kernel measures.
